"Declaration of Policy: The Board 

"Sec. 102. [72 Stat. 740, 49 U.S.C. 1302] in the exercise and performance of its powers and duties under this Act, the Board shall consider the following, among other things, as being in the public interest, and in accordance with the public convenience and necessity: "(a) The encouragement and development of an airtransportation system properly adapted to the present and future needs of the foreign and domestic commerce of the United States, of the Postal Service, and of the national defense; "(b) The regulation of air transportation in such manner as to recognize and preserve the inherent advantages of, assure the highest degree of safety in, and foster sound economic conditions in, such transportation, and to improve the relations between, and coordinate transportation by, air carriers; "(c) The promotion of adequate, economical, and efficient service by air carriers at reasonable charges, without unjust discriminations, undue preferences or advantages, or unfair or destructive competitive practices; "(d) Competition to the extent necessary to assure the sound development of an air-transportation system properly adapted to the needs of the foreign and domestic commerce of the United States, of the Postal Service, and of the national defense; "(e) The promotion of safety in air commerce; and "(f) The promotion, encouragement, and development of civil aeronautics."

HIGHLIGHTING FISCAL 1968 

Mergers, route realignment investigations and new expeditious procedural methods in formal proceedings were among the more significant developments in the regulation and promotion of the air transport industry during fiscal 1968, the 30th anniversary year of the Civil Aeronautics Board. 

These actions largely affected the local service industry and together they contributed substantially to another facet of industry progress, the continued significant reduction in Federal subsidy paid to air carriers. 

At $59.3 million, the lowest dollar amount in 9 years, subsidy estimates for the entire industry for fiscal year 1968 were $6.5 million below those of fiscal 1967. Subsidy actually paid to local service carriers alone accounted for $4.4 million of the reduction. 

Bonanza Air Lines, Inc., Pacific Air Lines, Inc., and West Coast Airlines, 

Inc., were merged to form Air West;

Central Airlines was merged into 

Frontier Airlines, and Lake Central into Allegheny Airlines. The CAB 

determined that the respective first-year subsidy need reductions in these mergers would be $676,000, $503,000, and $902,000. 

Three other mergers among Alaskan carriers, Alaska Airlines with Cordova

Airlines, Alaska Airlines with Alaska Coastal Airlines, and Northern Con solidated Airlines with Wien Alaska Airlines, are expected to strengthen the carriers, improve their services and reduce future subsidy payments. 

Five local service carrier route realignment proceedings, covering most major regions of the United States, were pending at the close of the fiscal year.

New Procedural Regulation 

Possibly the most significant new development during the year was the Board's adoption of a new procedural regulation (subpart M of CAB rules of practice) which provides expedited procedure for modifying or removing certain limitations in the certificates of local service carriers. 

Subpart M has been hailed by administrative lawyers as one of the more significant forward steps in aviation administrative law in at least the last decade. Proceedings expedited under subpart M are those which have local service subsidy reduction or improved service for the public as their principal goal.

The trunkline carriers, too, wrote an important part of the air transport industry story for fiscal 1968. 

Where the revenue passenger miles of the local service carriers increased 1

by percent over fiscal 1967, those of the domestic trunks increased by 24 percent. Where local service operating revenues rose 19 percent to $446 million, operating revenues of the trunks increased by nearly 20 percent, to $4.7 billion. Operating expenses in the local industry increased 22 percent over the preceding year and the industry showed a net loss of $4 million compared with a net income of $4.3 million a year earlier, reflecting in part the decrease in subsidy. Domestic trunkline operating expenses rose by 23 percent and net income at $192 million, was down somewhat from a year earlier.

The rates of return for the industry were: domestic trunklines, 6.29 percent, excluding investment tax credit; 7.23 percent, including investment tax credit; local service carriers, 2.33, excluding; 2.61, including; international and territorial operations, 11.01, excluding and 12.21 including. 

In the trunkline industry, the CAB turned increasing attention to many rapidly growing markets, instituting and processing a number of investigations to determine the need for single-plane or first competitive service in such markets. As in the local service route realignment proceedings, these cases covered virtually every major region of the Nation. By fiscal year's end, seven such cases had been heard, three were to be scheduled for hearing.

The CAB took several measures against the burgeoning problem of airport congestion. It is giving greater attention to the use of satellite airports as a means of diverting traffic from some of the busier metropolitan airports and consideration of grants of new authority to bypass historical connecting points.

The CAB also has looked into helicopter services between larger airports and the metropolitan areas they serve, between mid-city points alone, and between metropolitan area airports alone.

In the Northeast Corridor VTOL Investigation, the CAB is seeking to determine the need for and the feasibility of establishing an air route between central city communities, using vertical or short takeoff and landing aircraft. 

Authority Is Extended 

Reflecting the growing importance of air taxi operations, the CAB extended through fiscal 1969 the mail carrying authority of air taxi operators. 

The Board also amended its regulations to permit air taxi operators to file joint tariffs with certificated air carriers, thus encouraging lower rates and fares for through service. At fiscal year's end, action was pending on a proposed rule requiring air taxi operators to carry liability insurance and register with the CAB.

The Board during the fiscal year decided the Reopened Supplemental Air Service Case, issuing certificates to Overseas National Airways, Inc., Standard 

Airways, Inc., and Vance International Airways, Inc., bringing to 13 the total number of certificated supplemental air carriers. 

At June 30, 1968, the supplementals had total assets of $409 million. 

Their operating revenues of $287 million were about 3.8 percent of the industry's total operating revenue. 

In cargo matters, hearings were held in the Domestic Coterminal Points- Europe All-Cargo Service Investigation to determine the need for additional 2

all-cargo service across the Atlantic and the CAB approved the transfer of Slick Corp.'s route certificate to Airlift International, Inc. The Board also set for hearing an application by The Flying Tiger Line, Inc. for broad expansion of its authority throughout the United States. 

Two administrative changes were made during the fiscal year. President 

Lyndon B. Johnson appointed John H. Crooker, Jr., Chairman of the Board, 

March 11, 1968, to succeed Charles S. Murphy, who resigned to become a consultant in the White House. Associate Chief Hearing Examiner Thomas 

L. Wrenn was named Chief Hearing Examiner to succeed Francis W. Brown, who retired.

DOMESTIC AIR COMMERCE 

The CAB's domestic route program during the fiscal year had the following primary objectives: 

1). Reduction in Federal operating subsidies for local service carriers and Alaskan carriers; 

2). Improvements in carrier authorizations in markets where traffic had grown sufficiently to permit first single plane or first competitive air service; 

3). Adjustment in route licenses to permit traffic flows to avoid congested airports (e.g., New York, Chicago, Los Angeles).

The route program for 1968 also included the processing of a number of merger proceedings involving subsidized carriers with favorable subsidy impact. These classes of matters are discussed below. 

Local Service Carriers 

The local service carriers recorded increases over fiscal 1967 of 25 percent in revenue passenger miles, to 4.7 million, and 19 percent in operating revenues, to $446 billion. These increases would have been greater except that fiscal 1967 reflected some diversion of traffic to the local service carriers during the strike against five major trunklines. Operating expenses increased 22 percent. The industry showed a net loss of $4 million, compared with a net income of $4.3 million for fiscal 1967. Return on investment, excluding investment tax credit, was 3.6 percent. This compared with 4.8 percent for fiscal 1967. Subsidy payments to the local service carriers decreased by $4.4 million, continuing the downward trend of recent years.

A major element of the CAB's efforts to reduce subsidy payments and improve traffic routings consists of the local carriers' route realignment investigations. Four of these cases have proceeded through the public hearing stage. They are the Ozark Air Lines Route Realignment Investi gation, Southern Airways Route Realignment Investigation, Central Airlines (Frontier) Route 81 Investigation, and Allegheny Airlines Route 97 In vestigation.

A second CAB approach is to extend a local service carrier's route within a geographical area not totally foreign to the area of the carrier's historical interest especially where this will provide important service improvements to cities on the carrier's existing system and where there will

be a reduction in subsidy need. Thus, Allegheny received an amended certificate extending its system westward from Huntington, W. Va., to the new coterminal points Nashville and Memphis, Tenn., via the intermediate point Lexington, Ky.

In addition, cases are in progress in which Frontier and Piedmont are seeking to enter Chicago, and Ozark is seeking to have its present systems extended into New York and Washington. Southern recently was awarded authorization to serve New York and Washington, D.C., on direct flights from Columbus, Ga.

A third approach to the CAB's goal respecting local service carriers. culminated during this fiscal year in adoption of a new procedural regulation (subpart M) which provides an expedited procedure for modifying or removing certain limitations on operations that are contained in the certificates of local service carriers. The CAB is hopeful that this new regulation will provide a means whereby local service carriers will be able to reduce their subsidy need requirements. Applications filed at close of fiscal year:

Local Service Carrier Mergers 

The CAB also expects a reduction in subsidy to result from recent mergers between local service carriers. The merger of Bonanza Air Lines, Inc., Pacific Air Lines, Inc., and West Coast Airlines, Inc., was approved Febru ary 23, 1968, as being in the public interest. The merged carrier is now called Air West, Inc. The CAB determined that a $676,000 subsidy need reduction will result from the merger during the first year of operations. 

The CAB also approved the mergers of Frontier Airlines and Central 

Airlines, effective October 1, 1967, and Allegheny Airlines and Lake Central 

Airlines, effective July 1, 1968. The CAB determined that, there should be first-year subsidy need reductions of $503,000 and $902,000, respectively, resulting from these two mergers.

These mergers also should provide benefits to the traveling public in the form of new single-plane through services, and each should provide needed improvement in the financial position of the respective airlines. 

New Route Authority 

New route authority on a nonsubsidy basis was awarded during the fiscal year to Allegheny between Trenton and Albany, and between Louisville and 

Pittsburgh; to Piedmont and Lake Central between Louisville and Cincinnati; to Piedmont between Memphis/Nashville and points in the Carolinas and Virginia; to Frontier between Denver and Las Vegas; and to 

Mohawk between Albany and White Plains, N.Y. and Washington, D.C.

Trunkline Carriers 

Revenue passenger miles of the domestic trunklines increased by 24 percent over strike-depressed fiscal 1967 to 76.5 billion. Operating revenues increased by nearly 20 percent, to $4.7 billion. Operating expenses increased by 23 percent. Net income was $192 million, compared with $222 million in fiscal 1967, and return on investment, excluding investment tax credits, was 6.3 percent, which compares with 9.1 percent for fiscal 1967. 

The CAB's most significant activity centering on the trunkline carriers was the institution and subsequent processing of several investigations to determine the need for single-plane or first competitive service in numerous rapidly growing markets. To this end, hearings were held during the year in the Twin Cities-California Service Investigation involving the Minneapolis/ 

St. Paul-Los Angeles and Minneapolis/St. Paul-San Francisco markets; 

the Gulf States-Midwest Points Service Investigation dealing with the question of additional service in such Dallas markets as Detroit, Kansas City, and St. Louis, and such Chicago markets as San Antonio, Nashville, New Orleans, and Memphis; and the Reopened Pacific Northwest-Southwest Service Investigation dealing in part with improved (turnaround) services in such Denver and Salt Lake City markets as New Orleans, Houston, Dallas, Fort Worth, San Antonio, Portland, and Seattle-Tacoma; the

Southern Tier Competitive Nonstop Investigation, involving competitive nonstop service between 18 pairs of cities; the Dallas/Fort Worth-Phoenix

Nonstop Service case; the Service to Albuquerque case dealing with the question of additional service in a number of Albuquerque markets; and the Memphis/Huntsville/Birmingham-Los Angeles Service Investigation.

Cases having similar objectives, but with hearings still to be scheduled, include (principal origin/destination markets are shown in parentheses) : 

the Service to Salt Lake City Investigation (Chicago, New York, Washing ton, Baltimore, San Francisco); the North Carolina Points Service Investigation (from various North Carolina points to New York/Newark, Chicago, Miami); and the Pacific Northwest-California Service Investigation.

In addition the CAB has authorized services by temporary exemption pending full-scale certificate proceedings in a number of other markets where improvements in service to the public on an interim basis appeared warranted. 

Alaska Service The CAB approved the mergers of Alaska Airlines and Cordova Airlines, Alaska Airlines and Alaska Coastal Airlines; and Northern Consolidated 

Airlines and Wien Alaska Airlines. These mergers should result in strengthened carriers, improved services to the public, and a reduction in Federal subsidy payments in future years.

The CAB awarded Wien Consolidated competitive Anchorage-Fairbanks nonstop authority in the Anchorage-Fairbanks Service case.

The Board extended Alaska Airlines' exemption authority to serve Sitka and to operate one daily Sitka flight beyond Anchorage to Nome/Kotzebue until 60 days after a final decision on Alaska Airlines' application for certificate authority. 10

CAB Record of Computation of Route Miles Operated (unpublished). 

11

Hawaiian Service 

The two certificated interisland carriers, Aloha and Hawaiian Airlines, continued to provide a high level of air service without the aid of Federal subsidy. Air taxi operators also conduct air service within the island chain. 

Air Taxi Operators 

A number of CAB actions during the fiscal year reflect the growing importance of air taxi operations in the air transportation field. 

In order to provide greater flexibility in airmail service the Board on October 17, 1967, amended part 298 of its economic regulations to extend to June 30, 1969, the air taxi operator's authority to carry mail. The amendment permits air taxi mail service to begin under a temporary rather than a final mail rate and, under certain circumstances, allows the carriage of mail by air taxis in markets authorized to be served by certificated route carriers.

Also by an amendment to part 298 of the economic regulations, the CAB is permitting in fiscal 1969 air taxi operators to file joint tariffs with certificated air carriers. This action will encourage lower rates and fares for through service and permit integrated long-haul service. 

On December 5, 1967, in further recognition of the growth of the class of carrier, the Board instituted a proposed rulemaking proceeding, to amend part 298 to require that air taxi operators carry liability insurance and register with the CAB, with the filing of schedules, where applicable, a part of registration. A final decision in this proceeding is pending. 

During the fiscal year the CAB granted exemptions to four air taxi operators to use DC-3 type aircraft in specific geographic markets, or in unique and special services. In addition, 13 air taxi operators were granted exemption to use jet aircraft over the air taxi weight limitation, but only where the flight equipment was configured to accommodate not more than 10 passengers. Such authorizations were limited to charter operations. 

Exemptions also were granted to two air taxi operators to use, respectively, a Sikorsky S-58B helicopter and a Grumman Mallard amphibian aircraft, each over the prescribed air taxi aircraft weight. In two other cases, the 

Board approved arrangements permitting air taxi operators to provide certain substitute services for route carriers. 

Supplemental Air Carriers 

The Board's decision in the Reopened Supplemental Air Service case resulted in issuance of certificates of public convenience and necessity to 

Overseas National Airways, Inc.; Standard Airways, Inc.; and Vance International Airways, Inc.; bringing the total number of certificated supplemental air carriers to 13. This action concluded the Board's review of the fitness for supplemental authority of all applicants that were providing supplemental air service prior to passage of the supplemental air carrier legislation in 1962 (Public Law 87-528). Like the authority previously awarded to 10 carriers, the three whose cases were deferred-Overseas, Standard and Vance were granted unlimited domestic authority, temporary 12

international authority limited to specified geographic areas, and temporary inclusive tour charter authority.

The supplementals are continuing their reequipment program with all but three now operating some turbine equipment. Several have the "stretched" jets which have passenger capacity ranging up to 250. 

As of June 30, 1968, the supplemental carriers had total assets of $409 million, which constituted 3.8 percent of the air transport industry's total assets. Operating revenues of the supplementals for fiscal 1968 amounted to $287 million, or about 3.8 percent of the industry's total operating revenue. A comparison of significant financial data for the supplemental air carriers is given in table 1. 

Airfreight Forwarders

The airfreight forwarder industry continues to show growth in size and financial strength. The combined domestic and international forwarders' revenues from airfreight forwarding increased from $185,908 in fiscal year 1966 to $224,514 in fiscal 1967. The forwarders performed 1,993,589 consolidations in 1967, compared with 1,765,303 in 1966, and their combined share of the air cargo business increased from 16.2 percent in 1966 to 16.6 percent in 1967. Total tonnage handled by forwarders in 1967 was 15.3 percent greater than in 1966. Gross airfreight forwarding revenues for the industry increased $137,603 but net profit decreased $2,746 from 1966 to 1967.

Cargo Service 

To determine the need for additional all-cargo service across the Atlantic, the CAB held hearings in the Domestic Coterminal Points-Europe All-Cargo 

Service Investigation. The U.S. coterminals under consideration are 

Atlanta, Chicago, Cleveland, Dallas, Detroit, Los Angeles, Minneapolis/ 

St. Paul, San Francisco, Washington, Boston, New York, Philadelphia, Baltimore, and Seattle.

The CAB approved, subject to conditions, the transfer of the Slick Corp.'s route certificate to Airlift International, Inc. At that time Airlift operated two all-cargo north-south routes in Eastern United States. Slick held an east-west route (101) which extended between Boston and San Francisco- 

Oakland via Hartford-Springfield, New York-Newark, Indianapolis, Chicago, St. Louis, Dallas-Fort Worth and Los Angeles.

Slick, certificated in 1949, sought and received authority in 1965 to suspend its route service temporarily because of financial difficulties but it continued its military charter services for the Military Airlift Command (MAC). In 1966 the CAB approved the transfer to Airlift of Slick's assets used in MAC operations.

The CAB in the Detroit-California Nonstop Service Investigation granted operating authority to American, TWA, and United to engage in air transportation of property in all-cargo aircraft between Detroit and Los Angeles, and between Detroit and San Francisco/Oakland.

The CAB set down for hearing another cargo proceeding of sweeping significance. It involves an application of the Flying Tiger Line for broad expansion of its authority throughout the United States. The application precipitated numerous applications and motions to consolidate and the proceeding should develop into major proportions. 

Airport Congestion

The CAB took several steps designed to adjust the domestic certificated route system in the light of existing and potential congestion and air traffic delays at major airports.

Among other things, the Board turned greater attention to the use of satellite airports as a means of diverting excessive air traffic away from some of the busier metropolitan airports. In granting new certificate authority, the designation of nearby cities as hyphenated points in carriers' certificates also has been an anticongestion measure. 

Toward this end, too, the CAB has made investigation into helicopter services between larger airports and points within the metropolitan areas they serve, between midcity points alone, and between metropolitan area airports alone.

And, to provide a speedier means of transportation by eliminating travel time to and from existing airports and to eliminate congestion at the existing airports in order to speed transportation between certain airports and between those airports and other points, the Board is conducting the Northeast 

Corridor VTOL Investigation. 

Another proceeding was Service to White Plains, New York, which involves new route segments from White Plains to Chicago, Cincinnati, 14

era Cleveland, Pittsburgh, and St. Louis. It is hoped that such new services will relieve the three major New York airports of some traffic, particularly that originating or terminating in populous Westchester County. 

The Twin Cities-Milwaukee Long-Haul Investigation (involving service from Minneapolis and Milwaukee to east coast points and Seattle) and the Milwaukee Short-Haul Investigation (involving new route segments between Milwaukee and Columbus, Dayton, Cincinnati, Indianapolis, and

Louisville) have as a major purpose the routing of passenger traffic and aircraft flights in such a manner as to bypass the congested transfer point, Chicago.

Looking to improve air service to the Washington-Baltimore area and relieve congestion, the Washington-Baltimore Airport Investigation has been instituted. The investigation seeks to determine whether amendments to air carrier certificates or other procedures are necessary to achieve the objectives.

Similarly, the Additional Service to San Diego case is considering direct routings from San Diego to the east. This would relieve Los Angeles International Airport of a very substantial volume of connecting passenger traffic. 

The Board designated San Jose, a satellite of San Francisco, as a hyphenated point with San Francisco and Oakland on the transcontinental trunkline routes and said it will consider similar action for Ontario and Long 

Beach, Calif., in relation to Los Angeles.

Another facet in the Northeast Corridor VTOL Investigation is to determine the need for and the feasibility of establishing an air route between central-city communities using vertical take-off and landing aircraft and, if possible, other equipment usable on small landing sites to serve metropolitan areas in the Northeast corridor (the megalopolis along the Northeastern U.S. coast). Fourteen carriers have filed applications.

Additional measures were under consideration and will be taken in the coming fiscal year.

Control and Interlocking Relationships 

The CAB acted on 79 applications for approval of control or interlocking relationships under sections 408 and 409 of the act. Approximately 57 percent of these pertained to relationships involving direct air carriers and, generally, surface common carriers and persons engaged in a phase of aeronautics, including aeronautical equipment suppliers.

For example, approval was granted of the acquisition by Ling-Temco- Vought, Inc., a firm engaged in a phase of aeronautics, of control of 

Braniff Airways, Inc.; the acquisition by Stevens, Inc., an investment banking company, and Purdue University of control of Purdue Airlines, Inc.; and the acquisition by Transamerica Corp., a holding company with a subsidiary engaged in a phase of aeronautics, of Trans International Airlines, Inc. Such actions were made subject to appropriate conditions. 

In another significant action the CAB approved by exemption an agree ment involving Pan American World Airways, Inc.; Trans World Air lines, Inc.; and New York Airways, Inc.

, under which the two trunkline 15

carriers have agreed to provide a helicopter operator, New York Airways, interim financial support pending Board action on more complex relationships of long-term duration. 

Intercorporate Agreements 

The CAB reviewed and acted on a total of 1,089 intercarrier contracts and agreements. Major emphasis was placed on those multilateral agree ments constituting concerted actions by members of various associations of air carriers and/or foreign air carriers, such as the Air Transport Association and the International Air Transport Association, and other significant agreements involving important antitrust and other substantial public interest considerations.

These agreements involve such matters as administration of the associations, passenger, baggage and cargo practices, reservations and ticketing, free and reduced-rate transportation, interline passenger procedures, traffic documentation, passenger and cargo agency matters, airport usage, tariff publication, air express service, and a myriad of other subjects affecting air transportation.

For example, the CAB approved an agreement among the members of IATA constituting a substantial revision of the provisions for the regulation and conduct of the traffic conferences so as to establish permanent conference committees designed to enhance the efficiency and effectiveness of the traffic conferences.

In another significant action, the CAB approved an agreement between 

Trans World Airlines, Inc. and Eastern Air Lines, Inc. It relates to the standardization of the forthcoming Boeing 747 aircraft and related crew training, maintenance, overhaul, and the provisioning of spare parts. 

The remaining applications were filed by airfreight forwarders and involved relationships with local pickup and delivery carriers, airline sales agents, and affiliated companies engaged in various activities. 

Travel Agency Matters 

The domestic and international airlines, through their respective trade associations, have developed over the years comprehensive rules to govern travel agency matters. These rules must be filed with the CAB and are subject to approval or disapproval in the public interest. 

The CAB adopted a new economic regulation authorizing air carriers to grant free or reduced-rate transportation to travel agents on group familiarization tours between points within the United States, Puerto Rico, the Virgin Islands, American Samoa, and Guam, subject to prescribed conditions. This program supplements one approved last fiscal year which permits air carriers to grant reduced-rate privileges to individual agency employees for the purpose of enhancing their professional ability and capacity to generate, promote, and sell air transportation. 

Another significant event was the completion of a joint survey by the airline and agency industries of the role of the travel agent in the marketing of air transportation. Also of considerable importance was the commencement of a carrier-agent study of the cost of marketing domestic air travel. 16

During the year IATA members made substantial revision of its proce dures for the review of violations by travel agents. The CAB, however, ind cated certain additional revisions were required in the public interest. Com ments were received and the Board had approved the revised rules. The

INTERNATIONAL TRANSPORT Traffic Growth 

The international air transport scene continues to be, as in former years, one of dynamic growth and activity.

In fiscal 1968, a total of 13.4 million international air passenger arrivals and departures was counted at U.S. gateway points. This is based on statistical data compiled by the Immigration and Naturalization Service (INS) of the U.S. Department of Justice. Data for the last quarter of fiscal 1968 were derived on the basis of a constructed estimation. INS data excludes 

United States-Canada air passenger travel and certain other categories of travel.

The comparable figure for fiscal 1967 was 11.3 million, a 1968-over-1967 gain of 2.1 million or 18.6 percent. Figures for both years include passenger arrivals and departures for both U.S. and foreign air carriers. 

U.S. air carriers alone accounted for 7.3 million international air passen- ger arrivals and departures or 54.5 percent of the 1968 total of 13.4 million. 

In fiscal 1967, the share of the U.S. air carriers was 5.9 million or 52.2 percent. Our air carriers have thus improved their position as measured in terms of total international passenger arrivals and departures by air. 

The United States-Europe air passenger market continued to be the largest single market for U.S. air carriers. A total of 2.6 million international air passenger arrivals and departures for U.S. air carriers was contributed by this market alone. On a percentage basis, it accounted for 35.6 percent of all international air passenger arrivals and departures for U.S. air carriers. 

Including both U.S. and foreign carriers, a total of 1.4 million passenger arrivals and departures by sea (substantially produced by cruise travel) was counted at U.S. gateway points in fiscal 1968 or 9.4 percent of the combined sea and air total of 14.8 million.

Turning to international airfreight, a total of 778 million pounds of freight (excludes estimated low-value shipments and shipments by the U.S. 

Government for its own account) moved in and out of the Nation by air in 1967 (calendar year). In 1966 the total was 701 million pounds or a year-over-year gain of 77 million pounds or 11 percent. It was the smallest percentage gain in the 5-year period, 1962-67. 26

The U.S. air carrier share was 327 million pounds in 1967 or 42 percent of the total (778 million pounds). Foreign air carriers accounted for the balance of the movement. The 42-percent figure represented the highest measured share of the international airfreight market for U.S. air carriers in the 5-year period, 1962–67. In the 5-year period, the U.S. share has risen from 36.5 percent in 1962 to 42 percent in 1965 and in 1967.

The United States-Europe airfreight market is easily the leading international airfreight market for this country. In 1967, the United States- 

Europe airfreight market accounted for 426 million pounds of airfreight or 50.2 percent of the all-market total of 847 million pounds (including estimated low-value shipments). Airfreight movements between the United

States and the United Kingdom amounted to 102 million pounds or 12 percent, making it the leading component in the United States-Europe airfreight market. The United States-North America airfreight market (comprising the United States, Canada, Central America, and the Caribbean) was the next most important regional market. It contributed 200 million pounds of airfreight in 1967 or 23.6 percent of the overall market total of 

847 million pounds.

International Routes 

Extensive new and additional routes from the U.S. mainland to Hawaii, to the Orient, and to Australia have been proposed by the examiner in the Transpacific Route Investigation case, and oral argument, the final procedural step before decision by the Board, was held in mid-June. 

In other actions involving the Pacific area, the CAB granted Pan American's application for a temporary exemption to serve New York City as a coterminal point on its route. This permits Pan American to mount a full level of round-the-world service. 29

The Pacific Islands Local Service Investigation, involving local air serv ice needs over the South, Central, and Western Pacific area, is scheduled for hearing early in fiscal 1969. 

Service to the Trust Territories The CAB by exemption authorized Continental Air Lines, Inc., and Air 

Micronesia, Inc.

, to conduct subsidy-free air services for the Government of the Trust Territory of the Pacific Islands. 

Continental and the government of the Trust Territory of the Pacific Islands entered into a franchise agreement for the service on January 17, 1968. In the agreement Continental stated it would provide some or all of the service through an organization such as Air Micronesia, an air carrier owned by Continental (31 percent), Aloha Airlines, Inc. (20 percent), and United Micronesia Development Association (49 percent).

The air service contracted for in the franchise agreement is essentially the same as that which had been provided by Transocean Air Lines from 

1950 to 1960, and since 1960 by Pan American World Airways, Inc.

The air service described in the franchise agreement began May 16, 1968, and is comprised of scheduled transportation of persons, property, and mail, and "other services."

In the transatlantic area, the CAB instituted the East Coast Points- Europe Service Investigation to determine the need for service between 

Boston, Hartford, Philadelphia, Washington, Baltimore, on the one hand, and points in Norway, Sweden, Denmark, Poland, the Netherlands, Belgium, Luxembourg, Switzerland, Austria, Yugoslavia, Greece, and Turkey, on the other hand. In addition, the CAB will consider new transatlantic service on an expedited basis in the Miami-London Route Investigation. 

Regarding Western Hemisphere service, the CAB's decision in the Northeast-Bahamas Service case, approved by the President in February, awarded 

Northeast Airlines authority between the coterminal points Boston and

New York/Newark and points in the Bahamas. Eastern was awarded a route between the coterminal points Philadelphia and Baltimore/Washington (to be served through Friendship International Airport); Pan American received authority for an indefinite period to provide service between the coterminals New York/Newark and Washington (to be served through 

Dulles International Airport) and points in the Bahamas.

The need for improved service between the United States and Bermuda is being considered in the Bermuda Service Investigation. 

Oral arguments were completed during the year in the United States- Caribbean and United States-South America parts of the United States- Caribbean-South America Route Investigation. This is an extensive route investigation embracing the principal air transportation routes between the North and South American continents. The cases are now before the 

Board for decision.

U.S. Carrier Operations 

Twenty U.S. air carriers-combination and all-cargo-were engaged, as of June 30, 1968, in scheduled international air operations, including 30

transborder, regional, and intercontinental air services. They operated a variety of aircraft types, including the long-range Boeing 707 and Douglas 8 series and the short/midrange Boeing 727 and BAC 1–11. The aircraft fleet operated is made up for the most part of turbojets and turboprops. 

Few piston aircraft now remain. Currently, we are on the threshold of large-capacity aircraft, such as represented by the Boeing 747, the Lockheed 1011, and McDonnell-Douglas DC-10.

Collectively, U.S. air carriers engaged in international air transportation serve a total of 167 points in 119 foreign countries throughout the world.

Foreign Charter Carriers 

The CAB had a busy year with reference to foreign charter carriers. In addition to granting initial foreign air carrier permits to 11 carriers, authorizing charter-only services without limitation as to size of aircraft, the Board amended the existing permits of four carriers to broaden their authority. 

There are now 17 foreign charter carriers holding foreign air carrier permits. All carriers have been granted authority to engage in planeload charters between the United States and their homeland. Some have been authorized to operate split charters, others to operate inclusive tour charters, and some hold limited cargo charter authority in addition to passenger authority. 

Certain carriers have been authorized to operate charters between the United States and countries other than their homeland. For the first time the CAB granted charter-only authority for services other than transatlantic. It granted permits to six Canadian carriers to engage in charter transportation between the United States and Canada; and it granted a permit to a Korean carrier for service between the United States and Korea. The latter is noteworthy because it is the first permit limited to cargo charter transportation.

The CAB granted a temporary foreign air carrier permit to a German indirect air carrier (Carl E. Press doing business as Deugro International 

Transport, Inc.) authorizing it to engage in international airfreight forwarding between points in the United States and points outside. The term of the permit is 5 years.

Foreign Aircraft Flight Permits and Off-Route Charters by Foreign Air Carriers 

Processing of off-route charter authorizations for holders of foreign air carrier permits for regular route services and noncommon carriage flight permits to foreign companies under the provisions of section 1108(b) of the act continues to be a substantial activity, as reflected in the following table:

Bilateral Relations 

1964 1965 1966 1967 1968 255 237 232 295 287 622 654 633 599 569 877 891 865 894 856 Scheduled air transport operations between the United States and other countries are conducted for the most part on the basis of bilateral air transport agreements.

At present, 51 bilateral air transport agreements are in force. 

These include two agreements-with Colombia and Uruguay-which are provisionally in force and which require ratification by the Governments of the two countries to become effective definitively. Three other agreements are based on bilateral exchange of notes confirming application to Pakistan of the air transport agreement made between the United States and India 40

in 1946, and to Trinidad and Tobago, and to Jamaica of the air transport agreement concluded between the United States and the United Kingdom in 1946. 

The agreement signed by the United States and Spain in 1944 is the oldest agreement in force today. The most recent agreement was signed with 

Indonesia in January of this year. These agreements serve as the means to exchange commercial air rights—importantly, routes—with other countries.

In addition, 18 other countries-all former possessions of Belgium, France, and the United Kingdom-have assumed the rights and obligations relating to bilateral air transport agreements concluded between the United 

States and the latter countries.

Administration of bilateral air transport and other international aviation matters within the CAB is divided into four geographic areas. They are: area I, comprising Canada, Greenland, Mexico, the Caribbean, and Central and South America; area II, comprising the United Kingdom and 

Ireland, western and northern Europe, and eastern Europe, including the Soviet Union; area III, comprising southern Europe, the Near and Middle 

East, and Africa; and area IV, comprising the Far East and the Pacific 

Basin generally, including Australia, New Zealand, and Indonesia. 

The Board member for each area, and countries within each area which the United States has an air transport agreement, are shown below. Bolivia Brazil Canada

Chile Colombia

During the fiscal year, the following consultations, negotiations, and other bilateral activities were conducted: 

Area I 

A consultation with Brazil was held from June 13, 1967, through July 19, 1967. A second consultation session was held from February 15, 1968, through March 18, 1968. A third and concluding session is expected to be held in the next fiscal year. Talks with Colombia began on April 15, 1968, and recessed May 1, 1968, and are to resume in fiscal year 1969. Both the 

Brazil and Colombia consultations involved proposed route changes, capacity questions, and other subjects.

A brief consultation was held with Canada February 7–8, 1968, to discuss interpretation of certain language in the route schedule of the agreement. 

The problem is still under review. Informal talks were held June 14-20, 1968, in Washington, with Mexico.

Area II 

The United States exchanged notes with the Federal Republic of Germany on. April 5, 1968, providing for the substitution of Los Angeles for 

San Francisco on German route 3.

From August 30 to September 2, 1967, talks were held with France involving flexibility for U.S. carriers all-cargo operations without regard to a lineal route on services between points in France, the United Kingdom, and the Federal Republic of Germany. French talks also were held on the same subject, June 27-28, 1968, in Washington. Final agreements on this and other phases of the exchange are pending.

On May 6, 1968, the United States exchanged notes with the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics (U.S.S.R.), amending the bilateral agreement to name Montreal, London, Copenhagen, and Stockholm as intermediate points from which one might be selected at the beginning of each season for service on the New York-Moscow route. Stopovers will be permitted at the point selected. (Aeroflot, the Russian designated air carrier, was issued a foreign air carrier permit by the CAB on June 15, 1968. Service commenced July 15, 1968, between New York and Moscow via Montreal. 

Simultaneously, Pan American commenced service to Moscow from New 

York via Copenhagen.)

Negotiations which began in Prague, Czechoslovakia, in April 1967, looking to a new air transport agreement were continued on January 22, and concluded February 9, 1968. An ad referendum agreement which was reached has been submitted to the respective Governments for final approval. 

Area III 

Negotiations with Senegal seeking to establish an air transport services agreement were held in two sessions (October-November 1967 and January-February 1968). Since the Senegal talks involved a regional carrier, Air Afrique, they were viewed as the forerunner of similar negotiations with the other of the 12 Air Afrique nations in Western Africa. The discussions produced no final agreement, but it is anticipated that further talks will take place. 42

Consultations with the Republic of South Africa were held in an attempt to redefine the route schedules in the existing bilateral. The agreement that was reached, and signed on June 28, 1968, modified the route patterns of both countries making available service via South America and Europe as well as Africa.

In March-April the Government of the Kingdom of Greece sought to expand its route rights to the United States. The United States, on its part, wanted clarification of the "beyond" rights and confirmation of U.S. interpretations of the existing agreement. Although discussions in this first round proved inconclusive, agreement was reached in subsequent talks held in July 1968, after the end of fiscal period covered in this report. 

In other discussions: (1) the United States and Portugal met in consultations concerning interpretation of the current agreement; (2) informal high-level talks and an exchange of statistical data took place with Italy in Washington and Rome; and (3) the United States received indications from the Government of Israel that Israel will seek more extensive route rights in this country. It is clear that further talks will be scheduled with each of these countries (Portugal, Italy, and Israel) in fiscal 1969. 

Informal talks also were held with Italian aeronautical officials on three different occasions in the second half of the fiscal year looking toward a new United States-Italian bilateral agreement. 

Area IV 

During the fiscal year formal negotiations were held with Indonesia and informal talks with Pakistan and Japan. Negotiations with Indonesia in 

November 1967 resulted in a Bermuda-type bilateral agreement with the 

United States receiving rights to serve Djakarta and Bali via the North,

Central, and South Pacific and beyond, around-the-world, and on circle routings. In exchange, the Indonesians were granted a route to San Francisco via several intermediate points, including Tokyo, with a mandatory stop at Honolulu.

Discussions were held with Pakistan in November 1967 resulting in that country's being permitted to add 11 new intermediate points to its route description. 

In May 1968, following talks, Japan was permitted to serve Vancouver for a period of three years on flights to San Francisco and beyond to Mexico City and Central America without traffic rights between San Francisco and Vancouver.

In August 1967 the Government of Thailand lifted traffic restrictions previously placed on TWA's services to Hong Kong and on one of PAA's services to Bangkok. The Board's study "Tourism and the United States- 

Far East and Pacific Air Passenger Market" described in the 1967 annual report, was believed to have played a significant part in achieving the lifting of these restrictions.

In those cases where air transport operations are conducted between the United States and other countries without bilateral air transport agreements, as in the cases of Argentina, Italy, and the Philippines, operating authority is provided on a unilateral basis by either or both countries as required.

43

Multilateral Relations Through the Interagency Group on International Aviation (IGIA) the 

CAB continued its participation in the activities of the International Civil 

Aviation Organization (ICAO), the NATO Civil Aviation Planning Committee (CAPC), the European Civil Aviation Conference (ECAC) and other international organizations. 

During fiscal 1968, the Board helped to formulate a number of U.S. positions with respect to international conferences of ICAO, such as the 

Sixth ICAO Conference on North Atlantic Stations, the Seventh Session, Facilitation Division, and Regional Origin and Destination Statistics. 

As a result of the recommendations taken at the 1967 conference on charges for airports and route air navigation facilities, the Board has helped in developing U.S. positions on a number of matters, including (a) principles and factors in determining airport costs allocable to international civil aviation; (b) passenger service charges; and (c) formulation of charging methods.

During the latter part of fiscal 1968, the CAB also devoted considerable study to preparation of positions for the 16th session of the ICAO General Assembly scheduled for September 1968 in Buenos Aires. Not only is the 

Board responsible for developing positions on economic items for the assembly, but also through IGIA, participates in formulating other items on international problems such as joint financing, regional organizations, and international airmail. Since much of ICAO's work is of a continuing nature, the CAB staff regularly contributes to its secretariat activities. The African and South American studies on developing air services are examples. 

Special Activities 

The Board helped prepare the report to the President from the Industry- Government Task Force on Travel. The objectives of the study were to (a) determine what practical steps could be taken to produce early improvement in the travel sector of the balance of payments; (b) determine medium and long-term measures to bring U.S. travel expenditures and receipts into better balance, with recommendations on the steps needed in both the private and government sectors to accomplish this objective; (c) determine how best to help foreign visitors know and understand the United States and the American people better through firsthand experience, thus providing through tourism a new bridge of understanding between the 

United States and other countries.

Among the measures which the task force recommended to bring travel receipts and expenditures into better balance were these: 

1. Twenty-five-percent discount on round-trip fares to the United 

States on tickets purchased in Europe.¹ 

2. Fifty-percent reductions in regular domestic airlines, making these fares the best available anywhere in the world.2 

3. Ten-percent discounts on charter coach rates on trips involving 400 miles per day, effective May 1, 1968.¹ 

4. A new dynamic national travel policy. 

See footnotes on following page. 44

5. A strong national tourist office to coordinate the U.S. foreign tourism effort by 1969, starting with an annual budget in the range of $30 million. 

6. A national convention bureau. 

7. All Americans to be friendly and hospitable to foreign guests. 

8. U.S. residents should invite foreign friends and relatives to be guests in their homes this summer. 

9. U.S. Government inspection agencies should instruct officials that meeting, greeting, and processing international visitors as quickly and courteously as possible is a responsibility equal to any other responsibility they have.

In another area of special activity, the CAB is currently participating with the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) of the Department of 

Transportation (DOT) in a survey of air transport resources in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. The project and survey is sponsored by the 

Agency for International Development (AID) in cooperation with the Government of the Democratic Republic of the Congo. The terms of reference call for an economic/engineering analysis of the Congo's existing airport facilities and a determination of the development and improvement requirements and related priority needs. The project also will consider the type of aircraft needed to best meet the transport requirements of the Congo. Work on the project started on June 1, 1968, and is due to be completed in 6 months.

RATE REGULATION Promotional Fares Encouraged 

In its continuing climate of encouragement of promotional fares the CAB permitted extension for another year of the round-trip "Discover America" excursion fares, offering greater benefits to the traveling public with certain revisions in the weekend and "blackout" provisions.

The domestic carriers established a special fare for travel within the United States available to residents outside the Western Hemisphere, and to military or civilian personnel of the U.S. Government stationed outside the United States traveling at their own expense. Subject to a $79 minimum, and a 14-30-day-ticket validity, the fare provides a 50-percent discount from normal fares for local or interline travel during the period April 28 through December 15, 1968. These fares, together with the continuation of "Visit 

U.S.A." fares and various area fares, provide substantial incentive for foreign visitor travel throughout the United States. 

The CAB permitted the continuation of discount fares for military personnel on leave or furlough with most carriers offering reductions of 50 percent on a standby basis, and some offering a reservation service with about a one-third discount. 

Reduced fares also have been offered to youths aged 12 to 22.

The CAB permitted all the domestic trunkline carriers to round their regular fares and most promotional fares to whole dollar amounts. Publication of fares in whole dollar amounts should be a worthwhile step toward fare simplification.

Fare Structure Study 

The staff completed and distributed a report, "A Study of the Domestic 

Passenger Air Fare Structure," which recommended long and short-term actions to be taken to improve the industry's fare structure. Comments on these recommendations have been evaluated. Further studies will produce final staff recommendations and a basis from which fare policies should evolve to guide future fare proposals and adjustments to the present structure. 49

Domestic Cargo Rates 

The principal airlines canceled their air cargo deferred rates and service that provided slower service at rates below those for standard service. Flying 

Tiger, one of these carriers, filed economy airfreight rates for an airfreight service intermediate between deferred and standard. These rates were typically at a level intermediate between the canceled deferred rates and standard rates.

Numerous intercarrier agreements on accessorial cargo services have been filed for Board approval. 

The CAB has approved revised container agreements filed by the airlines, and also has approved further discussions on the subject of containerization 

The carrier representatives are continuing their informal discussions on airfreight liability and claim rules and practices. 

A booklet, "An Introduction to Air Freight Rates," was issued by the CAB in response to public need expressed during the regional air-cargo workshops, sponsored jointly in 1966 by the Board and the National Indus trial Traffic League. 

During the fiscal year the CAB cosponsored workshops held in Atlanta, Boston, and Houston.

In the Revised Air Express Minimum Charge for Live Animals and Birds Investigation, the CAB found a Railway Express Agency proposal to increase its charge for minimum shipments of live animals and birds to $8 was not justified. The Board concluded, however, that certain extra costs were incurred by REA in handling live animals and permitted the carrier to establish a minimum rate of $6.25 for this type of shipment. This is 75 cents more than the minimum rate applicable to other air express shipments. 

International Fares in Major Areas North Atlantic 

Approval of the North Atlantic fare structure, which included an introduction of group inclusive tour-basing fares, previously limited through March 31, 1968, was extended for 1 year. An additional proposal, to establish family fares for travel originating in Europe or the Middle East to the 

United States, Canada, or Mexico, in conjunction with the 14-21-day excursion fares or inclusive tour-basing fares, was introduced late in the fiscal year. The fares, offering round-trip travel to accompanying family members at the 1-way normal fares, are expected to stimulate travel to the United States and thus ease the balance-of-payments problems. 

The open-rate situation which had existed in the Pacific area since 1963 was terminated by an agreement negotiated at a special conference in Paris. 

The agreement, effective October 1, 1967, embraced the then existing current structure with certain revisions. The reductions included a decrease in west coast-Tokyo round-trip economy-class fares during peak seasons, with smaller reductions in the off-season period, as well as in the round-trip inclusive tour travel fares, and minimal reductions in first-class fares. Affinity group fares remain unchanged. Group-inclusive tour fares between the 50

west coast and Tokyo also were introduced, providing further reductions from the individual-inclusive tour fares.

While offering further reductions from those embraced in the October 1967 agreement, the fares were not considered to be commensurate as measured by the carriers' financial situation. The U.S. Carriers' 1967 earnings in the transpacific area continued high, with Northwest earning 28.6 percent return, excluding investment tax credits, and 30.1 percent including such credits. The corresponding profit elements for Pan American were 

16.4 and 18.1 percent.

Other Areas 

The normal first-class and economy-class fares were maintained, in general, between the United States on the one hand, and points in Central America, Northern South America, on the other, as well as on Mid-Atlantic and 

South Pacific routes. New promotional fares were established for the South

Pacific including 14-28-day excursion fares and 21-45-day inclusive tour fares in the west coast-Sydney market. Reductions in the 14-21-day excursion fare for Miami-Lisbon also were offered.

The fares applicable to long-haul routes were closed following agreements adopting tentative proposals resolved at the Vina del Mar Conference in October 1967. The present normal first-class and economy fares were maintained, subject to limited increases stemming mostly from limitations on permissible mileage circuity, and tightening conditions on promotional or excursion fares.

In-Flight Entertainment and Liquor Service 

Domestic-The CAB deferred effectiveness of rules which would have required carriers to file tariffs stating charges for in-flight movies and liquor service.

International-IATA resolutions providing for a $2.50 in-flight entertainment charge were approved by the CAB for a period through March 1969. 

Warsaw Convention 

Fourteen additional carriers became parties during fiscal 1968 to the agreement stipulating increased liability limits of $75,000 and absolute liability for personal injury and death of international passengers subject to the Warsaw Convention.

The agreement was adopted in May 1966 by the principal carriers serving the United States. It provided a basis for the United States to withdraw its "Notice of Denunciation of the Warsaw Convention" which had been given because of the low $8,300 liability limits prescribed in the 

Convention. 

The 41 U.S. carriers now parties to the interim agreement provide almost all of the passenger transportation provided by U.S. carriers. 51

Service Mail Rates The Domestic Service Mail Rate Investigation, affecting 22 airlines, was concluded in August 1967. It resulted in a reduction in the rate retroactively to January 1, 1967, from an average of 34 cents per ton-mile to about 30 cents. It is estimated that in fiscal 1968 the CAB rate reductions will save the Government about $12 million. This was the first service airmail rate proceeding concluded on a formal hearing basis. All previous rates were established under informal conference procedures except for nonpriority mail.

An investigation is pending of rates for the carriage of airmail in the transatlantic and transpacific areas as well as for military ordinary mail, which is official Department of Defense mail moving at lower priority than airmail.

Mail rates were established, effective in May 1968, for additional categories of space-available military mail, for which space-available airlift was authorized by Public Law 90–206. 

Also under investigation is a proceeding to establish rates for domestic nonpriority mail, including first-class letters and cards, and certain parcels and second-class publications on which a special fee has been paid by the mailer for space-available airlift.

A CAB show-cause order proposed establishment of final service mail rates for States-Alaska service at the same level as set for domestic mail services by the Domestic Service Mail Rate Investigation decision. Objec tions have been filed and the matter is under consideration. 

Mail rates have been established for a number of air taxi operators, following a Post Office Department program to utilize air taxi services for intercity movements where scheduled air services are not available at the times needed.

Service mail rate proceedings also are under consideration for two small intra-Alaska carriers.

Military Transportation 

In recent years, the CAB has analyzed the carriers' costs for operating Military Airlift Command international and domestic charters, and established the existing minimum rates under which DOD pays for such services for the various categories of transportation. In fiscal 1967 the volume of such services had grown to about $600 million, approximately the level expected to be maintained in fiscal 1968 and 1969. 

For fiscal 1969, it is estimated that the rate revisions entail an overall reduction of about $27 million in the rates for international services. 

Rate and Fare Matters: The Look Ahead 

Continuing the practice of recent years, the CAB will comprehensively review the carriers' costs to determine for fiscal 1970 the minimum rates for military charters and other services by civil air carriers for the Department of Defense.

The transatlantic and transpacific, the domestic nonpriority, and the intra-Alaska mail rate proceedings are expected to continue into fiscal 1969. 

The CAB plans to give careful attention to the impact of the Post Office Department expansion of first-class mail service by air on current domestic airmail rates. This service may eventually supersede the present separate airmail and nonpriority mail classes.

The IATA carriers will meet in the autumn 1968, at Cannes to establish passenger fares on a worldwide basis for a 2-year period commencing April 1969. Promotional fares to stimulate travel to the United States will be of special interest.

SUBSIDIES 

A continued downward trend was noted in subsidy payments made by the CAB to eligible air carriers in fiscal 1968 and the trend is expected to continue in the next fiscal year.

Estimates for fiscal 1968 of $55.2 million were $8.8 million below those of fiscal 1967. International services of the U.S. carriers, domestic trunklines (other than $1.3 million for local New England services which became subsidy free on January 1, 1968), and helicopter services, received no subsidy.

The table itemizes subsidy estimates by carrier services: [In thousands of dollars] Estimated subsidy 1 

fiscal 1968 Carrier services: 

Alaskan.. 

Domestic trunkline. Hawaiian.. Helicopter. International.

Local Service. 

Total.. 

5,835 1, 343 48, 066 55, 244 1 Detailed information as to the amounts of subsidy provided each airline is contained in a separate report of the CAB, published annually, entitled "Subsidy for U.S. 

Certificated Air Carriers." 

NOTE. During fiscal 1968 subsidy was reduced to 2 carrier groups and to the lowest dollar amount since fiscal 1959.

The two carrier groups receiving subsidy at the close of fiscal 1968 were: (1) the 9 local service carriers and (2) the four Alaskan carriers serving within, to, and from the State of Alaska. As indicated elsewhere in this report, the number of local carriers was reduced to nine by mergers (Central into Frontier; and Bonanza, Pacific, and West Coast into Air West), and the subsidized Alaskan carriers to four (Alaska Airlines, Alaska Coastal, and Cordova into Alaska Airlines; and Northern Consolidated and Wien into 

Northern Consolidated). This has resulted in subsidy reductions flowing from the anticipated merger economies along with improved services to the public.

The CAB anticipates that under the currently effective class rate IV subsidy payments will continue to decline. A major feature of this rate is an automatic revenue-growth adjustment that reduces each carrier's subsidy 58

payments as its passenger revenues increase. This factor, along with the subsidy impact of route modifications and mergers, is estimated to reduce local carrier subsidy accruals to not more than $43 million for fiscal 1969. This is a drop of over $24 million from the peak levels of fiscal 1963 despite an increase since of about 200 percent in passengers carried by the local airline industry. 59

REGULATION OF CARRIER ACCOUNTS AND REPORTS Carrier Accounts 

The project to automate the CAB statistical data collection and processing system is moving ahead at an accelerated pace. In this respect a rulemaking proceeding was instituted late in the fiscal year. It will provide an automated system of communicating traffic data between the industry and the CAB through the media of magnetic tapes and punched cards and, when fully operative, will enlarge the scope of data available to the CAB for both general purposes and individual route and rate proceedings. 

It also will provide the industry with uniform statistical classifications and, as needed, will enable the CAB to call for additional information from these detailed computerized data. 

In following a national business pattern of diversifying risks and providing for a fuller utilization of management talent, some air carriers entered into business combinations. These nonregulated ventures include hotel chains, land development, aircraft sales and rentals, tourism, car rentals, etc. Where controlling interest in investment exist in both regulated and nonregulated companies, certain practical regulatory problems have emerged. 

A common set of financial statements based on the common management of both regulated and nonregulated investments must be presented fairly and completely but segregated for regulatory purposes. Consolidated statements, wherein the identity of each type of investment would be merged as though one on an economic basis, would not be disclosed. It is preferable, however, that the nonregulated investment be reported on an economic basis reflecting any changes in equity since the acquisition cost of the original investment.

To reflect economic values so the relevant significance of intercorporate investments may be known to the CAB, the uniform system of accounts is being amended by substituting equity or economic basis for the present cost basis in accounting and reporting of nonregulated investments to the Board. 

The reporting of civilian charter travel information by the U.S. certificated air carriers was expanded from the reporting initially proposed to include similar information with respect to the foreign air carriers. This reporting will alleviate the basic information deficiency concerning civil 60

charters performed to or from the United States by foreign carriers as well as by U.S. carriers. 

A new regulation was promulgated which provides for the separate reporting of results of charter services performed under contracts with the Military Airlift Command (MAC). The reported data will keep the Board currently informed of cost levels and trends in MAC charter operations and serve as a basis for rate reviews. 

The CAB and the Interstate Commerce Commission have been developing a more comprehensive system of accounts and statistics for freight forwarders. Work on the project has resulted in the completion of drafts of balance sheet accounts and revenue classifications for the forwarders. 

Work is continuing on the expense account classifications and the remaining aspects of the system.

During the year accounting actions for six airline mergers were consummated. The combinations were effected by exchanges of stock thereby signifying a continuance of former interests without changing the recorded amount of assets or accumulated earnings of the absorbed airlines. As compared with the method of merging through purchasing of interests, this socalled pooling of interest has current financial, operational, and tax advantages to the parties and will have certain impacts on the uniform system of accounts.

The accounting treatment of bonds with detachable warrants was changed during this year. Heretofore the bond and the warrant were treated as one instrument and the proceeds recorded without recognition of the value of the warrant and its impact on the cost of capital. The accounting now prescribed requires the issuer to treat the debt as two instruments and to disclose the proceeds attributable to each to facilitate the determination of the issue.

Under consideration is a reporting requirement for scheduled air taxi operators which would shed light on their role in the overall transportation picture. 

Carrier Reports 

During fiscal 1968 the direct air carriers filed approximately 1,450 monthly and quarterly traffic reports and 400 quarterly and yearend financial reports covering about 14,500 individual schedules. The total includes 

156 monthly traffic reports and 52 quarterly financial reports filed by supplemental air carriers. The reporting of both the route air carriers and supplemental air carriers was closely scrutinized through desk review. 

Indirect air carriers (airfreight forwarders) filed 292 semiannual reports covering 2,628 individual schedules. These were reviewed for compliance with the CAB's economic regulations with special emphasis placed on conformance to the insurance requirements.

Under an agreement with the Securities and Exchange Commission, 23 registration statements and related documents were reviewed for comparability with information filed with the Board. Comments resulting from these reviews were submitted to the Securities and Exchange Commission. 

An amendment to the Uniform System of Accounts required the submission by certificated air carriers of special memorandum reports de- 61

signed to produce refinements of data for ground property investment and operating expenses to facilitate costing of passenger and cargo services. An analysis was prepared to determine the usefulness of the submitted information for costing purposes. This analysis revealed the necessity for these refinements of the normal reporting and also the necessity for further study of this area to develop a more complete costing procedure. 

Another amendment to the Uniform System of Accounts prescribed the accounting for capitalization of interest and amendment No. 11 to part 399 of the policy statements set forth the Board's policy for capitalizing interest. 

These amendments required the submission of special reports to show the pertinent data on the capitalization of interest from November 25, 1960, through December 31, 1966. In a special analysis of these reports, the interest capitalized was summarized and the accounting reviewed for accuracy and completeness.

Under this amendment the carriers may include imputed interest on equipment purchase deposits in the capitalized value of property and equipment which is included in the investment base for computing the return on investment.

An in-depth analysis of the financial condition of supplemental air carriers was performed. This review encompassed an evaluation of data for the year ended June 30, 1967, compared with the data for the year ended June 30, 1966. Since the supplemental carriers have become permanently certificated, the Board observes their growth individually and as a group. This study pointed to the financial strengths and weaknesses, individually and as a group, of the industry.

Field Audits of Air Carriers 

The 47 certificated route carriers and 13 certificated supplemental carriers had general offices in 36 cities in 19 States, the District of Columbia, and 

Puerto Rico. 

Field audits are conducted by staff assigned to the New York City and the San Francisco regional offices and the Miami permanent duty station. 

The scope of the audits varies. Full-scope audits are made of the subsidized and the supplemental air carriers; subject-type audits are made of nonsubsidized trunk and other certificated route carriers; and special examinations are made of all classes of air carriers as the need arises. 

The full-scope audits have three principal objectives: (1) protection against overpayments of subsidy, (2) compliance with the Board's accounting and reporting regulations, and (3) evaluation of financial fitness of the supplemental air carriers.

A subject-type audit concept was introduced during fiscal 1967 due to manpower limitations. Under this concept, major accounting and reporting subjects are selected for examination in lieu of making a complete fullscope audit. Priority is given to the subjects of greatest importance to the 

Board's regulatory program. By rotation of the subjects, the whole scope of the carriers' accounting and reporting is examined every 3 to 5 years. 

Flexibility is the major advantage of the subject-type audit. The scope of the audit may be expanded or contracted commensurate with manpower 62

LOVES 

go bi fuck capability. Use is made of the public accountants' audits as a supplement to these audits.

Fifty-two audits and 28 special examinations were completed; 29 of the audits were full scope; 23 were subject-type audits. The full-scope audits were made of 20 subsidized and one nonsubsidized and eight supplemental carriers. The special examinations covered various subjects such as air carrier nontransport activities, flight equipment pricing policies, depreciation problems, accounting for overage tickets, and tax problems related to mergers.

Automatic Data Processing (ADP) 

In fiscal 1968 the CAB concentrated on development of new ADP systems and data banks and increased user servicing to make the best use of its third generation computer facility. The effort resulted in a capability for handling through the computer new projects of expanded size and complexity. Also there was improved efficiency and timeliness of computer end-products. 

The CAB leased a file management system which will make possible more timely and effective servicing of form 41 data needs. As a result ADP_resources will become more widely adapted to user needs and on-hand data will become more readily available to CAB analysts. 

Cost Finding and Statistical Services 

Under the cost-finding program, aircraft operating cost and performance data were developed making extensive use of CAB automatic data processing (ADP) capability. The results are available to the public in a new Board publication, Aircraft Operating Cost and Performance Report. Computer ized costing programs also were used to develop cost information for the domestic fare structure study and for the study on the impact of new large jets and SST's on the U.S. air transportation system. 

A revised method which more accurately measures break-even passenger load factor formula was put into effect after discussion with the air carrier industry. One of its key features is the separation of costs between the carriers' passenger, all-cargo, and military contract operations. 63

Efforts were concentrated mainly on the redesign and reprograming of the passenger origin and destination survey system, and the system planning and design for the revised form 41 traffic data system which will be programed in fiscal 1969. These data banks are to be used in preparing passenger travel pattern publications, a recurrent aircraft cost-performance system, and numerous ad hoc statistical data base analyses and special reports. 

A revised, modernized survey program covering U.S. air carrier domestic and international passenger traffic, based on a plan developed by a joint CAB-industry working group, was put into effect January 1, 1968. Canada's concurrent adoption of essentially the same survey plan will facilitate continued exchange of transborder origin-destination data which the two countries have been doing for several years. The International Civil Aviation 

Organization also is exploring possibilities of an expanded exchange of origin-destination data to include other Western Hemisphere countries. This general program for improvement in available origin-destination data is aimed at better meeting the varied domestic and international data needs of the Government and industry. 

A new semiannual report initiated on airfreight provides traffic and financial statistics on the relative development of freight service on combination flights and on all-cargo flights of the certificated route air carriers. 

To effect a new Board policy, methods for computing data on air carrier earnings position and average investment in the publication Air Carrier Financial Statistics were changed. Excluded from total investment are equipment purchase deposits and capitalized interest thereon recorded prior to the time the equipment is placed in air transport service. The resulting investment base is used in computing rate of return on investment. 

The CAB also issued the 1967 edition of the biennial publication, Handbook of Airline Statistics, expanded to include new rate-of-return and investment data and aircraft utilization information. 

The following are the principal CAB air carrier traffic and financial statistical publications produced during the year: 

1. Origin-destination Survey of U.S.-Airline domestic and Interna tional Passenger Traffic; 

2. Air Carrier Traffic Statistics; 

3. Air Carrier Financial Statistics; 

4. Air Carrier Analytical Charts and Supplemental Carrier Statistics; 

5. Aircraft Operating Cost and Performance Report; 

6. Airport Activity Statistics of Certificated Route Air Carriers; 

7. Handbook of Airline Statistics.
