Franss PLANNING, PROGRAMING AND RESEARCH ACTIVITIES 

akland 1. erican awk en.

The CAB continued its efforts with a committee of the American Statistical Association to develop more effective techniques of statistical res Inter search in air transportation economics. tem fa ific tern. versal Chwest a. tol. ova. diary di The Board is attempting to improve air transportation statistics by collecting more information on passengers entering and departing the United States. 

Other Federal agencies are cooperating with this project. 

In coordination with the Air Transport Association, the Board has been working with the airlines to develop standby plans for the CAB-War Air Service program regional air priorities control offices. 

Several forecasts were completed, including forecast of 1968 scheduled domestic air passenger traffic for trunk carriers, and forecast of domestic passenger traffic for the 11 trunkline carriers-scheduled service, 1968-77. 

Additional studies completed or nearing completion were the impact on reported earnings and rate of return of differences in accounting method employed; competition in domestic air passenger markets; and a study of demand for air travel in city-pair markets, the latter an attempt to measure the elasticity of demand for air travel with respect to fares, income, travel times, and other factors.

Other studies that will carry into the next fiscal year include airline profitability and the impact of new large jets and SST's on the air transportation system, 1970-72, the latter focusing on breakeven loads and traffic required to maintain an economically viable U.S. air transportation system after these aircraft enter the commercial fleets.

For the first time in its 30-year history the CAB will be making available publicly, combined traffic, capacity, and financial information within a reasonable time after such information is reported to the Board by the carriers. 

The first publication, Quarterly Airline Industry Economic Report, will be released early in fiscal 1969.

The findings, analyses, and evaluations for local service air carriers were published in September 1967 in the second issue of the CAB Chart Book of Airline Economic Trends, and the third issue, devoted to the supplemental air carriers, neared completion by the end of the fiscal year. It includes data through calendar year 1967. Operating and financial data for the trunk airlines and for the local air carriers were being updated through calendar year 1967 in revised editions of chart books Nos. I and II, during the fiscal year. 

A fourth issue, devoted to comparisons among air transport, other transport modes, and the general economy, is scheduled for completion in fiscal 1969. 81

GOVERNMENT-INDUSTRY RELATIONS The CAB's Industry Advisory Committee on Aviation Mobilization last met June 18, 1968, to discuss identification of essential civil air routes in time of national emergency and other matters involving CAB planning for the 

War Air Service program under Executive Order 11090. 

There are no requirements for CAB funds to maintain the advisory committee. 

The committee members are: M. A. Brenner, American; D. D. Taylor, Eastern; B. G. Griggs, Jr., Northwest; J. T. Shannon, Pan American; T. K. 

Taylor, TWA; C. H. Barkes, United; P. J. Rodgers, Ozark; C. K. Faught, Alaska; C. Botsford, Flying Tiger; W. P. Neth, Seaboard World; E. J. 

Driscoll, National Air Carrier Association; J. L. McGruder and J. L.

Rodgers, Department of Transportation. 

CAB's Cooperation With Other Agencies 

The CAB provides staff members to serve on an ad hoc committee formed by the Office of Emergency Transportation. The Committee determines what procedures should be taken by the Federal Government to alleviate transportation problems during nondefense emergency situations.

Pursuant to the authority of the Secretary of Transportation under section 

2(b)(1) of the Department of Transportation Act, Public Law 89-670, a transportation facilitation committee was established effective August 1, 1967. All functions, records, and programs formerly the responsibility of the 

National Facilitation Committee, Department of Commerce, were assigned to the new Tranportation Facilitation Committee, Department of Transportation, effective August 1, 1967, in accordance with the Bureau of the 

Budget authorization of July 14, 1967. 

Another area of cooperation with the Office of Emergency Transportation is a task group established in 1964 to study common United States-Canada emergency transportation problems.

The Board serves on a coordination committee established by the Secretary of Transportation in January 1968, to develop a national program to alleviate the problems of aircraft noise including the sonic boom. 

The Board advises the Federal Aviation Administration in connection with its aircraft claimants group established to provide for the allocation of controlled material necessary in the manufacture of essential civil air carrier aircraft under the defense materials system. 82

The Board and the FAA have a joint committee known as the FAA/CAB Aircraft Dispersal Committee, established to develop the "safe haven airport program." The Committee arranges for the dispersal of civil aircraft in case of an attack on the United States. 

The CAB provides representatives to the Executive Council on Port Utilization and Control. This Committee provides advice and counsel to all ports during a national emergency.

The CAB with the Department of Transportation and the other regulatory agencies have a continuing program of coordination and exchange of information with respect to research activities. This avoids duplication and overlapping.

The CAB participates with ICC and FMC on an interagency group on joint rates and through services, the purpose of which is the facilitation of through services and simplification of tariffs. 

The CAB and National Transportation Safety Board have working arrangements designed to insure the flow and exchange of necessary data vital to the operation of both agencies. 83

ECONOMIC ENFORCEMENT 

The CAB's enforcement program objective is to achieve compliance throughout the industry with the economic provisions of the Federal Aviation Act of 1958, and the rules, regulations, orders, and certificates issued by the Board. The CAB also handles complaints from the public regarding the quality and dependability of air transportation service. 

Enforcement during fiscal 1968 was concentrated in five main categories: tariff violations, including rebating; unfair and deceptive practices; unauthorized air transportation; unauthorized control and interlocking relationships; and charter violations. 

The volume of overall enforcement activities during fiscal 1968 follows:

The following are examples of enforcement actions: 

International Air Cargo Tariff Violations.-Following informal com plaints, the Board's enforcement officers made an investigation to determine whether international air carriers may have been giving illegal concessions to freight forwarders to secure their cargo business. As a result of the investigation seven enforcement proceedings were instituted against international carriers alleging that these carriers were giving indirect rebates to airfreight forwarders.

All of the seven carriers have agreed to discontinue the rebates and have consented to the entry of cease-and-desist orders. 

Parallel cases against airfreight forwarders have been disposed of by stipulations and consent cease-and-desist orders. 

Discrimination.-A passenger filed a third-party complaint claiming he was excluded from a carrier's airport club lounge while other passengers of the same class were admitted to the same lounge because they held club memberships. Investigation of the report and procedures at all airport club 84

lounges resulted in enforcement complaints being filed against nine trunkline carriers alleging discrimination against those passengers excluded from the airlines airport lounges. One carrier moved to dismiss the complaint against it. The CAB on February 6, 1968, denied the motion to dismiss. 

Two carriers have agreed to admit passengers on a nondiscriminatory basis, and the proceedings against them have been discontinued. Similar agreements are being sought from the seven remaining carriers. 

Rebating. An extensive investigation under the nonpublic hearing procedures of the Board was concluded in a foreign air transportation case. The investigation resulted in a compromise civil penalty of $75,000—the largest ever collected by the agency-and a cease-and-desist order of the Board. 

An investigation had established that the carrier had made rebates to airfreight forwarders, had paid excessive travel agent commissions, and had granted free or reduced rate transportation to passengers for the period 

Airfreight Forwarders.-The Board issued a consent cease-and-desist order against a U.S. airfreight forwarder. This carrier was charged with a large number of tariff violations and unfair and deceptive business practices. A civil penalty of $70,000 was collected from the forwarder, the highest penalty ever paid by an airfreight forwarder. 

Unauthorized Air Transportation.—The Federal Aviation Act forbids foreign or domestic interstate air transportation unless authorized by the Board. Proceedings were conducted during the fiscal year to enjoin such transportation in both foreign and domestic areas. 

1. FOREIGN AIR CARRIERS.-The Venezuelan carrier, Venezolana Inter nacional de Aviacion (VIASA) entered into a series of agreements with the Dominican carrier, Compania Dominicana de Aviacion (CDA) involving routes between Santo Domingo, Dominican Republic, on the one hand, and Puerto Rico, on the other hand. Although VIASA has no authority to serve Puerto Rico or Santo Domingo/Miami, it undertook to provide CDA with DC-9 aircraft and crew for operations. An enforcement complaint was filed against VIASA seeking the issuance of an order which, among other things, would direct VIASA to discontinue engaging in unauthorized foreign air transportation by conducting charters for CDA or otherwise participating without authority in air transportation over CDA's routes. 

CDA at its own request became a party to the proceeding. The Board on 

June 26, 1968, adopted the examiner's initial decision ordering VIASA to cease and desist from violating the act. 

2. DOMESTIC CARRIERS.—An exhibit filed by a commercial operator in a certificate proceeding revealed that it had operated numerous flights with DC-3 equipment for at least 68 persons during the previous 9 months. 

Acting pursuant to rule 800 of the Board's procedural regulations, the operator was notified that it had violated the Federal Aviation Act and might be liable for a civil penalty for each unauthorized flight. The carrier undertook a drastic contraction of its activities and sought to compromise any liabilities for civil penalties, and made a $5,000 compromise penalty payment. 86

ngers 

Complaints From the Public 

In fiscal 1968 a survey was completed dealing with the causes and handling of customer complaints. The CAB ordered the survey as a result of a sharp rise in the number of complaints concerning the quality of airline service.

Mishandling of passenger baggage either by loss, damage or delay was by far the leading cause of complaint. Another major cause of complaint was flight delays.

Oversales still ranked high on the list of problems confronting the carriers at the time the survey was made. The Board, during the fiscal year, adopted a regulation providing for substantial denied boarding compensation and boarding priority rules. The regulation is expected to tighten up reservation and ticketing procedures with a resultant reduction in the number of oversales.

Those carriers considered to be deficient with complaint handling ability were advised of the survey findings. Reports explained what other carriers in the industry, considered more effective in the handling of consumer complaints, were doing to regain the good will of inconvenienced customers and eliminating causes of valid complaints. 

The Board also urged the Air Transport Association to study the baggage and scheduling problems in an effort to find solutions within the industry.

COURT ACTIVITIES 

A! orwz of Re pri ا والد Cab 

Cane 

Court action involving the CAB during fiscal 1968 produced a number of significant developments in cases of major importance to the Board. 

With the exception of one case decided by the Supreme Court, litigation for the most part consisted of direct statutory review of CAB orders in the U.S. courts of appeals. In addition, on two occasions during the period, litigants unsuccessful against the Board in the courts of appeals filed petitions for certiorari in the Supreme Court, and in each instance the Supreme Court refused to review the lower court's decision in favor of the Board. 

Thirty-two cases were disposed of during the fiscal year, with four cases pending at its close. This compares with 33 cases disposed of during fiscal 

1967, and 22 cases pending at the end of the year. 

The year's most significant litigation involved the Board's power to authorize inclusive tour charters by the supplemental air carriers. As reported last year, the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit set aside several Board orders in the Supplemental Air Service Proceeding and the Transatlantic Charter Investigation, authorizing inclusive tour charters by supplemental air carriers in foreign and overseas air transportation. The Second Circuit held that the inclusive tour charters authorized by the Board did not fall within the definition of "supplemental air transportation" in section 101 (33) of the Federal Aviation Act and hence that the awards were in excess of statutory authority. Pan American World Airways, et al. v. C.A.B., 380 F. 2d 

770 (1967). This decision conflicted squarely with the decision of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit which had sustained the Board's award of inclusive tour charter authority to the supplementals in interstate transportation. American Airlines, et al. v. C.A.B., 365 F. 2d 939 (1966). The second circuit's judgment was affirmed by an equally divided vote of the Supreme Court (Mr. Justice Marshall not participating) on 

May 27, 1968. World Airways v. Pan American World Airways, 88 S. Ct. 

1715.

The Supreme Court's mandate was stayed, however, pending disposition of a petition for rehearing. 

While the Supreme Court litigation was pending, four trunkline carriers (American, Eastern, Pan American, and TWA) petitioned the Second

Circuit for review of additional awards of inclusive tour charter authority made by the Board in the Reopened Supplemental Air Service Investiga tion. Trans World Airlines, et al. v. C.A.B., C.A. 2, No. 31,901. The sole issue tendered was the same as the one before the Supreme Court and the 89

case was held in abeyance pending that Court's decision. Following the 

Supreme Court's action, Public Law 90-514 was enacted on September 26, 1968. This law specifically empowered the Board to authorize inclusive tour charter trips and validated against any claim of lack of statutory authority in certificates previously issued by the Board embodying such authorizations. 

Another case involved the operation of inclusive tour charters, this time by a foreign air carrier. With the approval of the President, the CAB authorized Sudflug, a German carrier, to charter aircraft to German tour operators for the operation of tours from Germany to the United States, at the same time declining to exercise direct regulatory jurisdiction over the 

German tour operators. As reported last year, Pan American and TWA challenged the Board's declination of jurisdiction in both the District Court for the District of Columbia, where they sought a declaratory judgment and mandatory relief, and in the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit where they sought direct statutory review of the Board's order. Following dismissal of their district court action, Pan American and 

TWA appealed to the court of appeals and their appeal was consolidated with their petition in that court for direct review. The court of appeals upheld the Board. It found it unnecessary to pass upon the Board's contention that neither court had jurisdiction (though it indicated that this issue was substantial), holding instead that the Board has discretion under the act to decline to exercise regulatory control over the foreign tour operators and that its determination not to do so was a reasonable exercise of such discretion. Pan American World Airways, Inc., et al. v. C.A.B., 392 F. 2d 483 (1968). 

An important case was decided by the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit involving the applicability to new air route proceedings of the doctrine of Ashbacker Radio Corp. v. F.C.C., 326 U.S. 327 (which requires comparative consideration of mutually exclusive applications). 

National Airlines, Inc., et al. v. Civil Aeronautics Board, 392 F. 2d 504 

(1968). The Board has instituted five separate proceedings (Southern Tier 

Competitive Nonstop Investigation, Dallas/Ft. Worth-Phoenix Nonstop

Case; Service to Albuquerque Case, Southern Airways Route Realignment 

Investigation; and Memphis/Huntsville/Birmingham-Los Angeles Service

Investigation), dealing with the southern transcontinental tier of States, each with its own focus on the service needs of specific points. Delta and National contended that this procedure would deprive them of comparative consideration of various applications in one proceeding which were allegedly mutually exclusive of applications being considered in another, and that the Board's procedure was in any event an abuse of discretion. The court held that Ashbacker does not necessarily require the Board to enlarge a proceeding to include abutting segments or prohibit the Board from considering in a separate proceeding an area that borders, or even lies wholly within, the area covered in another proceeding. 

Another case involving a claim of denial of Ashbacker rights grew out of the Denver-Calgary Service Case, a proceeding instituted to determine whether Western Air Lines' certificate of public convenience and necessity should be amended so as to designate Denver as a coterminal with Great Falls, Mont., on its existing Great Falls-Calgary route, thereby making pos- 90

豆 ​היר E sible nonstop Denver-Calgary service in accordance with a new Air Transport Agreement between the United States and Canada granting to the United States a Denver/Great Falls-Calgary route. The Board declined to consolidate into the proceeding Frontier's application for Denver-Calgary nonstop authority upon the grounds that the Air Transport Agreement prohibited the United States from designating more than one airline for the route at least until January 17, 1969; that Western (which had for some years been operating Great Falls-Calgary under the prior agreement) had already been designated for the Great Falls-Calgary portion; and that the route is indivisible under the agreement. In a per curiam order, the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit agreed with the Board that Ashbacker did not require consolidation of Frontier's application since no other carrier could be designated to serve the route, or any part of it, regardless of whether Western's application for Denver-Calgary nonstop authority were granted or denied Frontier Airlines v. Civil Aeronautics Board, C.A. 

D.C. No. 21, 670 (Mar. 1, 1968). 

24 تو An important case involving subsidy determinations by the Board in the Reopened Trasatlantic Final Mail Rate Case was decided by the Court of 

Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit. Trans World Airlines, Inc. v. 

Civil Aeronautics Board, 385 F. 2d 648 (1967). The case involved TWA's subsidy mail rate for transatlantic mail operations during the period 1946 through 1953. The Board concluded that the carrier was entitled to $2.1 million less than it had actually received in temporary mail pay for those years and ordered a refund. The court sustained the Board's order, holding that it was rationally grounded on findings which were arrived at through the application of appropriate legal standards and which were supported by substantial evidence. The Supreme Court later denied TWA's petition for certiorari. 390 U.S. 944 (1968).

The Board's approval of the merger of Remmert-Werner, a fixed-base operator and the national distributor of the Sabreliner, a business jet aircraft, with Eastern Air Lines was sustained by the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit in Butler Aviation, et al. v. Civil Aeronautics Board, 

389 F. 2d 517 (1968). The Board found that Eastern's acquisition of R-W would not lessen competition in the air transportation, business jet aircraft, or aviation service industries, and that it would not otherwise be inconsistent with the public interest. The Board's order was challenged by Butler Aviation (also a fixed-based operator) and Lear Jet Industries (the manufacturer of a competing executive jet aircraft). In sustaining the order, the court held that, in passing upon applications of approvals of merger, the Board must take into consideration the policies of the antitrust laws, that it had done so in this case, and that its findings were supported by substantial evidence. 

A significant determination by the Board in the Motor Carrier-Airfreight Forwarder Investigation was set aside by the Court of Appeals for the Second 

Circuit in ABC Air Freight Co., Inc., et al. v. Civil Aeronautics Board, 391 

F. 2d 295 (1968). The Board has historically followed a restrictive policy when surface carriers or their affiliates have sought to enter the field of airfreight forwarding. In this case, however, it granted such authority for an experimental 5-year period to four long-haul motor carriers. It found that the authorizations would result in substantial benefits for the public, other air 91

cargo industries in general and the airfreight forwarder industry in particular, and the entry of motor carriers into the airfreight forwarding business would be unlikely to create adverse conflicts of interest or lead to unfair competitive practices or other illegal activities. 

The Board's decision was challenged by 18 airfreight forwarders. The court held that the Board's orders did not disclose whether it had established a policy of entry for all truckers who want to act as airfreight forwarders or had merely granted the four applications that were before it, and that if the former were the case, the Board had given inadequate consideration to the effect on the existing forwarders. The case was remanded to the 

Board for further proceedings.

A novel enforcement case was decided favorably to the Board in Capitol 

International Airways, Inc. v. Civil Aeronautics Board, 392 F. 2d 511 

C.A.D.C. (1968). In connection with a transatlantic charter, the chartering organization had, contrary to the charter contract, made payments for the charter to a travel agent who subsequently embezzled a portion of the funds. 

Capitol collected from the individual charter participants an amount sufficient to make up the difference between what it had actually received and the total charter price. The court sustained the Board's finding that the travel agent received the funds as agent for the carrier, and hence that its collection of the additional amounts violated the act's prohibition against collection of fares greater than those specified in the carrier's tariff. 

Two cases were filed involving the Washington-Baltimore Helicopter Service Investigation, a proceeding concerned with the need for and feasibility of scheduled helicopter service between Washington, Baltimore, and the airports serving those cities. One involved preliminary orders of the Board in the proceeding granting 10 of the fixed-wing carriers serving Washington and Baltimore time to form a jointly owned helicopter carrier and file an application in the proceeding on its behalf. The Board successfully sought dismissal of the petition for review on the ground that the orders were interlocutory. Washington-Baltimore Airways, Inc. v. Civil Aeronautics Board, C.A.D.C. No. 21,052 (1967). 

In the second case, several citizens associations of the Washington area sought review of the CAB's orders denying their applications for leave to intervene. They sought to intervene on the ground that their participation was essential to the proper presentation of so-called "environmental impact" issues.

The CAB ruled that these were matters which could be urged and supported by relevant evidence and position statements submitted pursuant to its rule 14 (14 CFR 302.14), and that, because the petitioners' interests were no different from those of thousands of other residents, limited participation was the only alternative to "a nearly uncontrollable" proceeding. After the close of the fiscal year the court determined that "the petitioners have not at the present time been prejudiced by any final order of the Board," granted them no immediate relief but retained jurisdiction of the proceeding. 

On its own initiative the Board permitted the petitioners to appear at oral argument and file a brief. Palisades Citizens Association, Inc., et al. v. Civil 

Aeronautics Board, C.A.D.C., No. 21,422, July 16, 1968. 

92

In Transcontinental Bus System, Inc., et. al. v. C.A.B., 383 F. 2d 466 

(1967) decided during the period covered by this report, though included in last year's report, the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit sustained the Board's dismissal of complaints against the validity of military standby tariffs but remanded to the Board for further consideration complaints against youth and young adult fares. The petitioners in that case (various bus companies) sought Supreme Court review of so much of the decision as was adverse to them. The Supreme Court denied certiorari. Transcontinental Bus 

System, Inc., et al. v. C.A.B., 390 U.S. 920 (1968). 

Two petitions for review were filed challenging the Board's refusal to institute an investigation into the family fare tariffs of 28 air carriers. One was filed in the Court of Appeals for the First Circuit. Trailways of New 

England v. Civil Aeronautics Board, C.A. 1, No. 7112. The other was filed in the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit by Transcontinental Bus System 

Inc., of which Trailways of New England is a wholly-owned subsidiary.

Transcontinental Bus System, Inc. v. Civil Aeronautics Board, C.A. 5, No. 

26,029. The first circuit denied a motion filed by the Board to dismiss the petition of Trailways of New England upon the ground that the petitioner lacked standing to obtain review. Thereafter the Board moved to transfer the fifth circuit case to the first circuit, and after the close of the fiscal year the motion was granted and the case is docketed in the first circuit as No. 7162. 93

LEGISLATIVE DEVELOPMENTS Legislation Enacted 

During the fiscal year, only two measures were enacted which directly affected the functions and activities of the CAB. 

Public Law 90-206, approved December 16, 1967, permits certain parcels not in excess of a specified weight and size mailed at or addressed to any Armed Forces post office, whether such post office is established within the United States or overseas, to be transported on a space-available basis by scheduled U.S. air carriers. The rates are fixed by the CAB, on payment, in addition to the regular surface rate of postage, of a special fee fixed by the Postmaster General. Such parcels may be transported by aircraft other than scheduled U.S. air carriers when adequate service by such carriers is not available. This public law also authorizes publications to be transported on a space available basis by scheduled U.S. air carriers at rates fixed by the CAB, on the request of the publisher and on payment of the regular postage plus an additional charge to be fixed by the Postmaster General. 

Public Law 90-321, approved May 29, 1968, known as the "Consumer

Credit Protection Act," vests the CAB with responsibility for enforcement of those provisions of the Act requiring full disclosure of the terms and conditions of credit charges with respect to air carriers and foreign air carriers subject to the Federal Aviation Act of 1958. Violations by air carriers or foreign air carriers of such provisions are deemed to be violations of requirements imposed under the Federal Aviation Act. The CAB's general powers under the Federal Aviation Act are available for the discharge of its duties under the Consumer Credit Protection Act. 

The only measure enacted which was of interest to aviation in general was 

Public Law 90-264, approved March 12, 1968, directing the Administrator of General Services to select as the site of a permanent heliport the parking facility to be constructed pursuant to the National Visitors Center Facilities 

Act of 1968 (82 Stat. 43). 

As stated earlier, shortly after the close of the fiscal year, a measure was enacted which was of substantial interest to the CAB and the aviation industry. This was Public Law 90-514, approved September 26, 1968, which specifically empowered the Board to authorize inclusive tour charters by the supplemental air carriers. 94

Matters Presented to the Congress 

During the fiscal year, the CAB presented its position to the Congress on many matters. In addition to presenting testimony on matters relating to its operations and to appropriations, the CAB presented testimony on the following bills: S. 3566 and H.R. 17685, designed to remove any doubts about the CAB's authority to permit supplemental air carriers to conduct inclusive tour charters with S. 3566 being substantially enacted as Public Law 90-514;

S. 3641 and S. 3645, providing for additional Federal financial assistance for airport development; and S. 3235, permitting carriers of the various modes of transportation to enter into agreements to establish joint rates and issue single bills of lading for through movements in international transportation. Also, at the request of the Congress, the CAB submitted approximately 64 reports on legislation to the Congress or to the Bureau of the Budget for coordination and subsequent submission to the Congress. 595

LEGISLATIVE PROPOSALS 

The CAB believes that there is a need for legislation which would: 

1. Authorize the CAB to establish just and reasonable rates, fares, and charges in foreign air transportaion in a manner similar to that now authorized with respect to domestic air transportation. 

2. Clarify the CAB's procedural powers with respect to the consolidation of proceedings pending before it so as to bar interlocutory appeals from its consolidation orders. 

3. Grant the CAB the right of independent representation through its own counsel in court proceedings in a manner comparable to that exercised by other independent regulatory agencies. 

4. Authorize the CAB to regulate the depreciation accounting practices of air carriers to the same extent as comparable agencies regulate such practices of carriers under their jurisdiction. 

5. Permit the CAB to issue appropriate remedial orders in enforcement proceedings directing the repayment of charges in excess of those contained in lawfully filed tariffs. 

6. (a) Limit subsidy eligibility to (1) air carriers providing service primarily of a short-haul, local or feeder nature, (2) the short-haul, local, or feeder operations of air carriers which received subsidy for such operations during the preceding 5 years, and (3) carriers conducting operations to, from, or within Alaska or Hawaii, or within the territories and possessions; and (b) Make ineligible for subsidy all carriers that had conducted operations without subsidy assistance for 5 consecutive years. 

The CAB also has under consideration various other legislative proposals. 

Legislation implementing the CAB's proposals will be submitted to the Congress. 96

ADMINISTRATION 

Only two administrative changes were made during fiscal 1968. President 

Lyndon B. Johnson appointed John H. Crooker, Jr., to the Board and designated him Chairman to succeed Charles S. Murphy, who resigned to become a consultant in the White House. 

Mr. Crooker's current term expires December 31, 1968. Later the Senate confirmed his reappointment to the Board by President Johnson for a full 

6-year term ending December 31, 1974.

The other change was the naming of Associate Chief Hearing Examiner 

Thomas L. Wrenn as Chief Hearing Examiner to succeed Francis W. Brown, who retired.

Fees and Charges for Special Services 

The Board on March 1, 1968, adopted a rule requiring payments of fees for filing certain documents and of license fees for the issuance, amendment, modification, renewal, or transfer of a certificate of public convenience and necessity to engage in air transportation. 

The system of filing and license fees was established in accordance with Congressional policy (5 U.S.C. § 140) Congress directed that "any work, service, publication, report, document, benefit, privilege, authority, use, franchise, license, permit, certificate, registration, or similar thing of value or utility performed, furnished, provided, granted, prepared, or issued by Federal any *** to or for agency any person except those engaged in the transaction of official business of the Government, shall be selfsustaining to the full extent possible ***." Each agency is empowered to prescribe by regulation fees which are fair and equitable "taking into consideration direct and indirect cost to the Government, value to the recipient, public policy or interest served, and other pertinent facts ***”

The fees schedule adopted represents the CAB's judgment as to what charges are fair and equitable, taking into account the statutory standards and the staff's best estimates of costs. Under the system of filing and license fees, the Board estimates that it will recover approximately $450,000 annually. HIGHLIGHTS OF 30 YEARS OF ACTIVITY OF THE CIVIL AERONAUTICS BOARD ... the Civil Aeronautics Act is the Magna Charta of aviation. With some defects and some uncertainties, it is nevertheless the finest thing that has happened to aviation since the World War conclusively proved its practicality." 

The Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938 (Public Law No. 706) established a five-member Civil Aeronautics Authority to foster the development of civil aeronautics, to regulate air commerce with respect to promoting greater growth and safety, to regulate and coordinate interstate and foreign air transportation, and to establish and maintain U.S. civil airways and all related civil aviation facilities. The passage of this Act terminated the former system of civil air transport promotion and regulation in which Federal jurisdiction was divided among the Department of Commerce, the Post Office Department, and the Interstate Commerce Commission. The Civil 

Aeronautics Act unified these separated duties under one agency and expanded the powers and jurisdiction of the Authority beyond those previously exercised by the Federal regulatory bodies dealing with civil air transportation in the United States. 

*Logan, Aeronautical Law Developments, 1939, 11 Jour. Air Law and Commerce 

1-16, 16, (1940). 101

DOMESTIC TRUNKLINES BECOME GIANTS 

It is one of the paradoxes of our modern technological and business world that although the air transportation industry was responsible for the birth of the Civil Aeronautics Board, the CAB has "raised" the industry from its infancy to its present gianthood. 

Today, 30 years after the Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938 established the CAB, the industry as a whole can look back on phenomenal growth and the industry is one of the most important factors in the Nation's economy. 

Two major factors appear to have shared about equally in the biggest part of the industry's progress during this period—the continued improvement in the national economy and the major technological advances affecting air transportation.

Used by the Masses As a jot of evidence in the technology story, one of today's "Stretch 8" aircraft (the DC-8-61) with high average daily utilization can produce in a year more available seat miles than the entire domestic trunkline industry produced in 1938. It is advancement like this and its accompanying operating economies that have transformed air transportation from a luxury available only to the wealthy and the businessman into a commonplace mode of travel for the masses with near-average economic means. 

Trunkline industry development since 1938 logically falls into three approximately 10-year periods, each marked by a major technological advance accompanied by significant economic and sociological change in the United States and throughout the world. The following table highlights certain measures of the domestic trunkline industry during the 30 years. 

Year ended Dec. 31 (in thousands) 

Year ended 1938 1948 1958 1967
