August 1938-June 1948.-The first of these decades was overshadowed by World War II but significant developments did take place which were not to become evident until later years. While the newly created independent 318-932 O - 68 8 103

aviation agency was translating grandfather rights into certificates of public convenience and necessity, concrete elements of supply and demand were being implanted. In this period, too, the local service industry was born as domestic route air carriers began to operate routes of lesser density between the smaller traffic centers and between these centers and principal centers. 

First of all, a manufacturing industry being expanded for the war also provided a base for research, design and mass production of aircraft and flight equipment. At the same time, vast numbers of military personnel and many members of the public grew increasingly aware of the advantages of air transportation.

The DC-3 Period 

This period is perhaps best remembered as the DC-3 era, because of the aircraft which dominated commercial air travel throughout the war and the immediate postwar years. Most significantly during this period, aviation became more than a weapon system, a sport or luxury for a few- -an estab- lished mode soon to become common, indeed vital, to our changing way of life.

Several noteworthy events involved the domestic trunkline industry in this decade. In fiscal year 1940 the first promotional tariff was introduced. 

One carrier published reduced fares applicable to and aimed at filling up planes less modern or smaller than the then latest types of aircraft. These fares were published for a 6-month period. 

That same year the Board denied a merger request of United Air Lines 

Transport Corp. (now United Air Lines, Inc.) and Western Air Express 

Corp. (now Western Airlines, Inc.) but approved an interchange between the two carriers at Salt Lake City. This was to avoid making sleeper passengers change planes at inconvenient hours during the night. 

Acquisition Approved 

During fiscal year 1941 the Board authorized on a permanent basis an airmail pickup and delivery service, unique in that the use of special apparatus obviated the need for landings and takeoffs and carriers could serve many towns situated close together and still maintain a rapid flight schedule. That year the Board also approved the acquisition by Transcontinental and Western Air, Inc. (now Trans World Airlines, Inc.) of Marquette Air lines, Inc. and its certificate of public convenience and necessity. 

In May, 1942 the trunkline industry sold approximately one-half of its aircraft to the Government for wartime use, thirty-five were to be used as cargo planes for the Air Transport Command and 165 in scheduled air transportation. Service was suspended at many points and the daily scheduled mileage was reduced to 55 percent of the prewar figure. This figure gradually increased during the war years until scheduled miles reached the prewar status.

The Board made a number of new route authorizations in fiscal year 1944, although equipment and other wartime restrictions prevented the inauguration of service over about one-half the new services authorized. In addition, the Board approved Western Air Lines' acquisition of control of Inland Air 

Lines and Northeast Airlines' purchase of Mayflower Airlines, Inc. 

104

Restrictions End 

Trunkline carrier operation under wartime restrictions had ended by the close of 1945. Equipment was becoming available for the full operation of authorized routes. The total domestic fleet exceeded the prewar fleet but the passenger load factor held at more than 87 percent. The Board continued to authorize many new route miles in the domestic air transportation system. 

A general fare reduction in fiscal year 1946 reduced the yield per revenue passenger-mile from 5.24 cents in fiscal 1945 to 4.73 in fiscal 1947. In the same period, operating expenses increased 61 percent while operating revenues increased only 33 percent. 

During fiscal year 1947 the operating expenses kept growing at a much faster rate than revenues, despite the Board's permitting a general fare increase which raised the average yield to approximately 5 cents per revenue passenger-mile. During this period the domestic trunkline industry experienced a $24 million operating loss. 

Fare Increase Okayed 

Carrier earnings continued depressed in fiscal 1948. The Board issued a revised "service" mail rate (as opposed to a "need" rate) to the five largest carriers in the industry (American, Eastern, Northwest, TWA, and United) which represented over 80 percent of the total air transportation performed. 

The Board permitted a further increase in passenger fares, but emphasized the need for some promotional tariffs to utilize unused capacity. Declining load factors were of major concern to the carriers and the Board. Board decisions in numerous cases continued to expand the authorized route pattern and during this period of concern over excess competition on short-haul segments, the Board began imposing long-haul service restrictions. In addition, the need for single plane service between major terminals without the expensive expansion or uneconomic duplication of individual carrier systems prompted added interest in domestic and international interchange air services. 

July 1948-June 1958.-Major changes in flight equipment, new passenger conveniences and service arrangements and promotional innovations introduced the trunkline industry's second decade under the Civil Aeronautics Act. Four engines, pressurized cabins, and the nose wheel had become the norms for trunkline service. Technological developments in both aircraft and navigational aids made nonstop service possible both day and night between most of the important cities in the United States, and no more than one en-route stop was necessary on coast-to-coast service. Average block speeds moved from less than 200 to over 300 miles an hour. 

Family Fare Plans 

Trunkline industry traffic rested on a near plateau a short while after 

World War II. To fill empty seats during off-peak days the trunklines late in 1948 introduced the family-fare plan, which permitted wives and children to travel with the head of the family for half the first-class fare on Mondays, Tuesdays, and Wednesdays—the family head paying the full first-class fare. 105

The same year, scheduled coach service was offered by the trunk carriersfirst at night, and about 3 years later, during the day. 

In 1950, the impact of the Korean hostilities was felt in substantial passenger and cargo traffic increases and by January 1, 1951, the big four carriers American, Eastern, TWA, and United had become subsidy-free on their domestic routes. Nine months later five more domestic operations, Braniff, Capital, Delta, Inland, and Western followed suit. 

This was the period when popular opinion held that all of the significant routes had been dispensed. Attention focused on the arrangement of the interchange in lieu of route extension. 

This also was the period when subsidy was administratively separated from service mail pay, surface-mail-by-air was begun and uniform multielement service mail rates were established. The CAB prescribed domestic minimum airfreight rates to halt destructive competition between the combination trunk and the all-cargo carriers.

New Domestic Awards 

Late in the second decade the Board granted a number of important domestic route awards designed to provide more effective competition in the top 50 markets and to strengthen the regional domestic trunk carriers. These included the New York-Chicago Service Case, the Denver Service Case, the 

Southwest-Northeast Service Case, the New York-Florida Case, and the Florida-Texas Service Case. 

The introduction of the DC-7 and Super Constellations, last of the huge piston engine airliners, made it possible regularly to schedule nonstop transcontinental and transatlantic flights in both directions. 

Chronologically, these were the major second decade events: 

Fiscal 1949-the domestic trunkline carriers reversed the trend of the 2 preceding years and showed an operating profit. The family-fare plan and air coach service were introduced. The Board began to examine possibilities for reducing route competition among the trunkline carriers and also certificated four domestic all-cargo carriers, two transcontinental, one north-south carrier and one carrier to operate certain routes in Texas. Emphasis continued on interchange services to provide new single plane services. 

Routes Strengthened

Fiscal 1950-the Board continued to devote much attention to refining and strengthening the existing route pattern, particularly in the area of uneconomic competitive authorization. Operating profits for the trunkline industry gained substantially over 1949.

Fiscal 1951-the trunkline industry growth trend turned sharply upward; operating profits increased almost 70 percent. Coach fares were increased from 4 to 42 cents per passenger mile.

Fiscal 1952-the trunkline industry continued its interest in providing new interchange services. The Board approved the merger of Braniff Airways and Mid-Continent Airlines and the merger of Western and Inland. The carriers adopted a fare increase of $1 per ticket which the Board approved.

106

Investigations Started 

Fiscal 1953-all but three of the current trunkline carriers were removed from subsidy. Coach traffic and other promotional fare traffic became an increasing portion of the trunkline total. The Post Office Department, with 

Board approval of rates, initiated the movement of first class mail by air. 

The merger of Delta Air Lines, Inc. with Chicago & Southern Air Lines, 

Inc., was approved. Implementation of new interchange services continued, but the Board instituted a number of new route investigations involving large areas of the country and the possibility of extensive new route authority. 

Fiscal 1954 after a slight reduction in operating profit for each of the 2 preceding years, the trunkline industry profit curve again turned upward. 

The trunkline carriers were kept busy procedurally by the large domestic area cases being heard by the Board where route expansion and added competition were the key words. Further domestic interchanges also were approved.

Fiscal 1955-the trunkline industry experienced its most profitable year up to that time. Coach passenger-miles had increased to 35 percent of the domestic trunklines' total passenger-mile volume. Unit costs showed a decline due to increased traffic and load factors. The Board continued its investigation of broad geographical areas with a reexamination of virtually the entire route structure in the areas involved. The Board approved Continental 

Air Lines' purchase of Pioneer Air Lines and the transfer of Pioneer's certifi cate to Continental.

Revamp Route Pattern 

Fiscal 1956- -a landmark year for the trunkline industry. The Board, to strengthen the less favorably endowed trunkline carriers and to assure effective competition and high quality of service, made extensive and comprehensive new route awards which, in effect, rewrote the domestic trunkline route structure. The Board handed down decisions which changed the operating authority of every trunkline carrier. Among these decisions: 

approval of Eastern Air Lines' acquisition of Colonial Airlines. By the close of fiscal 1956 only one trunkline carrier (Northeast) required subsidy. It was hoped that the extension of Northeast to Florida (in August 1956) would permit this carrier to become self-sufficient. Operating profit for the trunkline industry declined slightly in this period, but the figure remains third highest in the industry's history. The first turboprop aircraft in domestic transportation was introduced by Capital Air Lines-the British Vickers Viscount for initial service between Washington and Chicago. It also was the first use of a foreign-built aircraft in U.S. airline service in modern times. 

Fiscal 1957-the Board continued remaking the domestic trunkline route structure with final decisions in a number of area type route cases. Traffic continued its steady rate of growth but operating profit for calendar 1957 reached its lowest point in the 1950's, falling more than 50 percent from the preceding year. Both the Douglas DC-7C and the Lockheed L-1649A 

entered scheduled service in this year and made possible scheduling of nonstop transcontinental services. The airlines became the leading intercity passenger common carrier in terms of passenger-miles. 107

Traffic Declines 

Fiscal 1958-the Board made fewer decisions affecting the trunkline route structure but in calendar 1958 trunkline passenger traffic declined for the first time in 10 years, an occurrence attributed to the economic recession of 1957-58 and the two fare increases during 1958. This traffic decline was only the second sustained in domestic trunkline operations since 1938 and was not to recur for the next decade. Despite the traffic decline trunkline carriers' operating profit in calendar 1958 more than doubled the 1957 figure.

July 1958-June 1968.-The most recent 10-year period of trunkline development began administratively with the passage of the Federal Aviation Act of 1958 and operationally by the introduction of turbojet air service in our domestic system.

Transcontinental superskyways, established from New York and Washington to Los Angeles and San Francisco, helped to accommodate trunkline operations and control mounting congestion aloft. Planes operating within these routes at above 17,000 feet on three 40-mile wide transcontinental corridors were put under absolute ground control and traffic separation, regardless of weather.

Adequacy of Service 

As the Nation's towns and cities became aware of the importance of air transportation to their economies, a number of formal complaints were filed alleging inadequacy of service and the CAB initiated and decided several important adequacy of service investigation cases. In the Toledo Adequacy of Service Investigation, the Washington-Baltimore Adequacy of Service In vestigation and the Flint-Grand Rapids Adequacy of Service Investigation the Board found that air service was not legally adequate and ordered certain remedial services to be provided. 

Major overhaul of trunkline domestic route systems also was made during this period. In the Great Lakes-Southeast Service Case and the St. Louis- 

Southeast Service Case, Northwest was extended from Chicago to Miami, Capital from Buffalo to Miami and TWA from St. Louis to Miami. Single carrier service across the southern tier of States was made possible for the first time as a result of the Southern Transcontinental Service Case (docket 

11699). National was certificated to serve between Florida and California; Eastern was awarded Miami-Fort Worth authority; Delta between 

Georgia and California; and American and Continental between Texas and California.

UAL, Capital Merge 

The Board approved the United-Capital merger in order to let airline service continue over the routes of financially distressed Capital Airlines.

During this same period in the United Air Lines Competitive Service Investigation, the Board concluded that the public convenience and necessity required the restoration of two-carrier competitive service in four markets where competition had been eliminated as a result of the United-Capital merger. American was selected to provide nonstop service between New 

York and Cleveland and Northwest to provide service between Cleveland 108

and Chicago, Cleveland and Philadelphia, and between Philadelphia and Detroit.

As the third decade of trunkline operations under the act drew to a close, preparations were afoot for a new generation of aircraft and explorations of world markets. The transocean investigations were renewed as trunk carriers placed firm commitments for stretched subsonics, airbusses and other jets and looked ahead to the next decade when CAB concern will include transportation of three times as many passengers moving three times the speed of sound.

These are some highlights of the industry's progress through the last 

10 years.

Turbojets Enter Service 

Fiscal 1959-the Board continued, through route authorizations, its policy to maintain balance in the industry as to carrier size and opportunity. Traffic resumed its growth trend and the trunkline carriers' operating profit increased about 10 percent over the previous year. December 10, 1958, a landmark date for the domestic trunkline industry, saw the first turbojet aircraft put into domestic airline service. In January 1959 Northeast Airlines went off subsidy, marking the first time in history that the entire trunkline industry was on a subsidy-free status. 

Fiscal 1960-major emphasis of the Board's route work changed from trunkline area to local service area. The Board also issued decisions in three adequacy of service cases and ordered certain additional services. Traffic continued to grow in calendar 1960 but at a declining rate. Operating profit decreased 70 percent from 1959.

Engineers Strike 

Fiscal 1961-the Board approved the United-Capital merger finding that Capital was financially in extremis and that no alternative to merger was shown to offer satisfactory solution. Major route activity in this period was the certification for the first time of single-carrier transcontinental service across the southern tier of States. The Board permitted a further increase in air fares during this period. In the aggregate, fare increases since 1958 totaled about 25 percent. In calendar 1961 the trunklines showed their first year of operating loss since 1947. Passenger revenue miles increased less than 

1 percent over calendar 1960 and passenger load factors dropped more than 

3 percentage points. Flight engineers struck eight U.S. carriers for 7 days. in February. In the first quarter of 1961, for the first time in airline history, domestic trunkline coach passenger-miles exceeded first class passengermiles.

Fiscal 1962-turbine-powered aircraft accounted for more than threefourths of the domestic trunkline passenger-mile volume. The Board permitted a 3 percent increase in domestic passenger fares but continued to encourage promotional fares and permitted extensive experimentation in this respect. Coach traffic accounted for 66 percent of the total revenue passenger-miles flown. Domestic trunk operating results soared from a loss of $11 million in 1961 to a profit of $75 million in 1962. Special items and taxes reduced the net increase to the carriers to approximately $8 million. 109

Fiscal 1963-Activity concerning major routes of the trunklines centered around the elimination of trunkline service at low traffic-generating points. 

In each instance where a trunkline carrier was eliminated, alternate air service was available either from other trunk or local service carriers or both. Domestic trunkline passenger-miles rose consistently. Operating profit rose to more than $129 million for calendar 1963, reduced, after special items and taxes, to approximately $13 million. This year the first U.S. carrier orders were placed for the Concorde supersonic aircraft. In calendar 

1963 the airlines' share of the total common carrier intercity market topped all other modes combined (in terms of passenger-miles). 

Favorable Trends 

Fiscal 1964-The domestic trunkline industry had now passed the crest of the wave created by the conversion to turbojet operations. All economic signs were favorable, traffic growth was accelerating, profits were increasing and the public was being better served. Calendar 1964 operating profit rose to near $300 million and the net increase after special items and taxes topped $134 million. Coach passenger-miles reached 72 percent of the total domestic trunkline passenger-miles.

Fiscal 1965-The Board continued to give hearing priority to trunkline deletion applications and single-airport-service consolidations which would provide opportunities for economies without adverse effect on the public convenience. A number of route adjustments resulted from several trunkline route extension cases. Traffic continued its accelerating growth and load factors improved over the previous year. Calendar 1965 operating profit for the domestic trunkline industry exceeded $416 million. Net income after taxes and special items was nearly $222 million. 

Fiscal 1966-A year of unprecedented prosperity for the domestic trunkline industry. Expanding at more than several times the rate of the general economy, air passenger traffic increased 22.5 percent over 1965; revenues were up 19 percent exceeding $3.5 billion for the first time; cost increases were held below revenue gains so that net income reached an alltime high of $259 million. The Board again turned its attention in the route area to new and competitive service trunkline route investigations. The Board also approved the merger of Eastern and Mackey Air Lines. 

Fiscal 1967-Domestic trunkline carriers' economic progress continued. 

Revenue passenger-miles increased by 13 percent, passenger revenues by about 10 percent, but operating expenses were up 14 percent, resulting in an 8.1 percent return on investment. In a far-ranging series of cases the CAB initiated action to hear the need for competitive service in a number of major route cases and also made a number of new route awards between major cities in the Pacific Northwest and cities in the Southwest area. The trunks continued the emphasis on promotional fares and, with Board approval, implemented a number of new fares and renewed several existing fare plans. 110

LOCAL CARRIERS MAKE BIG STRIDES 

While some segments of the air transportation industry were in existence when the Civil Aeronautics Act was passed in 1938, the local service industry was not even a gleam in the legislators' eyes. It was not until the early 1940's that the CAB began to wonder whether air service should be authorized to smaller communities, in addition to those included in the trunklines' certificates.

In March 1943, the Board instituted the investigation of local, feeder, and pickup air service to "examine into (a) the propriety of extending air transportation to communities and localities throughout the continental United States to which such transportation may not appear warranted under usual economic conditions or under existing standards of operation, and (b) the coordination of any such transportation that may be authorized with air transportation presently authorized, or which may be authorized under usual conditions and existing standards." 

A hearing was held and the existing air carriers, applicants for local service routes, manufacturers, and other segments of the transportation industry presented comprehensive evidence on the questions raised by the Board. 

Subsidized Experiment 

The CAB's decision in the investigation was somewhat less than optimistic as to the future of local air transportation. However, a pinpoint of hope pierced the mantle of doubt which arose in that proceeding and the Board found that local service warranted a try. The Board thus decided to experiment on a subsidized basis and to hear cases involving the award of temporary certificates to carrier applicants.

The Board cautioned, however, that certain safeguards should be applied to insure that the Federal Treasury would not be overly burdened by the new services. First, it provided that the authorizations should be of a temporary nature, probably of 3 years' duration. Also, the Board stated that the authorization should be confined to those operations which show a justifiable expectation of success at a reasonable cost to the Government. 

The first local service route award had been made in 1943, even before the Board's decision in the investigation. It involved service between the 

Texas cities of Amarillo-Houston and the intermediate points Austin, San

Angelo, Abilene, and Lubbock. It was granted to Essair Inc. (later to become 

Pioneer Airlines). 

111

Area Cases Investigated 

Between 1945 and 1949 the Board conducted a series of area cases in which it investigated the need for local air transportation in virtually every section of the country. The first case of this type to be decided by the 

Board was the Rocky Mountain States Area Case, in which two new local service carriers, Monarch Airlines and Summit Airways (later Challenger) were awarded temporary route authority. (Monarch and Challenger subsequently merged into Frontier Airlines.)

From these area cases evolved the original concept of what local and feeder transportation should be, although it was not specifically defined by the Board. The new class of carriers was to serve the smaller communities in their areas, and to feed passengers from them into the larger area hub cities, either for connections to continue their journeys, or to serve some interest in the hub city itself. In order to insure that these carriers would focus their attention on service to intermediate points on their routes, they were specifically required to serve each intermediate point on each flight provided. The condition became standard in the certificates of all local service carriers.

By the end of 1949, 20 local service carriers had been certificated. In addition to the three named above, there were: Arizona Airways, Florida Air lines, Southwest Airways, West Coast Airlines, Empire Airlines, E. W. Wiggins Airways, Trans-Texas Airways, Central Airlines, Wisconsin Central Air lines (later North Central Airlines), Iowa Airplane Co., Parks Air Transport,

Southern Airways, Piedmont Aviation, Roscoe Turner Aeronautical Corp. 

(later Lake Central Airlines), All American Airways (later Allegheny Air lines), Robinson Aviation Inc. (later Mohawk Airlines), and Bonanza Air

Lines. 

By 1949, the first round of area cases had been completed and the Board had formulated a rudimentary pattern of local air transportation for the entire United States. The carriers' operations were in full swing. They were generating about 135 million passenger-miles in that year and the DC-3 had already become the workhorse of the fleet. 

Extension for Feeders 

A year earlier the CAB had announced a program for considering extension of the temporary feeder line certificates, a number of which were then approaching the end of their original 3-year terms. Show cause orders were utilized for this purpose and resulted in a number of formal renewal cases. 

These cases included route strengthening and service expansion issues as well as renewal issues.

Through these cases and several mergers and route transfers the local service industry began to crystallize into a more substantial transportation system. The weakest of the carriers were either not renewed or were merged into other companies.

Moreover, the carriers were given some measure of relief from the rather harsh point-to-point service requirements in their certificates. They were awarded a type of skip-stop authority which permitted the overflight of intermediate points after a minimum amount of service had been provided at 112

M these points per day. However, they were still required to serve two intermediate points between terminals; and even then specific stop restrictions were imposed to preclude any chance of competition with trunklines. 

Number Decreases 

By 1955 the number of local service carriers had been reduced to 13 and Congress felt that the local service industry had reached a stage in its development where it warranted permanent certification. 

The Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938 was amended to require such permanent certification. Grandfather certificate eligibility was granted to the local service airlines and the act provided that the Board grant permanent certification to all of the terminal points and one-half of the intermediate points named on the certificates of the above-named carriers. It left to the Board the question of whether the remaining intermediate points should be certificated on a permanent or a temporary basis. The Board immediately set down permanent certificate cases to effectuate the change in the act. 

Having given the local carriers the dignity and stability inherent in permanent certificates, the CAB moved on in 1956 to a series of cases designed primarily to hear further expansion of local airline service. The first of these proceedings was the Seven States Area Investigation. 

"Use-It, Lose-It" Policy

In this case, the Board enunciated for the first time its "use-it-or-lose-it" policy which was in essence an experimental program to give numerous additional communities an opportunity to prove that they could generate sufficient traffic within a reasonable period of time to warrant their receiving airline service.

The Seven States Case and the ensuing area cases also were used to improve the local carriers' route systems by (1) suspending or deleting trunklines at smaller points and giving the locals exclusive authority to serve them, and (2) further liberalization of the locals' skip-stop authority. The carriers were authorized to use skip-stop authority to fly nonstop in noncompetitive terminal-to-terminal markets, and to fly one-stop in competitive terminal-toterminal markets.

The area cases and the followup "use-it-or-lose-it" cases constituted a major portion of the Board's route activity in the late 1950's and early 1960's. 

Numerous cities received first airline service in these proceedings. The area cases were largely responsible for an increase of about 37 percent in the unduplicated route miles of local service carriers between 1958 and the end of 1963. 

Loans Assist Carriers 

By 1958 the locals had begun phasing out the DC-3 airplane and replacing it with higher capacity two-engine piston equipment. Legislation in 1958 authorizing Federal loan guarantees for equipment acquisitions assisted the carriers in these and later equipment acquisition programs. Now the local service carriers were generating 820 million revenue passenger-miles per year and moving upward rapidly. 113

The CAB, having established by the early 1960's a comprehensive pattern of local air transportation, began to concentrate more on improving the economics of these operations, and the financial position of the local carriers. 

In 1961, the Board adopted a class rate concept of subsidy ratemaking which eliminated the uncertainty which previously attended extended open rate periods.

The rate policy together with guaranteed loans stimulated investor interest greatly at a time when the entire airline industry was on the threshold of equipment revolution—the advent of pure jet aircraft.

The local carriers were able to finance and acquire first turboprop equipment and later short- and medium-haul jets and became equal competitors with trunklines for authority in short- and medium-haul relatively high density markets in which this equipment could be operated efficiently. 

Skip-Stop Authority 

Without relieving the locals of their obligation to serve the small communities, the CAB began to place more discretion in the hands of air carrier management by increasing operating flexibility through the further liberalization of skip-stop authority.

The Board began relieving the carriers of the requirements of stop restrictions in markets served by trunklines, and authorizing local carriers to fly nonstop in large terminal-to-terminal markets served by trunklines. 

In order to reduce the tax burden on such operations, the awards were accompanied by the provision that operations under the improved authority would be ineligible for Federal subsidy.

New Areas for Locals 

The CAB also began to consider allowing the local carriers to break out of their historic areas in order to connect points served within their systems with large traffic-generating hubs in other regions of the country not too remote from their historic areas. For example, Piedmont Aviation has been given exclusive authority to provide direct service from such points on its system as Charlottesville, Staunton, Lynchburg, and Roanoke to New York.

Thus, the carrier may now carry, all the way from origin to destination, passengers which it was previously carrying to a connecting point and turning over to another airline.

The Board also granted a route extension to Southern Airways from Columbus, Ga. to New York, and is considering Ozark Air Lines' route extension from Peoria-Springfield to New York, Frontier from Topeka, Kans. into Chicago and several other similar cases. 

The local service industry has indeed come a long way since 1944 when there was not much hope for its future because of the state of development of the surface transportation system. 

Supplants Rail Service

In 1967 the local service industry generated in excess of 4 billion revenue passenger-miles. Of course large numbers of passengers still ride long-distance buses, and air transportation will never replace the private automobile 114

for some purposes. But local air transportation has, to a large extent, supplanted passenger rail service, and it promises to make greater inroads into the province of the other surface modes as equipment technology advances. 

Board rate policy accompanied by vigorous route activity has started the Federal subsidy bill for local carriers downward, and at the same time made more service to the public available. At the present time, several local service carriers are approaching break-even operations. Others are decreasing their break-even needs, and the recent mergers have resulted in the elimination of some of the weakest of the local carriers. 

At present there are nine local carriers and the industry is stronger than ever and is looking forward to greater advances. 115

AIR TAXIS COME ON THE SCENE 

From an economic regulatory standpoint, the air taxi story began in 1938 

with the passage of the Civil Aeronautics Act. 

When the Board assumed jurisdiction as the regulatory body for air transportation on August 22, 1938, there were two general types of common carrier air transportation enterprises:

Scheduled service over prescribed routes operated by 16 air carriers, who had received "grandfather" certificates of public convenience and necessity, and

Several carriers providing nonscheduled air service on a demand basis. 

On October 18, 1938 the Board adopted a general regulation exempting persons engaged in air transportation on a nonscheduled basis only from the requirement of obtaining a certificate. This exemption was the fore runner of part 298 of the CAB's Economic Regulations. 

Impose Greater Control 

This regulation continued the nonscheduled carriers' exemption from the requirement of obtaining a certificate, but it subjected the carriers to a greater measure of Federal control. Each carrier was required to apply for and obtain a letter of registration from the CAB, and, so far as the larger carriers were concerned, to file tariffs and adhere to various other requirements.

The CAB also divided the nonscheduled carriers into two groups for regulatory purposes depending on size. Carriers utilizing small aircraft were defined as those which did not use in their transportation services aircraft units having a gross takeoff weight of 10,000 pounds. Subsequently the weight requirement was increased to 12,500 pounds. (Amendment No. 1 to pt. 291 of the Board's Economic Regulations, adopted Oct. 5, 1949 (ER-151).)

Aside from obtaining a letter of registration and reporting annually the nature and scope of their air transport operations, the smaller carriers were relatively free of economic regulation.

Significantly, however, the Board required both large and small carriers to operate between any two points on an irregular basis. 

New Class of Carrier 

In 1952, after considering the small carriers' annual reports and other factors, the Board enacted Economic Regulations, part 298, which created 116

a new class of air carriers known as air taxi operators. Companies formerly identified as small irregular carriers were brought under the new regulation, part 298 which:

Continued the exemption from the requirement in the Federal Aviation 

Act of obtaining a certificate of public convenience and necessity; and 

Removed, except in certain limited situations, the restriction which had prohibited small irregular carriers from operating scheduled service within the continental United States. The Board had recognized that air taxi services were not competitive with those offered by the scheduled airlines. 

Part 298 also changed the name of the class of carrier to one calculated to create a public image more closely reflecting the nature of the services performed.

It removed the reporting requirement from all but those companies operating aircraft having more than five passenger seats, and even that requirement was terminated the following year. (Amendment No. 3 to pt. 

242 of the Board's Economic Regulations, effective Nov. 12, 1953.) The requirement to obtain a letter of registration also was deleted. 

The new regulation prohibited operations between points served by a certificated helicopter carrier and maintained restrictions on operations in territories and possessions of the United States. 

Liberal Approach 

In sum, the regulation represented a rather liberal approach to Federal regulation of the air taxi industry.

With the enactment of part 298, cooperative arrangements between air taxis and the scheduled airlines definitely took an upward turn. Standard interline traffic agreements were negotiated, paving the way for the more sophisticated arrangements that were to follow for the handling of interline traffic.

The year 1965 was a significant year for air taxi operators—the Board adopted a number of important amendments to part 298. (Amendments 

Nos. 4, 5, 6, and 7 to pt. 298.)

The effectiveness of the regulation was extended for an indefinite period, rather than, as before, for a specific period of time.

Blanket Authority 

Air taxis were given blanket authority for 3 years to carry mail, under prescribed conditions, in the 48 contiguous States and the State of Hawaii. 

Restrictions were removed on the operation of regular services within territories and possessions of the United States. 

The prohibition against regular air taxi service between points served daily by a certificated route carrier with small aircraft was eliminated. 

Finally, the Board permitted greater freedom of operation within the 

Hawaiian Islands. 

The 1965 amendment to part 298 permitting air taxis to carry mail specified that the authority would not apply in any markets which are authorized on-line service by a single certificated route carrier. Also the service could be performed only after a final mail rate had been established. 117

New Need for Service 

Subsequently, the discontinuance of many passenger trains with post office cars dictated a need for mail service between various points on the routes of certificated air carriers. To meet this need in certain areas the 

Board exempted air taxi operators from the part 298 restrictions in order to permit them to carry mail between the certificated points. Generally, this has been done in areas where no service was being operated or where available airline schedules were not responsive to the needs of the postal service, and where there was no apparent adverse effect on certificated carriers.

More recently, the demands of the mail service have indicated the need for even greater flexibility. Accordingly, on October 17, 1967, the CAB amended part 298 again to extend from December 31, 1968, to June 30, 1969, the air taxi operators' authority to carry mail, and to permit air taxi mail service to begin under a temporary, rather than a final mail rate. Also, under certain circumstances, provision was made for the carriage of mail by air taxis in markets authorized to be served by certificated route air carriers. (Amendment No. 9 to pt. 298.)

Provide Safeguards 

Through these amendments the Board has established administrative machinery which will permit, where appropriate, the institution of mail service by air taxis much more quickly than before. This is true both with respect to noncompetitive and competitive markets. So far as the competitive markets are concerned, however, safeguards have been provided which will insure that the interests of certificated route carriers are given proper consideration.

Improved service to the public through close Government-industry cooperation is the hoped for result.

So far as piston aircraft are concerned, the CAB has authorized the use of large equipment where justified by unusual demands, such as the use of a specially equipped DC-3 to carry certain types of cargo, or the transportation of passengers in limited geographical areas. 

The Board is allowing air taxis to experiment and expand, but not beyond the point where there may be a significant adverse impact on scheduled certificated carriers, particularly those carriers which are subsidized. 

Substitute Service 

The problem of balancing various interests was certainly evident in the Board's approval on October 13, 1967 (E-25834) of an arrangement between Allegheny Airlines, Inc., a subsidized local service carrier, and Henson 

Aviation, Inc., an air taxi operator located in Hagerstown, Md. Under a 10-year contract between the companies, Henson is to provide service in lieu of Allegheny between Baltimore and both Hagerstown and Salisbury, Md. Henson will be permitted to use Allegheny's symbols, designs, and colors on its aircraft. Allegheny will furnish Henson with 24-hour reservation services and certain ground handling and support services at Friendship Airport. 118

Essentially, the agreement contemplates a situation in which Allegheny will employ an independent contractor to discharge its certificate obligations with small aircraft, rather than employing small aircraft in its own operations.

It is the CAB's belief that the arrangement will reduce Allegheny's subsidy need, provide Henson with an operating profit for fiscal 1969, and improve service to the traveling public.

Earlier on July 17, 1967 (E-25421) the Board authorized Apache Airlines to provide substitute service for American Airlines, a trunkline carrier, at Douglas, Ariz., and on August 21, 1967 (E-25564) permitted West Coast, a local service carrier, to terminate service at Port Angeles, Wash., in favor of an air taxi operation.

Number Increases 

In these and other actions the CAB has recognized that Federal regulation to be effective must be flexible. Regulation must change with the times. Certainly, the profile of the air taxi industry is not the same today as it was when part 298 was first adopted in 1952. The number of operators has increased from 2,000 to about 3,500, and there are many more scheduled taxi operations today than there were 16 years ago. 

The changing times also find several air taxis engaged in operations with large aircraft—that is, equipment having a gross takeoff weight in excess of 12,500 pounds. A number of applications for special exemptions permitting the use of such equipment have been granted by the Board. 

These operations have been treated somewhat differently depending on whether the large equipment is piston or jet. Although the Board's actions have been tailored to fit the nature of the equipment, the underlying objective of each decision is the same.

The first exemption permitting the use by air taxis of jet equipment was granted October 27, 1966, to Reading Aviation Service, Inc. In this decision (order E-24336), the CAB noted that the proposed service would afford small charter groups the advantage of the greater speed and convenience of jet travel, and service to points which are not on the route of any certificated air carrier. The CAB had concluded that the proposed operation would not divert traffic from any air carrier. 

That decision and those which followed restricted the operations of the jet aircraft by air taxi operators to planeload charters and limited the exemptions granted to 3 years. 318-932 - 689 119

SUPPLEMENTALS ARE CHARTER EXPERTS 

It is difficult to reconstruct a sharp picture of the nonscheduled air carrier industry in the late 1930's. There were relatively few carriers. The operation of sightseeing flights was a common activity. Many of those in business were engaged in flight school operations, aircraft maintenance and/or sales. The nonscheduled air services offered were performed usually in conjunction with one or more of these other activities. 

With the end of World War II, various developments occurred-surplus aircraft became available at low prices; thousands of men trained in military aviation returned to civilian life; the demand for air service, which had been contained during the war, grew tremendously. 

These and other factors led many persons with small capital investments to enter the air transportation field as nonscheduled carriers to accommodate passengers not being served by the certificated carriers. 

Fresh Look at Activities 

As a result the CAB decided that it had better take a fresh look at the activities of nonscheduled carriers of all sizes.

After an investigation,' the CAB concluded that additional regulation of the nonscheduled carriers was necessary in the public interest. On May 5, 1947, the Board adopted a new regulation which created a classification of carriers known as Irregular Air Carriers.² 

The Board took away the blanket exemption these carriers had and required them to not only obtain letters of registration but to file tariffs. 

These carriers were not limited to charter service. They could select the points they wished to serve and could provide individually ticketed and individually waybilled transportation. The authority could be obtained simply by filing a brief application and receiving a letter of registration. By 1948 a total of 162 letters of registration were issued. In August of that year the 

Board froze the issuance of further letters and began an investigation into the activities of these carriers.

The biggest problem faced was that some of the carriers were not restricting themselves to irregular flights. Rather, individually or in combination with other irregulars, certain carriers began to conduct regularly scheduled individually ticketed services hardly distinguishable from those of the scheduled airlines. 2 

¹ Investigation of Nonscheduled Air Services, 6 CAB 1049, May 17, 1946.

Regulations serial number 388 (revison of sec. 292.1 of the Board's Economic 

Regulations). 120

In general, they offered lower rates to the public. On the other hand, it could be argued (and was, repeatedly by the scheduled carriers) that such low rates were possible only because the supplemental carriers selected only the "cream" routes to serve― -those between major cities—and because the carriers were under no obligation to serve lesser points, or to fly on days of the week when there was no heavy traffic. 

Regulations Tightened

In a series of steps taken in 1949 and the early 1950's, the Board tightened the regulation of the large irregulars. Each carrier had to obtain an individual exemption order. Efforts to terminate the operations of flagrant violators of the "irregularity" rule led, however, to lengthy procedures in the courts. 

In trying to regulate this industry, the Board was handicapped because the underlying statute—the Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938—was not particularly designed for supplemental air carriers. It was based, rather, on the need to regulate a system of point-to-point routes. 

In the fall of 1951, the Board started the Large Irregular Case to license these carriers selectively. In 1955, it changed their designation from "large irregular" to "supplemental air carrier." It outlined a temporary pattern of authority which would leave planeload charter authority unrestricted but which limited the number of flights transporting individually-ticketed passengers or individually-waybilled freight. 

Limit on Flights

Instead of attempting to use further the concept of "irregular" or "nonscheduled" service, it allowed scheduled service, but with a numerical limit of 10 flights a month between any pair of points.

Attempts to define “irregular" and "nonscheduled" always had been difficult, and the terms lent themselves to evasions. The 10-flight provision was clear and more readily enforceable.

By permitting this limited degree of individual service, the CAB recognized that the planeload charter market, both civil and military, was inadequate to keep all of these carriers in business. The carriers were not recipients of subsidy, and have never been in their history. 

The 1955 action, and several subsequent Board actions seeking to establish a certificated supplemental industry, were extensively litigated in the courts which were called on to determine the power of the CAB to grant nonroute certificates.

The courts finally held that the Board could not issue the certificates. The legal basis for the industry thus continued to lack the stability which was all the more necessary as the advent of jet aircraft made it incumbent on the supplementals to invest heavily in equipment to keep up competitively with the scheduled airlines. 

New Legislation

The Congress, in turn, became interested in considering a sound legal basis for a qualified supplemental industry, particularly in light of the court's 121

decision against certificates and three fatal accidents in 1960 and 1961. In the summer of 1962 legislation was enacted (Public Law 87-528). In essence, this legislation confined the role of the supplementals to charter flights. A 

2-year period was given to phase out existing individually-ticketed services. 

The Board was given 90 days to conduct an expedited review of the qualifications of the 30 supplemental carriers then holding authority. Of these 30, only 15 successfully passed the test and were given interim licenses until hearings could be held on their requests for permanent certification. 

The omnibus case which processed the latter applications, as authorized by the 1962 legislation, has been substantially completed, including the review of the qualifications of all the carriers. Of the 15 carriers who had received interim authority in the fall of 1962, one had had its interim authority revoked because of lack of continuing fitness because of its financial condition. Another two had merged. This left 13 with interim authority: all received permanent domestic certificates. 

Today these are the supplemental carriers. The class is open to new entrants, but none has so far been certificated. 

International authority was issued to each carrier for certain areas. Six have transatlantic certificates, three transpacific, seven Caribbean, two to South America, five to Mexico, seven to Canada. This authority is for 5-year duration. 

Fitness Requirements 

The 1962 legislation was novel in that it gave the Board authority to require that the supplemental carriers constantly maintain standards of fitness. For example, the financial condition of each carrier is regularly reviewed.

The legislation also required each carrier to maintain liability insurance. 

Since 1962, the supplementals have made significant progress. In 1967, they realized revenues of $258,631,000, a 150 percent increase over 1962. 

Their civil charter revenues were $97,486,000, about 38 percent of total revenue. The remainder involved military operations. The 1967 civil charter revenues were almost 10 times those of 1962, showing a decreased relative dependence on military contracts, despite the current heavy military requirement.

Large modern jet aircraft characterize the fleets of most of the 13 carriers, and extensive new equipment purchases are on order. 122

WAR STIMULATED ALASKA'S AIR GROWTH 

The rugged terrain, the long distances and the lack of any other adequate transportation in Alaska created an ideal environment for the exploitation of commercial air transportation. By the middle of the 1920's the first air transportation was being conducted with primitive aircraft. 

From the commencement of the earliest operations until the passage of the Civil Aeronautics Act in 1938, a situation of open competition existed in which numerous carriers operated-many in precarious financial condition. 

Nevertheless, the growth of the industry was vigorous as these figures show. 

Period: 

Two years ended Mar. 31, 1929. 

Calendar year 1936...

Calendar year 1940.. 

Passengers

Freight 

(tons) 2,000 47.5 

13, 300 750.0 

32, 000 

2, 350.0

While a number of passengers were carried, the principal use made of air transportation was for the carriage of freight and mail. 

The first substantial use of aircraft for mail purposes began in 1934 when a number of 4-year star route contracts were bid in by aircraft operators. The contracts did not require the use of aircraft but air service was permitted by the Post Office.

Reliable Transportation 

By 1938 the Post Office was requesting bids for 62 star routes covering approximately 6,000 route miles of service, and the airplane had completely replaced the riverboat and dog sled as the most reliable form of transportation in the then Territory. 

Alaska was still very small in terms of population and commerce in 1940. 

The population of the Territory was 72,500, and the population of Anchorage, presently the largest city, was only 3,495. Other than fishing and mining, Alaska had no local industry and was completely dependent on the continental United States for practically all the needs of subsistence. All traffic between Alaska and the mainland portion of the United States was carried by steamship. However, the crisis created by World War II was to stimulate intensively the use of air transportation both in Alaska and to and from continental U.S. points. 123

Awards for 23 Carriers 

The chaotic financial condition of the Alaskan carriers, the deepening national defense crisis, and the requirement of the Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938 to regulate, prompted an early review of the Alaskan route situation by the CAB, and a proceeding called the Alaskan Air Transportation Investigation was instituted in 1939. The proceeding opened with 38 applications for air service, most for "grandfather" certificates, and ended in 

1942 with the award of certificates to 23 carriers.

While certificates and subsidy eligibility were granted to these 23 carriers, the full scope of the Board's regulatory powers was not brought into play and regulation was confined to the minimum consistent with the provisions of the act.

Services authorized included regular route service and irregular route service in the regular route service areas. Charter and other special services were authorized on a casual or infrequent basis. Reporting requirements also were kept to a minimum. 

Subsequent to the issuance of the initial authorizations, the routes of the Alaskan air carriers were modified from time to time to authorize additional regular route service to meet changing demands. With the passage of time the irregular route authority fell into disuse and eventually its use by some carriers ceased entirely. At the time of the Alaska Air Transportation case, the carriers involved operated about 100 aircraft of which only about eight were multiengined, the balance being single-engine aircraft of from four- to seven-passenger capacity, some of ancient vintage. 

New Review Necessary 

Within 10 years, circumstances had so changed in Alaska that another comprehensive investigation of air transportation matters in the Territory was required and, accordingly, the Board instituted the Intra-Alaska case. 

In the years between 1942 and 1953, the number of certificated carriers in Alaska had been reduced drastically by mergers, acquisitions, and business failures. In addition, the character of the operations had changed as better airports and newer, more suitable aircraft became available. 

Stimulated by the "cold war," national defense activity in the Territory had continued at a high level throughout most of this period, contributing substantially to the growth of the Territory's population and its prosperity, but creating an abnormal demand for transportation services. 

Federal subsidy for the nine operating intra-Alaska carriers in 1953 was approximately $5 million. They generated 177,000 passengers and 3,198,000 ton-miles of mail, express, and freight. 

Overhaul Route Pattern 

The principal decision in the Intra-Alaska Case was not reached until 1958 and the result was a substantial overhaul of the Alaska route structure. 

Routes were transferred or realined, services were suspended or deleted, and new services were authorized. In addition, a significant revision was made in liberalizing the Alaska pilot-owner regulation which covered the numerous noncertificated operations of small aircraft. At the end of the 124

proceeding the number of federally certificated carriers operating exclusively in Alaska had decreased to eight.

Today there are four federally certificated Alaskan air carriers. The carriers have introduced the most modern jet and turboprop equipment on the interior trunk routes and on the States-Alaska services. At the same time, however, a substantial part of Alaskan air service continues to be provided with small-capacity, single-engine aircraft, and up to one-half of the current $5 million intra-Alaskan subsidy requirement may be needed to support these services. The sustained growth of air transportation in Alaska is highlighted by the fact that 300 points were served in 1967, and 565,000 passengers and 35,618,000 ton-miles of freight, mail, and express were carried. 

With statehood in 1959, Alaska established the machinery for State regulation of various phases of air transportation. Since the flight operations of the federally certificated Alaskan carriers are almost wholly intrastate, the Federal role in Alaska may undergo significant changes in the future. 125

INTERNATIONAL AVIATION Industry Is Sensitive Thread in U.S. National Fabric 

"Early in June 1944, the chairman of the Civil Aeronautics Board, Mr. 

L. Welch Pogue, stood before a large world map and pointed to 140,000 miles of international air routes which had been selected as desirable for postwar operation by U.S. air carriers. On this map, in the simple terms of geography and stripped of all doctrinaire rhetoric or diplomatic phrases, was the basic draft of what the United States proposed to do in the field of international commercial aviation after the war. To the already existing 

80,000 route miles had been added an additional 60,000 miles of air lines extending virtually to every important country in the world." (Burnet Her shey, Skyways of Tomorrow, Headline Series, No. 57, Foreign Policy Asso ciation, Inc., New York, N.Y., August 1944, p. 1.)

The international scheduled air carrier industry of the United States is today a vital and indispensable segment of the overall national transportation system. The 21 combination and all-cargo carriers which make up this industry, including intercontinental, regional, and trans-border operators, provide regular and dependable air service to 167 points in 119 political entities (countries, their separate territories, and U.S. territories) located in every part of the world. Almost every population, industrial, and political center in the world is represented. 

The mobility and accessibility made possible by this industry and its carrier members has no parallel in the history of transportation. It also is an industry which has grown very rapidly. 

In calendar year 1967, U.S. international air carriers carried 6.7 million passengers between the United States and foreign countries. This figure does not include passengers carried who started and ended their trip on routes operated by U.S. carriers outside the United States. Including foreign carriers, a total of 13.9 million passengers arrived in and departed from the 

United States by air or 90 percent of all international arrivals and departures by air and sea combined via commercial transport means. Water transport accounted for only 1.4 million or 10 percent of the total. In fiscal year 1947, the number of passengers arriving in and departing from the United States by United States and foreign air carriers totaled less than 900,000 with sea adding 650,000. During the entire period of 1931-40 (fiscal years ending 

June 30), international passenger arrivals and departures at U.S. gateway points totaled 372,071 by air and 9.8 million by sea. 126

Continuous Upward Trend 

Over the years the trend of passenger traffic has been upward with no abatement in sight.

The contemporary role played by the U.S. international air carriers can thus be measured in terms of millions of passengers carried each year. They include military and civil Government travelers representing every part of the U.S. merchandising and manufacturing industries. They include the constantly growing number of pleasure travelers * * * teachers, students, retirees, and people from every walk of life. And they include not only people working and living in the United States, but people of other lands who use our carriers. The transport capability provided facilitates the attainment of different objectives, but for all it represents a ready means to go anywhere in the world with speed, ease, comfort, and economy.

The importance of the international air transport industry of the United States also can be evaluated in terms of political, military, economic, and social goals and benefits for the Nation at large. Politically, it provides lines of communications with Europe, Asia, the Pacific, South America, Africa, and elsewhere which are vital to our need to keep in touch with our friends around the world. Militarily, the industry provides aircraft, crews, and knowhow which already serve the military establishment on a civilian relationship basis, but which also can be quickly pressed into direct military service. 

Economically, it provides jobs for thousands directly and indirectly at home and abroad on the basis of its own diverse needs, the stimulation of tourism and trade, and promotion of U.S. aeronautical products. Socially, it has made it possible for millions of people through low-cost, convenient transportation to visit other lands and to develop contact between peoples. The industry is, by any measure, a key and sensitive thread in the national fabric. 

Freight Expands 

The role of the U.S. international air carriers in freight movement also has been one of steadily increasing national importance. The revenue freight tonmiles performed by our carriers in international scheduled air services has risen from 30 million ton-miles in 1947 to over 700 million ton-miles in 1967, or an increase of 23 times. The variety of freight carried by our international air carriers is infinite, including goods of every description. Mail carried by 

U.S. international air carriers has expanded, for its part, from a level of 19 million ton-miles to over 380 million ton-miles in the same period. 

Advancements in technology, in which the aircraft has played the central role, have figured prominently in the growth of the international air transport industry of the United States and of the world. From the slow-moving, short-range, limited capacity aircraft utilized before World War II, the international air transport industry has moved through a succession of aircraft types since the end of the War. Each type the Douglas series (DC-4, DC-6, DC-7), Boeing Stratocruiser 307 and 377, Lockheed Constellations, and others have provided greater capability and reliability than its predecessor model or the then available aircraft on the market. The military aeronautical developments which took place in World War II, and as a 127

consequence of the war, unquestionably gave a big boost to civilian aero nautical developments. 

Industry Revolutionized 

In recent years, we have seen the advent of the jet transport aircraft such as represented by the Douglas DC-8 and the Boeing 707. Both aircraft are used extensively on overseas routes by United States and foreign air carries. Compared to the Martin and Vought-Sikorsky flying boats used by Pan

American across the Pacific in 1938, the DC-8 and Boeing 707 provide approximately five times the passenger lift capacity, four times the speed capability, and three times the range. These two aircraft and jet transport aircraft generally have virtually revolutionized the international air transportation picture. An international trip that once took days to make by air now takes hours, and at a much cheaper price. 

In June 1938, the cost of an air ticket between San Francisco and Manila was $950 one way and $1,710 round trip. Only one class of service was available. With stops and overnight layovers, the trip took a week. Today, the air fare on the basis of the all-year economy jet ticket, is $460 one way and $874 round trip. The trip now takes 12 or so hours. The significance of the fare difference can also be examined in terms of the change since 1938 in the purchasing power of the dollar. The heart of the new aircraft has been the turbine engine with its remarkable simplicity and great power and reliability. 

Currently we are on the threshold of such aircraft as the Boeing 747 which is designed to carry up to 500 persons, the DC-10's, the Lockheed 500 Galaxy-commercial version of the Lockheed/USAF C-5A aircraft and the world's largest aircraft-which will provide three times the cargo-carrying capability of present-day transport jets, and the supersonic transports which will be able to span oceans in just a few hours.

More Than Technology 

But technology is only part of the story in explaining the growth of the international air transport industry of the Nation in the past 30 years and in the postwar period.

The other part of the story deals with the multilateral and bilateral legal relations established between the United States and other countries in the field of civil aviation. Regarding the former, they comprise: (a) the Interna tional Air Services Transit Agreement concluded at Chicago on December 7, 1944, and entered into force for the United States on February 8, 1945, subject to a reservation; (b) the Convention on International Civil Aviation concluded at Chicago on December 7, 1944, and entered into force for the United States on April 4, 1947, and subsequent Protocols relating to the Convention; and (c) three other multilateral agreements pertaining to civil aviation concluded in the postwar period. 

They are: (a) Convention on the International Recognition of Rights in 

Aircraft, done at Geneva on June 19, 1948, and entered into force for the United States on September 17, 1953; (b) Agreement on the Joint Financ ing of Certain Air Navigation Services in Iceland, done at Geneva on Sep tember 25, 1956, and entered into force for the United States on June 6, 128

1958, and Amendment;

and (c) Agreement on the Joint Financing of Cer tain Air Navigation Services in Greenland and the Faroe Islands, done at 

Geneva on September 25, 1956, and entered into force for the United States on June 6, 1958, and Amendments. Reference: U.S. Department of State, Treaties in Force, A List of Treaties and Other International Agreements of the United States in Force on January 1, 1968, Department of State Publica tion 8355, U.S. Government Printing Office, Washington, D.C. 20402, pp.

254-257. 

U.S. Participation 

The United States is currently a party to all five multilateral agreements. 

The first or "transit agreement" provides that each contracting State shall have, with respect to scheduled international air services, the privilege of flying across the territory of other contracting States without landing and the privilege of landing for nontraffic purposes. The second or "Chicago Convention" sets out the principles, rules, procedures, and practices governing the international air navigation of civil aircraft, as defined. It further provides for the establishment of the International Civil Aviation Organization (ICAO) which came into being on April 4, 1947, succeeding a provisional organization. The headquarters of ICAO are located in Montreal, Canada. 

As of January 1, 1968, a total of 72 nations were a party to the transit agreement and 115 to the Chicago Convention. 

Without the international understanding and cooperation represented by the several multilateral agreements concluded by the nations of the world in support of international air transportation, it is doubtful that the industry could have reached its present state of development. It would have been very difficult, if not impossible, for the airlines of the world to conduct their operations across oceans and borders on a day-in and day-out basis without standard navigational rules and common navigational aids. The cooperation demonstrated in the field of international air transportation is a source of great encouragement in contemplating generally the subject of cooperation among nations.

Concerning bilateral civil aviation relations, the United States is currently a party to 51 air transport agreements with other countries. 

Exchange Air Rights 

These agreements act to, or serve as the basis to, exchange commercial air rights-importantly and fundamentally routes-with other nations. They also serve to provide a legal framework and formal system for managing our commercial air relations with other countries. In the conclusion of the bilateral air transport agreements, as in the case of the multilateral agreements, the CAB has taken an active part in the negotiations conducted with other countries by the Department of State, both as advisor and participant. 

All of the bilateral air transport agreements in force today were concluded during and since the end of World War II. The agreement between Spain and the United States, signed on December 2, 1944, at Madrid, and effective on that date, has the distinction of being the oldest United States bilateral air transport agreement in force. 

Prior to the agreement with Spain, the United States had concluded a total of three bilateral air transport agreements-with Colombia in 1929 and 129
