soîteonove A livo

with Canada and France in 1939. All three agreements have since been superseded. Several other agreements also have been superseded by later agreements. The latest bilateral air transport agreement concluded by the United 

States was with Indonesia; it was signed on January 11, 1968 at Djakarta, and came into force on that date.

Agreements Terminated 

In the course of civil aviation relations with other countries, two countries have chosen to terminate their air transport agreements with the United States. The two countries are the Philippines and Italy. Agreements with those countries, the Philippines and Italy, ended in 1960 and in 1967, respectively, pursuant to their termination provisions. 

Where we do not have a bilateral air transport agreement, as in the case of the Philippines and Italy, airline operations between the United States and the other country are conducted on the basis of a unilateral grant of authority for such operation by one or both countries, as the case requires. 

U.S. airline operations to Central America and Africa, are presently conducted for the most part without benefit of bilateral air transport agreements. 

Most of the U.S. bilateral air transport agreements in force today embody, either as originally concluded or as amended, the so-called "Bermuda principles." These principles take their name from the bilateral air transport agreement concluded between the United States and the United Kingdom on February 11, 1946, at Bermuda. This agreement is, as amended, in force today. 

The Bermuda principles provide essentially standards and procedures for handling capacity and rate questions. In agreements with these principles— 

Bermuda type agreements—the designated airlines of each country are free to operate the level of services they believe is commercially justified consistent with the general rule that capacity should conform to demand and be keyed principally to home country movements. If, after a measure of capacity experience is obtained, either country to the agreement believes that the capacity offered by the airline or airlines of the other country is excessive, it may under the terms of the agreement request a bilateral examination of the capacity offered. In the matter of rate control, provision is made for governmental action in cases of extreme unacceptability of proposed rates or fares. 

IATA's Role Accepted 

The United States has accepted, as a practical matter, the International Air Transport Association (IATA) as a vehicle to deal with rates and fares, but with the provision noted. The Standard Form Bilateral-Chicago type agreement-produced by the Chicago Conference, which drafted the Transit Agreement and the Chicago Convention, was silent on the subject of capacity and rates. It has become the model for the great majority of bilateral air transport agreements in force today over the world. 

Not all countries have subscribed to our economic views of freedom in the market place of international air transportation. In our relations with some countries, with and without benefit of a bilateral air transport agreement, we are, and have been, confronted with national policies of protectionism and 131

restrictionism. They have usually taken the form of limits on capacity to protect the homeland carrier.

The United States signed with other nations the International Air Transport Agreement at Chicago on December 7, 1944. It provided for a multilateral exchange of commercial air rights and set out rules for administering the exchange. The agreement never got off the ground, however. A difference of views prevailed among nations regarding the feasibility and desirability of exchanging commercial air rights on a multilateral basis, and the United States and several other nations withdrew from the agreement. 

The nations of the world have fared well in exchanging commercial air rights with each other on a bilateral basis. Every negotiation between the 

United States and another country has required a recognition of its rights as well as our own. We have signed bilateral air transport agreements with most of the important countries of the world. Where the Bermuda principles have been fully implemented and given an opportunity to work, the growth in traffic, as exemplified by the United States-Europe air passenger market, has been spectacular.

Looking back over the past 30 years, the achievements of the international air transport industry have been amazing, and the future for the industry promises to be just as dynamic. 132

CAB PERFECTS SAFETY TECHNIQUES 

The CAB's Bureau of Safety functioned without interruption from 1940 to April 1, 1967, when it was transferred in its entirety to the newly created National Transportation Safety Board. During its tenure under the CAB the 

Bureau was responsible for the investigation and prevention of civil aviation accidents and served as technical advisor to the CAB on determining the cause of such accidents.

From a beginning with about 30 employees in 1940 the Bureau of Safety increased to about 180 by the time of its transfer in 1967. In the beginning its staff consisted largely of pilots but gradually changed, especially after World War II, to include many aeronautical technicians specializing in many diverse fields, including aerodynamics, metalurgy, meteorology, electronics, human factors, and air traffic control. 

Through the years the Bureau of Safety, starting in the DC-3 era, had to originate its own techniques and procedures in the developing science of aircraft accident investigation. As it perfected its procedures it began to use other support facilities of the Federal Government to assist it. Among them: the wind-tunnels of the National Aeronautics and Space Administration; the laboratories of the Bureau of Standards; the fingerprint facilities of the Federal Bureau of Investigation; the military services, especially the Army, for ground search, wreckage security, and helicopter support.

Although the route and rate functions of the CAB were probably better known within U.S. financial circles, the technical work of the CAB Bureau of Safety was gradually attaining worldwide notice. By the mid-fifties more than 51 nations of the world had sent delegations to Washington to observe the Bureau's techniques in aircraft accident investigation and cause determination. The Bureau's record was impressive: from 1940 to 1967 it had been able to find the cause of more than 93 percent of all air carrier accidents it investigated and was able to initiate corrective action in about half of these cases.

Throughout a life span of some 27 years, the CAB Bureau of Safety contributed immeasurably to making possible the world safety record achieved by the U.S. air carrier industry and the U.S. general aviation industry. 133

CITIES BENEFIT FROM SUBSIDY 

The CAB gets its authority for the payment of subsidy to air carriers from Section 406 of the Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938. (The section was later embodied in the Federal Aviation Act of 1958.) 

The section provides that the Civil Aeronautics Board (earlier the Civil Aeronautics Authority) shall fix and determine fair and reasonable rates of compensation for the transportation of mail by aircraft, for the facilities used, and for the services connected therewith by each holder of a certificate authorizing the transportation of mail by aircraft. 

The statute also provides that the compensation shall be sufficient to insure performance of the service and, together with all other revenue of the air carrier, to enable such air carrier under honest, economical, and efficient management, to maintain and continue the development of air transportation to the extent and of the character and quality required for the commerce of the United States, the postal service and the national defense.

Rates Fixed by CAB 

Under the 1938 Act, the Postmaster General paid the air carriers the total compensation due them for the carriage of mail based on rates fixed by the CAB. 

On October 1, 1953 the compensation for the carriage of mail was separated into two elements—(1) payment by the Post Office for the carriage of mail as a service, and (2) payment by the CAB of the difference between the service compensation and the total operating requirement for the air carrier (including a fair return on investment), which is subsidy. 

Subsidy for all periods prior to October 1, 1953, was based on an administrative allocation of the total compensation paid by the Postmaster General since no formal distinction between service mail pay and subsidy was made until that date.

The subsidy payment provisions of the Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938 

were not changed when the Federal Aviation Act of 1958 became effective and still are in effect. 

Achieves Policy Goals

Subsidy for the various air carriers over the past 30 years has assisted in achieving national policy objectives set out by the Congress. Subsidy support for the carriers has paid off in time of war for it has assured a reservoir of trained pilots, mechanics, and other airline personnel, as well as a ready fleet of modern aircraft. 134

Although the carriers receive the subsidy, it is, in effect, the communities that are the direct beneficiaries through the economic growth and development which is fostered by the subsidized transportation services. 

The soundness of congressional subsidy policy as expressed in the act is clearly evidenced by the growth of the airline industry and by the economic impact on the hundreds of communities receiving air service. 

During the first year of airline subsidies-fiscal 1939-22 U.S. airlines were receiving subsidy. Seventy percent of the subsidy dollars was paid to 

16 domestic trunk carriers and 29 percent to four international carriers. 

The other 1 percent was paid to one carrier in Hawaii and one in Alaska. 

During the 30 years to date the number of subsidized carriers has fluctuated. 

Fifty-six different carriers received subsidy in 1951. Today, subsidy is being paid to nine local service carriers (who account for 90 percent of the total amount) and four Alaskan carriers. No domestic trunkline, international, helicopter or Hawaiian carrier is receiving subsidy.

Steady Downward Trend 

The 70 percent of the subsidy payments to the 16 domestic trunk carriers in 1939 amounted to approximately $12 million. During World War 

II, the contribution of the domestic air carriers included the assignment of a major portion of the domestic fleet to the military, the operation of large scale military transport contract services, the carriage of high priority traffic on remaining commercial services, and the furnishing of a reservoir of highly trained transport personnel. 

As a result, during the 5 years 1943-47 the subsidy paid the domestic trunks was reduced substantially. However, in 1948 the domestic trunk carriers received more than double the subsidy of the 1939 level. It was not until the 1951-53 period that most of the carriers became able to operate without subsidy. By 1957 only one trunk carrier required subsidy support. 

In 1939 the international carriers received $5 million in subsidy or 29 percent of the total for that year. During World War II these carriers contributed to the war effort in the same manner as the domestic trunks. 

Subsidy for international carriers reached $10 million in 1941, declining substantially thereafter through 1945. However, beginning in 1946 the increasing impact of foreign competition required the U.S. international carriers to reequip with the latest technologically improved aircraft and to substantially expand services. As a result, subsidy increased steadily to a peak of $37.5 million in 1950. In subsequent years, continued growth and economic prosperity resulted in a sharp tapering off in the industry's subsidy need and, since 1958, all U.S. international carriers have been subsidy free.

Subsidy for Helicopters 

Commercial helicopter service was first instituted in Los Angeles on October 1, 1947, with the carriage of mail only. Until 1954, all compensation to the helicopter carriers was designated as "service mail pay” and was paid by the Postmaster General. With the introduction of passenger services in fiscal year 1954, service pay and subsidy payments were separated. 318-932 - 68 - 10 135

Commercial helicopter service was subsidized as an experimental effort. 

Although helicopters were costly to operate, it was hoped that experience gained in their operation, coupled with technological advance, would lead to production of economical rotor-winged aircraft within a reasonable period of time. However, in 1966 Congress determined that this prospect was not sufficiently imminent to justify continued subsidy support and it was discontinued. Since that time, two of the three helicopter carriers which had been subsidized have continued to operate scheduled services with financial support from several of the major carriers. 

Insures Alaskan Service 

This area exemplifies the role of subsidy in the national interest. Air service is essentially the only means of transportation within, and to and from, the State of Alaska. In recent years, the tremendous growth in the volume of passengers, mail and freight has made it possible for several carriers to operate without subsidy aid and for others to operate at reduced subsidy levels. However, for a considerable period in the past the low traffic density would not permit the operation of regular airline service except at substantial losses. Accordingly, subsidy has been provided to insure air transportation which is essential to commerce and the Postal Service and as a direct contribution to the national defense.

Subsidy payments for Alaskan services reached a peak of approximately $9.7 million in 1963, but have since declined to $6 million in 1968. Four carriers providing Alaskan services are subsidized. 

Hawaiian Service 

For approximately the past 20 years two certificated regularly scheduled airlines have served the Hawaiian Islands. Before then only one carrier provided intraisland services. While no subsidy was required during the years 1943-50, for the most part subsidy assistance was required in subsequent years. In peak year, fiscal 1966, $1.1 million was paid. 

With the growth in tourism stimulated by reduced fares, increased jet services between the mainland and Hawaii, and vigorous traffic promotion, the financial health of the two Hawaiian carriers improved to the point where, effective January 1, 1967, they no longer required subsidy.

Experimental Service 

In August 1943, the CAB initiated a public investigation to determine whether scheduled air services could feasibly be extended to small and intermediate size communities. By the end of that year, over 400 applications for authority to operate new domestic air services were on file with the Board. After extensive hearings, the Board announced its opinion in 

July 1944, in which a variety of local services were certificated on an experimental and temporary basis. 

In August 1945 the first local carrier initiated operations and in fiscal year 1946 it received $1,081,000 subsidy. By the mid-1950's local operations had expanded to 14 carriers and almost every State in the United States was served on a daily basis. In 1955, the certificates of public convenience 136

and necessity for these carriers were made permanent by legislation in order, among other things, to bring financial stability to the industry. 

Before 1961, subsidy rates were established for each of the carriers on an individual basis. However, since 1961 the carriers have received subsidy payments based on a class rate formula applicable to each carrier as a part of a homogeneous group. 

Continued expansion of services over new route segments and to many new communities resulted in a rapid growth of the local service part of the industry. This growth has been further accelerated in recent years with the introduction and integration of new and larger jet aircraft. As a result of this expansion subsidy payments increased to a peak of $67.5 million in fiscal year 1963. Since that year the trend has been steadily downward. In 

1968 it reached $52 million, the lowest level achieved since 1960. 

Prior to January 1, 1961, subsidy rates had generally been established for individual carriers on a case-by-case basis. By reason of a combination of factors—including particularly the rapidity of change in their developing route systems—the local service carriers were often in a position where their subsidy rate was "open" or undetermined. As a result, the carriers lacked the certainty of a final rate, and incentives for long-range planning and maximum operational efficiency were not created. Finding that the 13 local carriers, despite various differences, represented a homogeneous group which could be encompassed by a single rate, the Board established the first class subsidy rate for all 13 carriers effective January 1, 1961.

The introduction of the class subsidy rate technique was necessarily experimental and the Board was aware that modifications and revisions would be required as it accumulated experience under that rate. Experience dictated improvement. Revised rates were established as of January 1, 1963; 

July 1, 1964; January 1, 1966; January 1, 1967; and July 1, 1968.

After 8 years under class rates for the locals, it is clear that this technique has been successful from the standpoint of the public, the carriers, and the Government. It has, thus far, engendered sound long-range carrier planning, improved and stabilized the carriers' financial status, provided better incentives for continuing improvements in service to the public, and it is meeting the goal of maximum service for each subsidy dollar. It appears that the local service class subsidy rate will be a continuing technique in the 

Board's subsidy processes for the foreseeable future.

Long-Range Subsidy Outlook 

The outlook is good for continued reduction and ultimate elimination of subsidy. The downward trend since 1963 is the result of a combination of factors which, on balance, have resulted in a marked total reduction each year. Assuming continued favorable economic conditions, the integration of additional modern and more efficient aircraft, and capable and forward looking management, coupled with a route improvement program by the Board, dependence on subsidy should continue to decrease substantially. The utilization of more efficient aircraft, along with technological improvements and management efficiencies, should offset the impact of cost increases. 137

AIR FREIGHT GROWTH IS TREMENDOUS 

The enormous growth of air cargo in recent years is common knowledge to everyone even slightly aware of developments in air transportation. Since 

1946, the annual volume of domestic airfreight in scheduled service rose from about 14 million ton-miles to over 1.4 billion, a hundredfold increase. Since 

1957 (when data for international traffic first became available), the total volume of international scheduled airfreight traffic for U.S. international air carriers rose by 640 percent from 130 million to 1.6 billion ton-miles in calendar 1967. 

Airline and other industry press agents and speechmakers who cite this fantastic growth of air cargo, frequently point out, though, that "airfreight accounts for less than 1 percent of the total intercity ton-miles of freight." 

Literally this is true if we include under the term "freight" all of the barge and pipeline traffic, and such obvious rail bread-and-butter items as coal, sand, and gravel. If these were omitted, airfreight's penetration of the logical intercity ton-miles might stand revealed as much greater. On a revenue basis, a similar analysis indicates airfreight's share of the total market as being about 3.5 percent.

Air freight has become a significant source of revenues for the airlines. In the 12-month period ended December 31, 1967, all U.S. certificated route carriers earned revenues from freight amounting to $465 million in scheduled service, and $225 million in charter service. That $690 million total is more than 10 percent of the total transport revenues of these U.S. carriers. 

Commercial air cargo (freight, express, and mail) has become a major source of airline revenue, amounting to over 12 percent of the combined passenger and cargo revenues in 1967. 

Technological Developments 

The tremendous growth in airfreight which these figures indicate reflects some equally radical changes in the methods of transporting airfreight. Airfreight at first was carried almost exclusively in the cargo compartments of passenger aircraft. But by 1944 an airline was experimenting with scheduled

DC-3 all-cargo aircraft with a capacity of 3.5 tons. Other early aircraft used-the C-46 with a capacity of 6.2 tons, and the DC-4 holding 8 tonswere too small to permit really profitable all-cargo aircraft operations.

It was not until after the development of the jets that airfreight offered a promise of profit. The Canadair CL-44 propjet was followed by the allcargo versions of the pure jet DC-8 and B-707, with capacities of approxi- 139

mately 90,000 pounds and direct operating costs per ton-mile significantly below those experienced with the earlier piston-powered aircraft.

However, passenger aircraft cargo compartments still carry about half of domestic freight traffic. The cargo compartment of the B-707 passenger aircraft, for example, holds approximately 18,000 pounds, or more than the lift capacity of a DC-4. These cargo-carrying passenger aircraft serve more points more frequently than the freighter aircraft. However, they do not have many schedules at night, when most freight traffic is available for movement, nor can they accommodate large pieces of freight. Much of the advantage of all-cargo aircraft lies in these areas and in the ever-increasing volume of mail.

All-cargo aircraft also have an advantage in loading and unloading and related terminal and handling time and expense. Until the advent of the all-cargo jet aircraft, loading and unloading was essentially a manual operation, as it still is for the cargo compartments of passenger aircraft. 

Spotlight Inefficiency 

The jets spotlighted the inefficiency of manual operation. A $6to $10million vehicle, capable of earning $5 to $6 a minute while in flight, should not be idled on the ground by a loading and unloading method used for horse-drawn wagons. As a result, many airlines have built new terminals embodying the latest concepts in automated facilities. Pallets and containers, loaded on the ground prior to arrival of the freighter, can be loaded on the aircraft in 20 to 30 minutes. Unloading at destination is just as fast.

One of the most recent and interesting equipment developments has been the QC, or “quick-change” aircraft with passenger seats mounted on removable pallets. Used for daytime and early evening passenger flights, the 6to 9-seat abreast palletized interiors are then removed through the aircraft's large cargo door and replaced with prepalletized freight. In this configuration, the QC is flown as an all-cargo flight during the night and restored to its "passenger" appearance in the morning. The conversions take 20 to 25 minutes each, and substantially increase the aircraft's utilization (hours flown per day), thus reducing unit costs. 

More is to come. On the horizon are the all-cargo versions of the giant 

B-747 and L-500 jumbo-jet aircraft, designed to carry over 230,000 pounds of cargo.

All of these technological developments have resulted in increases in overall operating costs, both as to total dollars as well as on a per-flight basis. 

The high cost of jet aircraft and the investment in terminals and manpower devoted almost exclusively to the rapidly growing freight industry, have accelerated total airline freight costs and investments. However, the unit costs per ton, per ton-mile, per cubic-foot-mile and other indicators are evidence that the airlines are taking this cost rise in stride without a marked rise in prices, and that there is a reasonable chance for selective rate reductions in the future, if they contribute to further reduced unit costs. 

Situation To Improve 

As an example, utilization of cargo terminals is generally poor, with traffic volume peaking at certain hours. Thus, it is not surprising that 140

handling costs per ton are measured in 10-dollar bills, and not dollars and cents as for other modes. This situation will improve, hopefully, through such programs as containerization.

Historically, the airlines have attracted the shipper of package freight, averaging 30 to 40 pounds per package, with shipments averaging about 200 to 300 pounds. Thus, the airlines are handling five to 10 packages per shipment, and as total tonnage grows, so must the terminals and manpower to process it. One airline cargo executive has estimated that 100 million packages will be processed in 1970. To reverse this trend, yet preserve growth, reduce costs, reduce rates, and increase profits, the airlines will probably center on containerization, the essence of which is to put a lot of little boxes into a bigger box, and get someone else to do the work. 

At present, the airlines are experimenting with a five-container program, based on a pallet having a rigid hood shaped to match the interior of their all-cargo aircraft. Some carriers refer to this unit as an igloo, others as a hula-hut but it is identified by all carriers as the type A container, and and it ranges in cube-capacity from about 340 to 600 cubic feet. 

Subordinate to this type A container are its modules: the contoured type B container equal to about half the type A capacity; the contoured type B-2 container equal to half the type B capacity, or about 100 cubic feet; the boxlike type C container of about 127 cubic feet, and the boxlike type D container of about 64 cubic feet. These modules, singly or in combination, will fit efficiently into the aircraft as the type A does. 

Attractive Discounts

All type A pallet-igloo containers are carrier-owned. The others are shipper-owned. In all cases, the carriers grant attractive incentive discounts for the use of these containers. The first of these discounts is an allowance for the weight of the container itself; the second, a rate discount ranging downward from $1 per 100 pounds for loading and unloading the type A container to 35 cents for the type D container; the last, (to particularly encourage the shipper of goods of high density under general commodity rates) is an additional discount of one-third off the rate granted to that portion of the containerized shipment which exceeds 10 pounds per cubic foot.

The program has been successful. In its first 6 months of operation from 

November 1966 through April 1967 over 11 million pounds were transported in 6,500 containers; the second 6 months from May through October 

1967 produced over 28 million pounds, a 138-percent increase, and a movement of over 21,000 containers, a 230-percent increase. When one compares the average density of containerized freight of around 13 pounds per cubic foot with normal freight at about 9 pounds per cubic foot, it is evident that some degree of success also has been attained in this area. 

Significant Progress 

After some uncertain initial steps, the major air carriers have wholeheartedly recognized the efficiencies in containerized traffic and, from the available statistics, seem to be making significant progress. During the 141

second 6-month period after the air carrier container tariffs were approved, container ton-miles accounted for an estimated 3.97 percent of the combined system ton-miles of American, Flying Tiger, Northwest, TWA, and United.

That percentage was almost double the experience in the first 6-months period. The 3.97-percent figure may not sound impressive but it amounted to over 22 million ton-miles and, in the view of some, compared quite favorably with the 4.3-percent piggyback contribution to total railroad carloadings in 1967. The comparison is cited to indicate that the air transport industry is doing well in containerization, too. 

However, with the increases in capacity resulting from more and more all-cargo schedules to more cities in larger aircraft, and the increased use of the versatile quick-change aircraft, additional sales efforts must be expanded if the full potential of air cargo service is to be realized. Nor will the rails and trucks sit idly by and watch the airlines steal the bride. 

The air carriers have not completed their efforts, nor are they resting on their laurels. Inter-carrier and shipper-carrier talks on containerization are continuing, under CAB auspices, and there is good cause to expect still further advancement in this area.

Encouraged To Experiment 

As a general rule, the Board has encouraged the carriers to experiment with lower, developmental-type rates, and with airfreight service innovations.

As a further example, the introduction of low eastbound rates on perishables some years ago has now produced an annual multimillion-pound movement of strawberries, with air transportation being recognized as a major competitive mode.

In keeping with the mandate in the Federal Aviation Act to promote and encourage the development of air transportation, the CAB joined with the 

National Industrial Traffic League in sponsoring five regional air cargo workshops, which were held September through November 1966. The workshops provided a forum for shippers to discuss their needs and their problems with air carrier representatives.

That expectation was realized. As a result of the workshop dialogue, many areas of concern were discussed. The airlines and the CAB found many obstacles to getting freight into the air, and have already begun to work to that end in the following areas:

The Board has produced an information booklet on the ratemaking process in air transportation;

In response to shipper complaints concerning late receipt of airfreight tariff revisions, the airlines created an advance subscription offering for their tariff revisions. Now on request, tariff revisions are mailed to subscribers (airmail or first class) the same day they are filed with the

CAB; 

The CAB has permitted airlines to discuss accessorial services, and assembly and distribution rules. The meetings were open to and were attended by shippers, including airfreight forwarders. Several agreements have since been submitted to the Board for approval; 142

CAB Initiates Study 

The Board has initiated an informal study of airline liability and claim rules and practices;

The CAB has authorized the air carriers and airfreight forwarders to meet separately and discuss credit terms for air shipments. Shippers have attended these meetings and voiced their views; and 

The CAB has authorized discussions among the domestic airlines to explore the feasibility and implementation of the Standard International Trade 

Classification (SITC) commodity coding system for airfreight tariffs.

The workshops are believed to have been an imaginative program, cooperatively well-organized by members of the NITL, CAB, and air carrier staffs, and were well received by the participants. 

Discussions of like nature, but with increased attention to the marketing aspects of air cargo, are continuing under the auspices of the carriers and numerous local civic organizations. 

Pace To Continue Rapid 

There is little question that air cargo will continue to grow at a rapid pace, although the rate of growth must inevitably reflect the broadening base. One aviation executive has stated that the airlines will be flying 10 billion ton-miles of air cargo by 1972.

Air carriers, and particularly airfreight forwarders and cooperative shippers associations, hold great promise as the possible answer to the muchbemoaned small-shipment problem in surface transportation. 

Perhaps the savings-incentive to the airlines through containerization plus a substantially improved air-surface network into and beyond air consolidation and break-bulk centers will permit these small-shipment specialists to continue to offer low rates and a wide range of services whereby the package shipper will be able to continue to participate in the worldwide marketing of his products.

Coordinated Service 

At the time the Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938 was enacted the only cargo service by air was that provided by the Railway Express Agency. Although wholly owned by railroads, REA performed a coordinated surface/air, doorto-door service that provided expedited movement of parcels under contracts with substantially all of the domestic air carriers. 

On passage of the 1938 Civil Aeronautics Act REA applied for a grand father certificate of public convenience and necessity. This proceeding (Railway Express Agency Grandfather Certificate, 2 CAB 531 (1941)) was a landmark one because it was the first in which the CAB had occasion to consider the status of indirect air carriers. 

The Board held that although REA acted as an intermediary between shipper and the direct air carrier, it nevertheless was engaged in the business of transporting property by air as a common carrier and therefore came within the statutory definition of "air carrier." 

Although the CAB did not grant REA a certificate of public convenience and necessity, it subsequently exempted the agency from the certificate 143

requirement and permitted it to engage in air transportation as an indirect air carrier pursuant to air express contracts then existing with the airlines. 

Although REA provided the beginning of air cargo through its air express service, this service was geared primarily for small shipments of high intrinsic value or emergency nature.

New Concept Introduced 

During World War II, the airlines began to sense the potentials of an air cargo system oriented to large shipments and the everyday movement of freight rather than the emergency-type shipment of articles which by nature require rapid transportation. Looking toward the end of the war and the availability of larger capacity aircraft, the airlines introduced the airfreight concept. This new type of service was provided by the airlines themselves, and it was founded on a volume rate principle that was designed to encourage larger shipments.

The airfreight forwarder industry as such came into being subsequent to World War II. With the transition of the economy to a peacetime basis, there was a realization of the need of forwarder services to fill the void that was developing between air express and airfreight service.

As a result, more than 70 applications were filed with the Board seeking authority to engage in airfreight forwarding service.

An air freight forwarder is an indirect air carrier which in the ordinary course of its business assembles and consolidates property or performs breakbulk and distributing operations with respect to consolidated shipments, and is responsible for the transportation of property from the point of receipt to point of destination and utilizes for the whole or any part of such transportation the services of a direct air carrier. In relation to the shipper a forwarder is a carrier, but in relation to the direct carrier whose services it utilizes the forwarder is a shipper.

In 1948, the Board instituted an investigation for the purpose of determining whether airfreight forwarding services should be authorized, and, if so, the type of authority that should be granted. (Air Freight Forwarder Case, docket 681 et al.)

Authority by Exemption 

As a result of this investigation, the CAB concluded that there was a need for airfreight forwarders, but it decided that they should be authorized by exemption rather than by certificate of public convenience and necessity. 

The CAB also decided in that proceeding that forwarders should be granted letters of registration on fulfillment of certain minimal requirements demonstrating their ability to engage in airfreight forwarding. Forwarders were required to have cargo and liability insurance for the protection of customers and they also were required to file statistical reports with the Board. 

Significantly, the decision of the CAB in that matter held that forwarders should not be required to demonstrate fitness, willingness, and ability, the statutory requisites for a certificate; and, moreover, it found that there should be no limitation imposed on the number of airfreight forwarders who might be authorized nor on the number of points between which airfreight forwarder service might be provided. 144

In view of the lack of actual experience in airfreight forwarding at that time (1948), the CAB decided to limit the forwarder authorization to an experimental 5-year period.

Eleven Receive Authority 

As of the close of the 1949 fiscal year, 11 of the forwarder applicants had completed their prerequisites for operating authority and had been granted letters of registration. These became the first companies authorized to engage indirectly in the transportation of property by air since the REA was issued an exemption in 1941.

At the conclusion of the 5-year period the CAB reviewed the status of the airfreight forwarder industry and concluded that existing forwarder authorizations should be renewed for an indefinite period. (Air Freight Forwarder 

Investigation, 21 CAB 536 (1955).)

In reaching this decision the Board found: there was a substantial and increasing acceptance of the forwarders' services by the shipping public; the forwarders handled many shipments at rates lower than charged by the airlines; they provided efficient ground handling services, extensive personal solicitation, and advertising for airfreight; these results benefited the shipping public and stimulated the development of air transportation; and these benefits had been gained without cost to the Government or net injury to the airlines.

The policy of free competitive entry into airfreight forwarding was reaffirmed, and again the CAB declined to impose a requirement that a forwarder-applicant show a need for its services. 

Public Need Recognized 

The international phase of the Air Freight Forwarder Case was decided in September 1949 (11 CAB 182 (1949).) and, as in the domestic phase, the

CAB concluded that there was a public need for international airfreight forwarding.

In the Board's opinion "The need for expedition, the documentary complexities of international shipments and the financial problems involved all call for the services of specialists, which, except in rare instances, neither the exporters, the importers, nor the direct carriers can economically supply. 

The primary purpose of having overseas and foreign airfreight forwarders is to offer to the shipper the usual transportation services plus the needed additional special services-services many of which cannot be provided by the forwarders except as independent operators, in which capacity they will have a healthy self-interest in the development of the air cargo potential." 

As in the domestic phase, the international authorizations were for a temporary experimental period.

The International Air Freight Investigation (27 CAB 658), decided No vember 6, 1967, renewed the operating authority of all international airfreight forwarders for an indefinite period. No need was found to impose a fitness test or a showing of public need. No restrictions were placed on the geographic areas to be served or on the commodities to be handled. 145

Very Little Diversion The Air Freight Forwarder Authority Case, decided July 10, 1964, involved the applications of seven direct motor carriers and one subsidiary of a direct motor carrier, for domestic and international airfreight forwarder authority limited to carriage of household goods. The CAB concluded that there was not likely to be significant diversion of household goods from air to surface because very little at that time moved by air. Moreover, the 

Board noted that the capital investment of the applicants was mainly in warehouses and local equipment.

Consequently, the CAB found that it would not be adverse to the public interest to grant airfreight forwarder authority limited to household goods, and such authorization was granted for an experimental period of 5 years. 

On September 22, 1967 the CAB concluded in the Motor Carrier-Air 

Freight Forwarder Investigation that general commodity airfreight forwarder authority should be issued to three long-distance motor carriers for an experimental 5-year period. This decision was appealed to the U.S. Court of 

Appeals for the Second Circuit by a number of airfreight forwarders. The

Court on March 16, 1968 remanded the case to the Board for further findings. The ultimate outcome of this case remains in doubt, as the Board has vacated its decision and on May 8, 1968 solicited comments on various questions raised by the court decision.

Twenty-Five-Mile Pickup Limit 

One additional regulatory action affecting the authority of airfreight forwarders concerns the permissible limits of pickup and delivery service that may be provided by air carriers. Historically, the CAB has considered services confined to a radius of 25 miles of the airport or of the city limits of a certificated point, as bona fide pickup and delivery service and has accepted tariffs providing for such services.

Similarly, the Interstate Commerce Commission has considered such services as "incidental to transportation by aircraft" and thereby exempt from regulation under the Interstate Commerce Act. (Section 203 (b) (7a) of pt. 

II of the Interstate Commerce Act.) 

In response to numerous complaints and comments, largely from airfreight forwarders seeking to extend pickup and delivery services beyond the traditional 25-mile radius, the CAB and the Interstate Commerce Commission instituted companion rulemaking proceedings to develop new procedures for determining pickup and delivery zones. 

In part 222 of the Board's Economic Regulations adopted June 12, 1964, the CAB established a procedure for approval of more realistic pickup and delivery areas. To date 13 airfreight forwarder applications for authority to file tariffs providing pickup and delivery service beyond the 25-mile zone have been granted. 

Authority for 148

Airfreight forwarders have played a significant role in the development of airfreight, and there has been a substantial growth both in the number of forwarders and in the amount of freight handled by them. In contrast to 11 companies authorized to engage in airfreight forwarding as of June 30, 146

1949, there are now 148 authorized forwarders. Thirty of these hold domestic authority only, 32 hold international authority only, and 86 hold both domestic and international authority. 

The forwarders' gross revenue has increased from $4 million in 1950 (the first full year for which reports were filed by air freight forwarders), to $206 million in 1966.

Most early reports did not segregate revenues by activity. In terms of traffic, the forwarders consolidated 332,614 tons in 1966 as compared with 19,433 in 1950. The industry's contribution to airfreight development also has increased dramatically. The percentage of airline cargo tonnage generated by airfreight forwarders increased from 8.3 percent in 1953 to 24 percent in 1966.

In general, the CAB's policy with respect to licensing airfreight forwarders has been one of permitting maximum competition and opportunity to enter the field in order to provide for full development of air cargo. 

The Board has imposed minimal requirements consistent with protection of the public interest.

Under this philosophy, the industry as a whole has flourished and made significant contributions to the development of air cargo. Although forwarders have provided rate savings in some instances, perhaps their most significant contributions have been the services they render shippers and the vitality and imagination they have brought to marketing air cargo service. 147

TRAVEL AGENCY MATTERS HAVE THE CAB'S EYE 

The CAB has no authority to regulate directly the appointment or retention of travel agents by the airlines or the business relationship between such persons. The Board, however, does have authority to look into unfair or deceptive practices or unfair methods of competition involving ticket agents under certain circumstances.

The domestic and international airlines through their respective trade associations-the Air Traffic Conference of America and the International

Air Transport Association-over the years have developed comprehensive rules to govern such matters. These rules, since they involve concerted action by the airlines affecting air transportation, must be filed with the CAB and are subject to approval or disapproval in the public interest. 

The initial rules relating to the appointment and retention of the agents by the domestic carriers were adopted in 1940. More comprehensive rules in the form of an ATC agency resolution were filed with the Board and approved in 1941. These rules provided for a 5-percent commission on the sale of air transportation. At the outbreak of World War II, however, the agency program became dormant and the payment of commissions was suspended for a period ending 6 months after the end of the war. 

Restraints Imposed 

During the postwar years, the domestic airlines incorporated in their agency program a number of restraints governing entry into the travel agency business on behalf of the airlines.

These restraints included such matters as a requirement that an agent have the sponsorship of an airline or airlines and a requirement that a showing be made as to the need for the agent's services in a particular area. 

In addition, the rules pertaining to suspension or cancellation were unclear and subject to possible discrimination. Furthermore, there was no provision for review of the turndown of a particular agent.

During 1947, the international carriers through IATA adopted an agency program which was patterned closely after the domestic carriers' program. 

In 1957, as a result of complaints from existing and prospective travel agents relative to the procedures used by ATC governing the appointment and retention of agents, and as a result of congressional inquiry into the matter, the Board instituted two investigations. One related to ATC and the other to ATA. The agency agreements were considered adverse to the public interest or in violation of the statute. 148

Program Requires Pevision 

In 1959 the Board issued its final opinion in the ATC Agency Resolution Investigation. It found the public interest required revision of the agency program. Thus the carriers were required to delete the sponsorship and need provisions; to establish procedures for recordation and notification of reasons for disapproving applicants or agents; to provide rebuttal opportunity to agents reviewed for violation or other charges, and, among other things, to provide for the arbitration of conflicts between the agent and his principals. 

With the completion of that particular investigation, the CAB activated the IATA Agency Resolution Investigation. In its decision, CAB reached many of the same conclusions as were reached in the domestic proceeding. 

Thereafter in 1962, the Board in an informal proceeding concluded a careful examination into the ATC agency procedures, many of which were adopted as a result of the formal proceeding. This proceeding resulted in a redefinition in subjective terms of certain broad standards previously used in considering an agent's qualifications.

Agreement on Forms 

During 1964, the CAB approved an important agreement among the domestic airlines which required all agents to utilize a standard ticket form and remit funds due the air carriers to a designated bank area instead of to the individual carriers. This program resulted in significant benefits to both the carrier and the agency industries.

At the same time the Board approved an agreement among the IATA members incorporating various reforms adopted earlier by the domestic carriers which were designed to expedite consideration of agents' applications for approval and also to improve various administrative procedures. 

That same year, as a means of expediting the processing of new agency applications, the ATC members adopted a program whereby the function of review and approval of agents was transferred from the ATC agency committee which met but twice a year to the ATC's executive secretary. 

The domestic carriers also adopted a bonding program which was designed to eliminate an agent's having to maintain a trust account and present evidence of a satisfactory credit standing. These programs were approved by the Board.

In-Plant Representatives 

The CAB in 1965 granted temporary approval of an agreement which precluded travel agents from having a representative on the premises of a commercial customer for the purpose of selling air transportation. In taking this action, however, the CAB concluded that agents should not be prohibited from having so-called in-plant representation but, by the same token, that such representation should be carried on within the framework of uniform procedures established by the airlines.

A resolution designed to implement these procedures and to revise substantially the then existing structure of commissions was filed. Intense opposition to the revised commission structure, however, prompted the domestic airlines to withdraw this aspect of the proposal. 149

Since 1951 the Board has permitted the international air carriers to grant free or reduced-rate transportation to agents for the purpose of familiarization with the product they sell. No similar concession had been granted for domestic transportation. In 1966, the Board reexamined its approval of the international program, at which time it authorized U.S. and foreign airlines, as well as representatives of the travel agency industry, to engage in discussions looking toward agreement on a program which would more effectively relate the grant of such transportation to the business interests of the carriers and of the agents.

The outcome of this authorization was a revised and more equitable international program, and a new program for domestic travel permitting agents for the first time to obtain one reduced-rate pass per year for each qualified employee.

Study on Agents' Costs 

Because of the controversy over a proposed change in commission structure, the CAB in 1966 urged the carriers and agents to undertake a joint study which would provide information needed for determining what it costs a travel agent to provide those services which are of value to his carrier principals, and what it would cost the air carrier itself were it to provide service. 

In 1967, the Board approved air carrier rules whereby the agents for the first time would be permitted to establish branch offices on the premises of commercial clients. Noting that the costs of operating such branch offices are somewhat less than those at other sales offices, the Board allowed the carriers to establish a commission level of 3 percent on the sale of point-to-point air transportation rather than 5 percent normally applicable to sales made in other agency locations. This action was contingent on an increase from 5 to 7 percent of all sales of "Discover America" excursion fares and family plan fares.

A significant development affecting agents in 1968 was the adoption by the Board of a new economic regulation authorizing air carriers to grant free or reduced-rate transportation to travel agents on group familiarization tours between points within the United States, Puerto Rico, the Virgin Islands, American Samoa, and Guam, subject to prescribed conditions. 

Previously, the CAB had granted domestic carriers individual exemptions to provide free transportation for this purpose. 

Some 24,000 agents were granted free transportation under individual exemptions since 1963.

The new regulation adopted this past year supplements one approved the year before which permitted air carriers to grant reduced-rate privileges to individual agency employes for the purpose of enhancing their professional ability and capacity to generate, promote and sell air transportation. 150

AVIATION LEGISLATION Broad Pattern of Regulation Remains Essentially Unchanged 

The first legislative step toward building what was to become the finest transportation service in the world was the Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938. 

Although this statute was amended and subsequently revised into the 

Federal Aviation Act of 1958 (with the economic regulatory provisions remaining unchanged), it has served successfully over the past three decades as the benchmark for the Federal regulation of a dynamic and growing industry. In reality, of course, the act of 1938 was far more advanced than the industry it was then designed to serve, since it was patterned on the regulatory provisions of the Interstate Commerce Act. Many provisions, in fact, were taken over substantially verbatim. Thus, the new statute was instantly supplied with the benefits of a half century of experience and refinement in the administrative regulation of large and significant transportation problems.

As a result, the 1938 act provided a sophisticated and well-developed system of regulation which has required little change, except for the area of aircraft safety regulation which was, in 1938, almost completely new and without precedent.

Single Agency for Industry 

Under the 1938 act, a single Federal agency was substituted for the several which had been attempting to regulate and promote the industry during its early stages. The new agency consisted of three largely autonomous bodies, the five-member Civil Aeronautics Authority, exercising quasi-judicial and quasi-legislative functions concerning economic and safety matters; a threeman Air Safety Board for investigating accidents; and an Administrator who exercised executive functions covering the development and promotion of civil aviation.

The scope of safety regulation was generally broadened by the new statute, mail contract bidding was largely eliminated, economic stability was insured through a system of permanent certification for the major trunk airlines, and the industry was accorded the opportunity for long-range development and cooperative effort with the authorization of antitrust immunity for intercarrier agreements approved by the Authority. 

The act also authorized subsidy assistance to air carriers in the form of mail pay, and the act imposed control over rates (other than in foreign operations). For the first time it appeared that there would be a single, uniform administration of aviation promotion and regulation in the United 

States. 

Within 2 years, however, the new infant was to be fitted with a new suit.

318-932 O - 68 - 11 151

CAA Is Reorganized 

In 1940 President Roosevelt submitted to the Congress two related plans for the reorganization of the Authority. Under the reorganization, the fivemember Authority became the independent Civil Aeronautics Board, the office of the administrator of Civil Aeronautics was renamed the Civil Aeronautics Administration and placed under the Department of Commerce wholly apart from the CAB; and the three-man Air Safety Board was abolished, its procedures and personnel forming a newly-created Bureau of 

Safety within the CAB. 

The CAB, however, still retained the responsibility for prescribing safety regulations and suspending or revoking certain airman or aircraft certificates. 

The President explained that the reorganization would reduce the number of administrative agencies and simplify the task of executive management, while drawing a more practical separation between the functions of the Administrator and the functions of the Board. 

Further advances in executive management were made with Reorganization Plan No. 3 of 1961. Pursuant to that plan, the Board was permitted to delegate functions to its staff. The Board delegates adjudicatory functions to its hearing examiners, with authority in the CAB either to give effect to an examiner's initial decision, or to review it.

Review Procedures 

A vote of a majority of the Board, less one, is sufficient to bring any action before the Board for review. Under CAB regulations such discretionary review may be granted (a) where the Board concludes that the examiner has made an erroneous finding as to a material fact or committed a prejudicial procedural error; (b) where a necessary legal conclusion is without governing precedent or is a departure from or contrary to law, Board rule or precedent, or (c) where a substantial and important question of law, policy or discretion is involved.

Additionally, certain nonhearing matters are delegated to the staff. In all delegations, the procedure was designed to enable the Board to operate more efficiently by providing the Board itself with more time to focus on and decide policy issues. 

The economic regulation of aviation has been eminently successful. 

The CAB and the courts have added specific definitions and interpretations unique to the air transportation field to the 50-year experience of surface transportation regulation which had served as the model for the Civil Aeronautics Act. Clearly the economic advances in the industry up to 1958, while great, did not present new problems of a kind which required any major change in the economic regulatory provisions. 

However, this was not true with respect to matters affecting safety. In the decade following World War II, there was a tremendous increase in the speed of aircraft and a rapid growth in the volume of daily flights. 

These factors, along with the fact that aviation navigation facilities then in use were rapidly becoming inadequate, were creating serious congestion in the airspace. The seriousness of the problem was dramatically displayed when, in May 1958, a military jet trainer collided with a civilian transport 152

plane, becoming the third major disaster in a 312-month period. The most costly civil air tragedy in the Nation's history had occurred in 1956 when two airliners collided over the Grand Canyon with a loss of 128 lives. The need, clearly, was for the creation of a single aviation agency, consisting of both civil and military personnel, to conduct the operation and development of aviation facilities and services relating to all air traffic control. 

Creates New Agency 

The Federal Aviation Act of 1958, while working virtually no changes. of substance in the economic regulatory provisions of the Civil Aeronautics Act, did establish a new agency-the Federal Aviation Agency-under the direction of a civilian Administrator. The Administrator was given power to regulate the use of the navigable airspace; establish and operate air navigation facilities; prescribe air traffic rules for all aircraft; conduct research and development; and suspend or revoke safety certificates, subject to review by the CAB. In addition, the Administrator retained all authority which his predecessor had under the 1938 act. In essence, the 1958 act vested in the Administrator the necessary authority to control all aspects of airspace management.

In the spring of 1967, the Department of Transportation was created and the Federal Aviation Agency placed within the new Department, with its name being changed to the Federal Aviation Administration. The new act also transferred the Board's accident investigating and related safety functions to the Department, and redelegated them to an independent agency-the National Transportation Safety Board. In all other respectsnamely those relating to economic regulation-the CAB's functions remained unchanged.

There have, of course, been several legislative modifications in the economic regulatory provisions of the Civil Aeronautics Act, but the broad pattern of regulation has remained essentially unchanged. Most of the significant changes are discussed in earlier sections of this report, and will not be repeated here. A few additions, however, are worthy of note. 

In 1952, for example, the CAB received statutory authority to regulate certain practices of ticket agents in order to prevent unfair or deceptive practices, rebates and unfair methods of competition. 

In 1962, as part of the legislation which had for its main purpose the permanent certification of the supplemental carriers, the Board received authority, which it had long sought, to impose civil penalties for offenses which did not involve knowing or willful misconduct and where the existing criminal procedures were too drastic, too cumbersome or inappropriate.

Statutes in the airmail field have established air parcel post service, and permitted military mail and tape recordings to be carried by the U.S.-flag carriers at no cost to the serviceman.

Several problems, of course, still require legislative remedy; notably, there is a need for Board authority in the field of foreign rates. Yet, in essence, the past three decades of economic regulation have been a refinement and adaptation of principles enacted in 1938, and, indeed, even earlier than that. 153

THE CIVIL AERONAUTICS ACT OF 1938 WITH CHRONOLOGICAL LIST OF AMENDMENTS 

1 

AND REVISIONS ¹ Civil Aeronautics Act of 1938, Public Law 75-706, 52 Stat. 973. Established first comprehensive structure for the regulation of the economic and safety aspects of commercial aviation.

Civilian Pilot Training Act, Public Law 76–153, 53 Stat. 855. Authorized Civil Aero nautics Authority to train civilian pilots.

Reorganization Plans Nos. III and IV, P. Recs. No. 75, 54 Stat. 1231. Separated and clarified functions of Civil Aeronautics Board from those of the Administrator of 

Civil Aeronautics. 

Act of August 4, 1947, Public Law 80-346, 61 Stat. 743. Eliminated the requirement for joint rates in cases of through or coordinated service involving an air carrier and common carrier subject to Interstate Commerce Act, and substituted a requirement of just and reasonable rates. 

Act of June 29, 1948, Public Law 80-815, 62 Stat. 1093. Authorized Administrator to train air traffic control tower operators. 

Act of June 26, 1949, Public Law 81-186, 63 Stat. 480. Provided for the regulation of explosives and other dangerous articles transported by air. 

Reorganization Plan No. 13 of 1950, 64 Stat. 1266. Transferred certain administra tive responsibilities from the Board itself to the Chairman of the Civil Aero nautics Board. 

Act of August 3, 1950, Public Law 81-635, 64 Stat. 395. Made it criminal to willfully display misleading markings as to the nationality of aircraft. 

War Risk Insurance Act, Public Law 82-123, 65 Stat. 65. Authorized provision of war risk insurance. 

Act of July 14, 1952, Public Law 82-539, 66 Stat. 628. Brought ticket agents within the regulatory jurisdiction of the Board for purposes of preventing unfair or deceptive practices, rebates and unfair methods of competition. 

Act of August 8, 1953, Public Law 83-225, 67 Stat. 489. Amended Air Commerce Act so as to transfer certain functions of the Civil Aeronautics Administrator regarding the navigation of foreign civil aircraft to the Civil Aeronautics Board. 

Reorganization Plan No. 10 of 1953, 67 Stat. 644. Established separate payment of service mail rate by Postmaster General and subsidy mail rate by the Civil Aero nautics Board. 

Act of May 19, 1955, Public Law 84-38, 69 Stat. 49. Provides for the permanent certification of the local service carriers. 

Act of July 20, 1956, Public Law 84-741, 70 Stat. 591. Provides for the permanent certification of Hawaiian and Alaskan air carriers.

Act of August 1, 1956, Public Law 84-865, 70 Stat. 784. Authorizes reduced-rate transportation on a space-available basis for ministers of religion. 

1 In addition to the basic statute, and the important amendments listed herein, there have been other enactments related to the administration of the basic act or aviation in general. They are not included here but may be found in Aeronautical Statutes and Related 

Material, published from time to time by the Board and available from the Superindendent of Documents. 154

Act of September 7, 1957, Public Law 85-307, 71 Stat. 629. Authorized Board to guarantee equipment loans for local service air carriers, metropolitan helicopter service and certain territorial air carriers. 

Federal Aviation Act of 1958, Public Law 85–726, 72 Stat. 731. Recodified the gen eral economic regulatory authority of the Civil Aeronautics Act and established the Federal Aviation Agency to regulate safety and provide for safe and efficient use of airspace by civil and military aircraft.

Act of July 8, 1959, Public Law 86-81, 73 Stat. 180. Facilities financing of aircraft engines and propellers. 

Act of August 29, 1959, Public Law 86-199, 73 Stat. 427. Authorized use of airmail for service of process. 

Act of July 12, 1960, Public Law 86-627, 74 Stat. 445. Clarified provisions relating to free or reduced-rate transportation for employees and directors of air carriers, and their families. 

Act of July 14, 1960, Public Law 86-661, 74 Stat. 527. Provided for temporary authorization for certain air carriers to engage in supplemental air transportation. 

Act of Sept. 13, 1960, Public Law 86-758, 74 Stat. 901. Authorized the elimination of a hearing in certain cases arising under sec. 408 of the act.

Act of September 13, 1961, Public Law 87-225, 75 Stat. 497. Provides for reasonable notice of applications to the U.S. Courts of Appeals for interlocutory relief against orders of the Board (and other agencies). 

Reorganization Plan No. 3 of 1961, 75 Stat. 837. Authorized the Board to delegate functions to the staff and provided for the transfer of certain functions to the chairman.

Act of July 10, 1962, Public Law 87-528, 76 Stat. 143. Provided for permanent certification of the supplemental air carriers and for civil penalties for certain economic violations. 

Act of October 15, 1962, Public Law 87-810, 76 Stat. 921. Provided additional authority to the Board in the investigation of aircraft accidents. 

Act of October 15, 1962, Public Law 87-820, 76 Stat. 936. Provided for the transfer of the loan guaranty functions to the Secretary of Commerce. 

Department of Transportation Act, Public Law 89–670, 80 Stat. 931. Established the 

Department of Transportation and transferred the Board's safey and accident investigation functions to the National Transportation Safety Board within the Department. 155
