THREE DECADES-A REVIEW 

In its first year, the Civil Aeronautics

Authority made great strides in reorganizing, stabilizing, and improving a civil air transportation system in chaotic and declining condition at the time of the act's passage. The Authority was able to adopt long-range programs based on sound economic principles and lay the foundations for extensive aviation projects and plans for the future. 

Industry Profits The aviation industry as a whole profited and advanced immensely during the Authority's first year of life. In 

August 1939, domestic passenger traffic was up 45.9 percent, express 49.7 percent, and mail carriage 12.5 percent over 

August of the previous year. During fiscal 1939, the number of civilian pilots increased substantially, civilian aircraft production increased by 100 percent and at September 1, 1939 air passenger safety enjoyed a 500-percent increase over the comparable 1938 date. 1938/1939 Among the Authority's first-year activities (fiscal year ending June 30, 1939) several were, by any yardstick, outstanding.

The Civil Aeronautics Act provided that any existing air carrier which applied for a certificate of public convenience and necessity within 120 days after the act was enacted would be entitled to receive "grandfather" rights showing it had offered adequate service prior to the act. The Authority issued 

23 certificates of public convenience and necessity authorizing U.S.-flag carriers to engage in domestic passenger, cargo, or mail service over domestic or overseas routes. 

Those receiving grandfather certificates were:

Domestic: American Airlines, Inc.; 

Boston-Maine Airways, Inc.; Braniff

Airways, Inc.; Canadian Colonial Air ways, Inc.; Chicago & Southern Air

Lines, Inc.; Continental Airlines, Inc. 

Delta Air Corp.; Eastern Air Lines, 

Inc.; Inland Airlines, Inc.; Marquette

Airlines, Inc.; Mayflower Mid-Conti nent Airlines, Inc.; National Airlines, Inc.; Northwest Airlines, Inc.; Penn sylvania-Central Airlines Corp.; Trans continental & Western Air, Inc.; United

Airlines Transport Corp.; Western Air 

Express Corp.; and Wilmington-Catalina Airline, Ltd.

Foreign: Boston-Maine Airways, Inc.; 

Canadian Colonial Airways, Inc.; 

Northwest Airlines, Inc.; Pan American 

Airways, Inc.; Pan American-Grace

Airways, Inc.; United Airlines Trans port Corp.; and Uraba, Medellin & Central Airways, Inc.

Territorial: Inter Island Airways, 

Ltd. and Pan American Airways, Inc. 

On May 20, 1939, the Authority granted Pan American Airways a certificate to provide the first regular trans-Atlantic service between the

United States and Europe by establishing reciprocal transport agreements with the United Kingdom and France. At that point, eleven American-flag carriers international were engaged in air transportation.

The Authority announced completion of a modernization and extension program (instituted in 1937) of the over 

24,000-mile Federal Airways System in which navigational aids were built on 

2,516 miles of new airways and improvements were completed on about 

161 airports. The Authority extended this program through fiscal 1940, announced a study of the possibilities of extension of airway facilities to Alaska, established more air traffic control cen- 164

ters in new areas of the United States, developed improved technical, maintenance, and navigational equipment, and opened the Indianapolis Experimental 

Station where refined instrument-landing systems were tested. 

Airport Construction Funds 

As required by the act, the Authority submitted a preliminary report recommending to Congress a definite sum of Federal relief money for continued construction of airports and improvement of the Federal Airways System for fiscal 

1940. While the Authority did not advo cate Federal assumption of the full burden of airport construction, it did recommend expeditious determination of a permanent program of annually appropriated Federal-support funds to aid in development of adequate airport and aviation facilities. 

That year the Authority also approved construction plans for Washington National Airport.

The Authority made strides in establishing and enforcing its safety regulations on pilot competence, aircraft "airworthiness," mechanical and repair certification.

The Authority also formally inaugurated the civilian pilot training program, as authorized by Congress in 1938.

First Transpacific Service 

In international service, the Authority established the first-transpacific service between San Francisco and Auckland, New Zealand, and service between 

Seattle, Wash., and Juneau, Alaska.

The Federal Airways System was greatly expanded and improved during fiscal 1940 with completion of 3,400 miles of new airways. The Authority also approved the establishment of new air traffic control centers, radio-equipped airport traffic control stations, teletype weather reporting stations, high-powered radio range stations, and other navigational and communicational aviation aids. 

In aircraft airworthiness certification the Authority approved the first Douglas 

DC-4, largest commercial landplane built up to that time, and the Boeing model 307 Stratoliner, the first supercharged cabin airplane approved for aircarrier service in the United States. 

8,313 Pilots Get Certificates

The Authority completed the first civilian pilot training program, certifying 8,313 pilots out of approximately 

9,900 who underwent training.

The Authority also worked with 29 State representatives on airport development plans in accordance with the national plan. 1939/1940 

U.S. civil aviation growth accelerated in this fiscal period. Pilot and aircraft certification; passenger, express, and mail traffic; and domestic and international certificated route miles all showed substantial percentage increases. The domestic scheduled airlines reported that no fatal injuries occurred during any accident in fiscal 1940.

A consolidated local and joint passenger tariff (naming rates and routes for through transportation) was filed on behalf of all airlines then operating within the United States. It was an important step in securing compliance with statutory requirements.

The Authority granted experimental permission to one carrier to reduce its fares on the carrier's smaller or less modern and luxurious planes in an effort to secure full passenger loads on planes. 

This experiment, in essence, constituted the prototype for promotional and air coach fares, later successfully instituted in all air service.

In this period the Authority inaugurated the first coordinated air-carriermotor-carrier service between New York and Canada. Tariffs were filed jointly with the Authority and the Interstate 

Commerce Commission. 1940/1941 In 1940 the President sent to Congress two related plans for reorganizing the Authority. Under the reorganization, the five-member Authority became the independent Civil Aeronautics Board. 

In part due to the increased national defense activities of the Nation, airline traffic of all types grew substantially during this period; but increased demand was not always satisfied because of the preemptive needs of the military and the inability of the airlines then to obtain or build additional aircraft for civilian use. 

Since the war emergency would severely tax existing aircraft and person- 165

nel and all applications for new civilian service would require additional aircraft and personnel, inauguration of such services would be contingent on advice from the War and Navy Departments in light of national defense needs.

The CAB helped establish an Interdepartmental Air Traffic Control Board to coordinate military and civilian use of air space and to alleviate air traffic congestion. The Board also adopted many regulations to facilitate the movement of military aircraft and provide greater safety on the crowded airways.

The CAB completed several compre hensive surveys of passenger and mail traffic. 

For the first time, the Board authorized on a permanent basis an airmail and express pickup and delivery service in a small area between Pennsylvania and 

West Virginia—a service unique in that it could serve many towns situated closely together, yet still maintain a rapid flight schedule.

More Nonstop Air Services The CAB continued to approve the establishment of special experimental fare rates and joint tariff filings (rail or motor/and air) to stimulate passenger traffic in different sections of the country and more nonstop air carrier services.

To help expand transtlantic air service and encourage a sound air transportation system through fair competition, the CAB granted authority to American 

Export Airlines to engage in transatlantic passenger service to supplement that of the initial carrier, Pan American, operating in this service. The Board also authorized air service on a temporary basis between the U.S. and points in Africa and granted extension of transpacific service. In accord with the reciprocal air traffic agreement, important developments and changes occurred in the air services between the United

States and Canada. 

War requirements increased safety hazards and the CAB added 61 safety amendments to its regulations in this period, covering, among other matters, altitudes of flight, occupancy of the pilot's compartment, standards for repair stations, and requirements of oxygen supply in flight. The Board also conducted 

18 studies covering groups of accidents in various phases of aeronautical development. 1941/1942 U.S. involvement in the war during fiscal 1942 caused the CAB to concentrate most of its activities on integration of civil aviation within military needs and war requirements. In civil aviation generally, advancements continued in commercial air travel and in the airlines' financial condition. Much of this increased activity was due to the rise in national defense activity.

In redirecting its activities towards the needs of the war program in this period, the CAB, among other things, suspended all further actions on route application proceedings except in cases of significant national interest. It also amended many of its safety regulations to meet operation requirements under war conditions and it instituted special wartime procedures for new carrier service authorization. 

The need for emergency airline equipment (over half the civilian aircraft were utilized by the military) was tremendous. 

The Board instituted a wartime schedule control program which temporarily suspended certain routes, curtailed or eliminated many schedules, and reallocated or merged many route schedules. 

Air Priorities Adopted 

The Board also established an air priority system restricting nonessential traffic and outlining minimum route and stop requirements to facilitate transportation of military personnel and supplies. 

The CAB in this period also: 

Adopted certain emergency safety regulations to aid in coordination and use of air space and ground facilities by both military and civil groups; 

Adopted a faster, improved procedure in fixing rates for mail compensation to carriers; 

Expanded international service by

U.S.-flag carriers to meet growing demand. Total revenue passenger miles flown in scheduled services by U.S. carriers in foreign and territorial operations increased by more than 60 million passenger miles over 1940 totals, as compared to a 28 million passenger-mile increase between 1939 and 1940;

Modified certain of its regulations with regard to safety to guard against sabotage and provide measures essential to the security of the national defense;

Assisted the military tremendously in arranging war contracts between com- 166

1 mercial carriers and the military services; and

Adopted a new uniform system of accounts and report forms to facilitate handling and use of carrier records in the Board's mail rate work. 1942/1943 Wartime efforts and operation still held first precedence in fiscal 1943. War contract carrier operation often exceeded commercial air transport operation. The enforcement of wartime service control patterns caused a decrease in the total daily plane-mile figures scheduled for domestic operation, as compared to 1942 figures; however, domestic civilian air service decreased only about 27 percent in 1943 despite the fact that over half of its equipment was in use for military purposes only. Domestic revenue passenger miles, however, declined only 6 percent. 

International air carrier service increased its plane miles flown by about 21 percent; its revenue passenger miles by 0.09 percent. 

On June 21, 1943, the CAB terminated its emergency policy (initiated in 1941) which had suspended the majority of applications for air service due to wartime aviation requirements.

On December 5, 1942, the Board certificated 21 intra-Alaska carriers, authorizing service over both "regular" and "irregular" routes.

The great number of applications for and wide interest in local and feeder service prompted the CAB to institute a study of such service.

For the first time since its establishment, the Board investigated increases in passenger fares of 11 of the domestic carriers.

The Board established mail rate reductions which contributed to the reduction of mail revenues for 1943. 

The CAB contemplated inauguration of service between the United States and points in Mexico, Central and South 

America, and the Caribbean. To insure appropriate procedure with respect to U.S. foreign relations and policy, the air carriers were advised in a joint statement by the CAB and Department of State that they must discontinue dealing on their own with foreign governments for landing rights and that all such rights would, henceforth, be negotiated in intergovernmental dealings as provided under the law.

Regulations Clarified 

The Board reorganized, simplified, and clarified a number of its regulations involving aircraft wreckage, air traffic control authority, and changes in flight-time limitations due to wartime requirements. 

Concerning safety problems, the CAB added 59 amendments to its regulations and 32 special regulations necessitated by wartime activity. Safety hazards multiplied during this period largely because of the shortage of proper and adequate aircraft equipment and of airport facilities. The number of accidents reported exceeded 5,700, a substantial increase over the 1941 and 1942 figures. 

In connection with its accident investigation duties, the CAB began issuing safety bulletins (25 in 1943) to all classes of operators, pilots, trainees, manufacturers and other pertinent parties. These bulletins dealt with statistical studies and findings of accident causes as well as information related to precautionary measures against certain weather conditions in flight and technical difficulties among other things. 1943/1944

Fiscal 1944-the CAB, continuing its work against a backdrop of war, noted substantial commercial growth, and intensified its planning for the future.

Despite insufficient equipment, domestic carriers flew more revenue passenger miles and more mail and express-pound miles than at any time in air transport history. All revenues increased but the surpluses which most carriers earned were generally outpaced by operating expenses.

The inauguration of airfreight service by American Airlines, Inc. was the first venture in the airfreight field in the United States on certificated routes.

The Board also authorized local and feeder service on an experimental 3year basis. Essair, the first local feeder service carrier, was certificated on 

November 5, 1943.

In other major actions, the CAB initiated an investigation into the operation of nonscheduled air carriers and, at the direction of Congress, began a study of the multiple taxation of air carriers by States, the latter study generated by a Supreme Court decision which upheld the right of a State to tax the entire fleet of a particular air carrier 318-932 - 68 - 12 167

although only a fraction of the carrier's plane mileage was located in the State. 

The effect of the decision was to further encourage the multiple taxation of carriers by all States where a carrier service operates. 

After a 2-year study of future international air service needs in sections or points of the world not then served by U.S. air carriers, the Board announced a list of international air service routes as desirable extensions for post-war operation. 1944/1945 With war's end, the CAB turned full attention to the transition to peace. 

Domestic and international scheduled air carrier service continued to expand and prosper. Operating revenues of the domestic carriers increased 33 percent, revenue passenger-miles 54 percent. The fiscal 1945 safety record showed a 15.5percent decrease in the number of accidents. To continue that trend, the CAB revised a number of the "airworthiness" requirements of civil aircraft necessitated by the major technological advancements and war-year developments in aviation.

The Board in fiscal 1945 also extended the transatlantic routes of Pan

American World Airways and American Export Air Lines and authorized another 

U.S.

carrier Transcontinental and 

Western Air Lines, Inc.-to operate between the United States and Europe, North Africa, and India.

The Board took part in the 54-nation "Chicago Conference" on the drafting of a multilateral agreement governing international air rights and operations.

Although no multilateral agreement was reached at Chicago, the Board subsequently assisted the State Department in negotiating bilateral air transport agreements with many nations. In fiscal 1945, the United States reached such agreements with Spain, Iceland, Ireland, Sweden, and Denmark. 

The CAB approved the establishment of the International Air Transport Asso ciation, an organization of international airlines, to foster cooperation and collaboration among nations for economical and safe transportation and air commerce.

The Board submitted to Congress its study of multiple taxation of air carriers, requesting statutory protection of air commerce from this burden. 1945/1946 In this first full peacetime year of civil aviation operation, certificated domestic and international route miles were extended, and the development and construction of many new larger, faster, more efficient and safer aircraft types was meeting the growing demand for air travel.

The CAB meanwhile attacked the backlog of pending service applications accumulated from wartime suspension of hearings. The Board authorized seven new companies for local feeder service, the extension of foreign operations of certificated U.S. carriers, and the addition of four more U.S. carriers to engage in overseas and foreign air service, bringing the total to 13.

In one of the more notable proceedings, the Latin American Service Case, the decision granted extensive new routes in the Caribbean, Central America, and South America. Braniff Airways was certificated into Mexico, Cuba, the 

Canal Zone, and South America; Western Air Lines to Mexico City; Chicago and Southern to Caracas, Venezuela and 

Caribbean points; Eastern Air Lines to 

Mexico City and San Juan; National

Air Lines to Havana; Colonial Airlines to Bermuda, and American Airlines' temporary extension into Mexico City was made permanent. Pan American received a direct route between New 

York and San Juan and its temporary authorization for New Orleans-Merida Guatemala City service was made permanent. Pan American-Grace was authorized to operate nonstop service between the Panama Canal Zone and 

Buenos Aires.

New Routes in Pacific 

In another proceeding, The Pacific 

Service Case, the Board established new routes to Alaska, the Orient, and Aus tralia. Northwest Airlines was authorized to operate over one of the Orient routes (via great-circle across the North Pacific) and Western Air Lines' route was extended in Canada to enable connections with Northwest's great-circle route to the Orient. Concerning the other 

Orient route, Pan American was authorized to extend its Central Pacific route from Midway Island to Tokyo, Shanghai, and Hong Kong and from Manila to 

Saigon, Singapore, and Batavia. Pan 

American's South Pacific route was ex- 168-

tended from New Caledonia to Sydney, Australia. TWA's North Atlantic route also was extended from Bombay, India to Shanghai to connect with the newly authorized Pacific routes, thus establishing, in effect, a second U.S.-flag roundthe-world service, in addition to the one established by Pan American's

Pacific authorizations. 

new The Board instituted investigations into all phases of civil aviation, including pilot fatigue in air safety, universal air travel on a credit basis, fire prevention in aircraft, development of airfreight, matters relating to nonscheduled air transportation, and problems involving intercorporate air relationships.

In cooperation with the Department of State, the Board concluded bilateral air transport agreements with 10 nations -Switzerland, Norway, Portugal, Czechoslavakia, the United Kingdom, Turkey, France, Greece, Belgium, and Egypt. 1946/1947 

Civil aviation's advance continued despite economic and postwar adjustment problems. Increases occurred in the size of the U.S. civil aircraft fleet, in the use of newly developed transports, including the Douglas DC-6's and the Lockheed Constellations, and in the extension of certificated domestic and international route mileage. But, with expenses up and revenues increasing more slowly, airline earnings dropped substantially (a $24 million operating loss). This came about even though most carriers had placed larger, faster transports in operation. U.S. air safety suffered a setback in comparison to 

1946. The 8,710 accidents reported to the Board represented an almost 50percent increase over 1946 figures.

A large segment of the domestic airline industry was in critical financial straits and the CAB established emergency temporary mail rates with a view toward establishing more reasonable permanent rates as soon as economically feasible. Most of 1947 was devoted to processing emergency temporary rate cases and tariff filings (fare changes) which increased 90 percent over 1946. 

The Board approved a 10-percent domestic fare increase on trunk-line carriers.

Five feeder lines inaugurated service and their initial passenger fares were somewhat higher than those of trunk-line carriers.

The Board launched a major reexamination of existing safety standards. An unprecedented series of commercial accidents had happened during a 30-day period and the Board (in cooperation with committees of Congress and a temporary Board of Inquiry established by the President) investigated each one.

The Board's findings resulted in a complete revision and overhaul of the CAB air traffic rules and amendment of the nonscheduled air carrier certification and operation rules to improve the safety of these carriers and maintain them on a level with those of the scheduled air carriers. The regulations also were amended concerning aircraft certification and reliability of mechanics and maintenance in new model aircraft.

First Helicopter Service 

The Board also authorized a number of new domestic air routes, one additional international route and six new local feeder routes and certificated the first helicopter service in the United States. 

To more efficiently utilize the new, long-range aircraft, the CAB authorized additional nonstop services between a number of the Nation's leading cities. 

On June 17, 1947, service was inaugurated under the Pan American-Panagra interchange agreement. In its approval of the agreement the Board had indicated its favorable attitude toward establishment of one-plane service (by intercarrier agreement of two airlines) without having to extend route authorizations.

The Board took part in the work of the Air Coordinating Committee (established by Executive Order 9781) made up of representatives from the Board and other Government agencies conconcerned with the development of improved aviation policies. The committee also was responsible for developing recommended U.S. positions on matters involved in the activities of the Inter national Civil Aviation Organization 

(ICAO). 

The Board revised its economic regulations to reclassify nonscheduled carriers as either noncertificated irregular air carriers, or noncertificated cargo carriers.

Fiscal 1947 was also the year Congress enacted legislation eliminating 169

the requirement for joint carrier service rates in cases where an air carrier desired to provide through service with a common carrier subject to the Inter state Commerce Act. This legislation substituted instead a requirement that just and reasonable rates be established. 

(Public Law 80-346) 

1947/1948 

Civil aviation's expansion continued in fiscal 1948 but the financial position of the U.S. aviation industry continued unfavorable. The CAB had to find a solution.

While the Board realized that Government support through increased mail compensation could be only a temporary remedy, it made clear in several major policy cases, such as the Air Freight case, the Freight Forwarder case, and numerous mail rate proceedings, that the existing system and its future growth should be as nearly self-supporting as possible. 

Fare Adjustment Okayed 

The CAB issued a temporary revised "service" rate-not a subsidy for the five largest air carriers as well as for six other trunkline carriers and 17 feeder lines. The critical financial straits of the carriers resulted in double the number of tariff filings received by the Board in 

1947. Carrier fare revenues had not risen at rates proportionate to the rate of increase in carrier costs and the CAB approved a second postwar fare adjustment which increased domestic trunk fares by 10 percent. However, to offset this increase, the Board also approved carrier experimentation with and institution of various types of reduced excursion and promotional fares to stimulate greater passenger traffic. 

Due to the rapid growth of air freight, the CAB (in the Freight Forwarder case) allowed 58 domestic forwarders (noncertificated all-cargo carriers) to engage in airfreight-forwarding activities under letters of registration. Freight rates also came under CAB scrutiny and the Board fixed minimum rates for all carriers to prevent uneconomic rate cutting. 

The Board revised its economic regulations to clarify the operating rights of irregular nonscheduled carriers, which, the Board had found, had been violating this provision by providing scheduled service in competition with the certificated scheduled carriers and at a level substantially lower than these carriers. 

This revision terminated former irregular carrier blanket exemptions from carrier certification and required each carrier to file for individual exemptions.

The CAB also instituted a study of existing domestic route patterns to determine what alterations and modifications might be required by new technologies and equipment, mergers, and changes in passenger traffic.

The Board approved a number of trunkline route extensions, the creation of five new feeder routes and extension of others. 

Internationally, the Board concluded a number of bilateral air agreements with foreign nations and aided in the preparation of U.S. position on minimum worldwide safety standards to be promulgated by the International Civil 

Aviation Organization for its members nations. 

The aviation industry's safety record improved greatly over fiscal 1947, however, accident investigations by the Board determined the cause of three disasters which resulted in the grounding of three of four new transport aircraft due to safety limitations. 1948/1949

In fiscal 1949, the downward trend of the past 2 years was reduced and the aviation industry began to move forward. While improvements were instituted and benefits realized, the Board considered its mail rate compensation increases only a short-term corrective measure to the airline problem and initiated a number of major investigations and actions, their tone set in a statement of policy, "economic program for 1949," which described the existing situation, outlined objectives, and recommended specific actions to be taken.

The Board investigated route systems over which there appeared to be excessive competition and also investigated the cost to carriers of transporting mail and all class freight rates.

The CAB also prepared a study to establish the appropriate rate of return on investment to which a carrier should be entitled.

Aviation in general (including the 

CAB) underwent intensive investigation from 1947 until 1949 by four major groups: the Air Policy Commission, the 

Congressional Air Policy Board, the 

170

Hoover Commission, and the Senate

Interstate and Foreign Commerce Committee. 

In other specific actions, the Board: 

Certificated four all-cargo carriers. 

Approved experimental "air coach" service (off-peak hours, high density seating, nondeluxe accommodations, lower fares) on certificated carriers to attract aditional passenger traffic. 

Collaborated with the Reconstruction 

Finance Corporation to help ease and expedite the making of RFC loans to airlines. 

Updated and revised certain safety regulations, including those pertaining to irregular carriers.

Recommended to Congress the initia tion of a study to distinguish between “subsidy” and “service" in mail compensation rates. 1949/1950 In fiscal 1950, trunkline dependence on Government support started to decline and fares decreased. Major Board actions revolved around encouragement of air coach service and promotional fares and regulation enforcement. 

The CAB extended experimental air coach service until 1951. Ten major carriers then supplied it. The Board also extended a temporary trial of the family excursion fare plan and continued to encourage experimentation with promotional fares.

After a review of air traffic movements to Europe, the Board approved low, round-trip fares on one domestic certificated and three irregular air carriers to carry educational charters, religious or charitable groups to Europe from June 1 to September 30. This action was instituted in order to take advantage of the demand for foreign travel, to increase the flow of tourist dollars, and to facilitate air travel to Europe without disruption or harm to the existing trans-Atlantic service setup.

In regulatory and enforcement matters, the CAB added to its economic regulations a new section to regulate international airfreight forwarders and amended a regulation concerning irregular carriers and ticket agent relations. 

The Board instigated proceedings against a number of carriers operating without, or in excess of, authority; those engaging in rebating, tariff violations, or unfair and deceptive practices; and those failing to file agreements, secure Board approval, or comply with applicable labor relations requirements.

The Board readjusted route patterns to include smaller communities not previously served. 1950/1951 In fiscal 1951 all phases of traffic operation increased and future prospects appeared bright due to the generation of additional passenger traffic through air coach and promotional fares, the introduction of larger aircraft with greater seating capacities, and the expansion of local feeder service to accommodate more traffic from smaller communities. 

Contributing to this climate, the CAB approved and encouraged the expansion of air coach service on scheduled airlines over more extensive routes at even lower rates. The Board also extended the certificates of three local service carriers. At the same time it suspended service of a number of existing trunklines at certain points where local service could be provided more adequately. 

Helicopter experimental operations (passenger, mail, and cargo) were expanded in this period to include service in the New York area (in addition to that already authorized in Los Angeles and Chicago). 

Pan Am Acquires Carrier

In the North Atlantic Route Transfer 

Case, the Board, directed by the President, approved the acquisition by Pan 

American World Airways of American 

Overseas Airlines. At the same time, the 

Board granted additional European cities to the routes of TWA and Pan American. 

A CAB report that year recommended the separation of subsidy mail payments from total mail payments made to domestic and international air carriers; the object to distinguish clearly between Government mail "service" support and 

Government "subsidy” support.

The Board again instituted an investigation into irregular air carriers to review its entire economic regulatory policy toward them. Also begun was an investigation of air service between 

Alaska and the U.S. to reappraise future needs. 

That year the CAB also was studying air operations problems to promulgate a 171

complete new set of rules governing domestic and international air operation. 

Finally, the Board probed more deeply the aircraft accidents of fiscal 1951 due to their substantial increase in number. 

After conferences with the Civil Aeronautics Administrator and the scheduled airlines, the CAB established an Airline

Flight Safety Committee represented by both Government and industry.

1951/1952 Air traffic (mail, cargo and passenger; freight forwarder registrations, etc.) reached new heights in fiscal 1952. Increases in air coach service accounted for 15 percent of total domestic passenger miles and 18 percent of total international passenger miles. Carriers were less dependent on mail compensation support than at any time in the preceding 5 years. Domestic mail revenue accounted for only 7 percent of total revenues; international mail revenue, 16 percent. 

More carriers were placed on final mail rates than ever before.

Four trunkline carriers merged into two and two local service carriers merged in an action that marked the first significant decrease in the number of domestic air carriers serving the United States.

The trunk mergers effected were Braniff 

Airways with Mid-Continent Airlines and Western Air Lines with Inland Airlines. The merger of West Coast Airlines and Empire Air Lines was approved to improve local service in the northwest region and provide a basis for subsidy reduction.

The Board began the Irregular Air 

Carrier Investigation hearing to determine the need for nonscheduled service in addition to scheduled, and the role of nonscheduled, certificated air carrier service in the U.S. commercial aviation system.

The first official report was issued which showed revised mail compensation figures, divided as to "service" and and "subsidy" amounts, paid domestic and international carriers.

Role for Air Taxis Amendment of the CAB economic regulations established a classification for small carriers and, by blanket exemption, authorized them to render connecting service from points receiving scheduled air service to those not receiving service. These unrestricted air taxi operations were authorized within the continental United States and between points in continental United States and foreign and overseas points.

The Board instituted another investigation into the domestic airline fare structure and permitted free or reduced rate transportation of travel agents, tour conductors, and others employed in interstate or international air transportation.

A multielement service mail rate study. was established to determine a uniform payment regardless of carrier when transporting identical mail shipments between identical points. 

The CAB also assumed regulatory jurisdiction over ticket agencies to prevent deceptive practices or unfair competitive methods in the sale of air transportation (Public Law 82-539). 

Amendments to Safety Regulations that year increased emphasis on emergency evacuation provisions in aircraft and the Board instigated plans for wartime (Korea) use of civil aircraft. 

Congress enacted legislation making crimes committed on American aircraft in flight over the high seas a matter subject to Federal court. 1952/1953

Air traffic growth was getting to be a familiar story. Again in fiscal 1953 all categories of air traffic (except those in 

Alaska) spiraled to new levels and, while the general fare and rate structure of all other forms of transportation rose, cost per mile of air travel declined. Subsidy support for the domestic trunklines decreased. Nine of 13 domestic trunks were free of mail subsidy, making 96 percent of carriage on domestic trunks without 

Government support. However, the nature of the local service airlines' operations increased their need for mail subsidy. Air coach service rapidly expanded and tourists could now visit at substantially lower fares than before.

The scheduled domestic airlines wrote their best safety record-0.4 passenger fatality per 100 million passenger miles. 

The CAB now assumed full responsibility for all air subsidy payment func tions. The Post Office Department as sumed that of direct mail compensation for service to air carriers (Reorganization Plan No. 10). In this connection the CAB fixed final mail compensation rates for transport of first class and other preferential mail by scheduled airlines 172

As of June 30, 1953, 39 air carriers (including domestic, international, and territorial trunklines, local service and

Alaskan carriers) were operating on final mail rates.

The Board also assumed authority for economic control of foreign civil aircraft navigation in the United States. (Public Law 83-225)

Delta Merger Approved 

The Board approved the merger of 

Delta Air Lines, Inc., and Chicago & 

Southern Air Lines, Inc. and also some route modifications to improve service to the public.

A CAB policy statement endorsed the operation and contribution to the growth of total air traffic of air coach service. 

The Board also eliminated the temporary status of air coach service and abolished all renewal requirements and continued its endorsement of touristclass fares, overseas and family fare plans. con- Bilateral air agreements were cluded with Japan and Cuba to supplement the 41 existing ones negotiated steadily over approximately the previous 

10 years. 

New safety regulation amendments dealt with the design and certification of new aircraft and special studies were made on a number of accident cause factors.

The CAB also recommended legislation to provide for Federal aid in development of large transport aircraft. 1953/1954 Air travel volume and the financial condition of the air industry continued robust in fiscal 1954. Overall dependence on subsidies leveled off. In light of the traffic increase, the 1954 safety recordfour fatal accidents in domestic traffic, two in international-was commendable. 

The CAB extended coach fare service to new travel markets, including shorthaul operations, and it maintained a "hold-the-line" policy on air fares, encouraging the carriers to reduce or stabilize their fares wherever possible and institute air coach service whenever economically feasible.

The CAB now also eliminated its Alaska office and replaced it with one 

CAB representative, direct supervision from Washington being considered appropriate at this stage in Alaskan air carrier development. 1954/1955 Fiscal 1955, the charts still pointed upward. All air travel volumes increased and continuing favorable cost and revenue trends resulted in the highest total airline earnings to date, the major increases in net profit achieved despite an $18 million decrease in airmail subsidy. 

That year the Board made the local service carriers' certificates permanent for all their certificated terminal points and over half their certificated intermediate points.

The basic route structure of many of the domestic trunkline carriers was redirected to increase competition and provide improved airline service.

Concluding the uniform service mailrate proceedings, the CAB instituted the first "multielement rate formula" for mail service and saved the Federal Government more than $5 million. 

Policy on passenger fare increases for the subsidy-free carriers was still "holdthe-line" but the subsidized carriers enjoyed greater flexibility in experimenting with fare changes with an eye to revenue increases and subsidy reductions. 

In a number of its recommendations to Congress, the CAB pointed up the inadequacy of existing airport facilities and the need for stability in any Federal airport program. And the Board took a searching look at Washington National 

Airport and stated that a second airport in the Washington area was a definite need if future air requirements were to be met. 1955/1956 

Perhaps one of the better measurements of the burgeoning air industry in fiscal 1956 lay in the apparent disparities that materialized: airmail service grew but the mail rate remained stable, and industry services and all categories of air traffic increased. By fiscal year's end only one domestic trunkline carrier still required subsidy payments. The accident fatality rate, surveyed over the 

10 years since World War II, had decreased nearly 75 percent while passenger miles had nearly quadrupled. The order of 250 long-range jet transports by the major trunkline carriers and the implementation of VORTAC, an electronic short-distance navigation aids 173

program hinted at the growth and technological development expected in the years ahead.

In the Large Irregular Air Carrier 

Investigation decision, the CAB established a new class of carriers called "supplemental" air carriers and issued temporary exemptions pending decision of their applications for certificates. The

Board permitted the supplemental carriers to conduct charter flights without limitations as to frequency or regularity as well as individually ticketed operations not to exceed 10 trips per month between any single pair of points.

CAB route modifications eliminated the frequency of transfer flights and, in some cases, augmented the activity of the smaller, less profitable carriers.

The Board granted permanent certificates to U.S. territorial air carriers operating within Alaska and Hawaii. 

(Public Law 741) 

Mindful of future aeronautical developments and increased air traffic congestion indicators, the Board began a reevaluation of air traffic and future needs with respect to aviation safety and operating regulations.

And, as the curtain began to rise on the jet age, the Board embarked on a study of the economic impact which the introduction of jet equipment would have on future subsidy-free carrier operation. 1956/1957 

U.S. air transportation continued climbing in fiscal 1957 but unparalleled demand for increased air service, in the face of a temporary equipment shortage and inadequacy of many facilities, prompted a deluge of formal complaints to be filed with the CAB alleging insufficient or poor air service. 

To combine traffic flows of major cities, generate more effective long-haul traffic competition and permit local service carriers greater operational flexibility, the Board authorized several new trunkline services and extended certain routes. 

Decisions in CAB proceedings concerning proposed local service air routes revealed the Board's continued vigilance of local service progress and limitations. 

Local service was encouraged to seek "optimum fare level" rates to increase revenues and decrease subsidy need. The 

CAB permitted greater flexibility of local service operation and often favored its substitution at certain traffic points in place of existing trunkline service.

The Board launched its General Passenger Fare Investigation to determine the appropriate standards on which to evaluate fare increases or decreases on the major domestic trunkline carriers. 

In regard to international passenger fares, the Board disapproved of the 

IATA proposal for a 5-percent increase.

In other actions, the Board: 

Instituted an investigation of Pan

American World Airways Inc., in specific regard to its complex financial relationships with various affiliated airlines. 

Adopted a new part 234 to its economic regulations which required domestic certificated air carriers to publish and adhere to realistic flight schedules-to complete at least 75 percent of all flights within 15 minutes of the elapsed block-to-block time shown on the carriers' schedules. 1957/1958 In fiscal 1958 the airlines became the 

Nation's number one intercity common carrier (outdistancing both the railroads and the buslines). It marked the seventh straight year that the airline accident fatality rate was less than one in every 100 million passenger-miles in scheduled airline service. Congress en acted the Federal Aviation Act of 1958 

which left the Board's economic regulatory authority essentially unchanged from its authority under the Civil Aero nautics Act of 1938. Throughout the pe riod of evolution under the Civil Aeronautics Act, the Board's functions and responsibilities had increased substantially.

The Board granted international and overseas route authority to U.S.-flag carriers in seven cases and issued new permits to a number of foreign-flag airlines. 

In the cases involving U.S.-flag carriers:

The West Coast-Europe Case gave Pan American and Trans World Air lines polar routes to Europe;

legislation made permanent Northwest Air Lines' 

authority between New York, Chicago, and Anchorage via Minneapolis/St. Paul, Minn., and Edmonton, Alberta, Canada; the reopened Trans-Pacific Certificate Renewal Case resulted in authority of unlimited duration for Pan Amer- 174

ican to operate over the great circle route from Los Angeles and San Francisco via intermediate points to Japan; 

the New York-Mexico City Nonstop Service Case awarded Eastern Air Lines the nonstop route until termination of the existing United States-Mexico agreement; the Chicago-Mexico City Case authorized American Airlines to serve those cities via Fort Worth, Dallas, and 

San Antonio, Tex., and Monterrey, Mexico; the San Juan-Madrid Case allowed 

Pan American to add Madrid as an intermediate point on its South Atlantic route and gave the carrier a new segment between San Juan and Roma via the Azores, Lisbon, and Madrid; and the Service to Puerto Rico Case made permanent Eastern's authority to operate between New York-Newark and San 

Juan, designated Boston, Philadelphia, Baltimore, and Washington as coterminal points with New York/Newark on 

Pan American's route to San Juan, and authorized Trans Caribbean Airways, Inc. to serve between New York/Newark and San Juan. 

The review of local air service needs continued and the CAB established its "use it or lose it" policy which pertained to the offer of local air service to communities of possibly marginal traffic potential. On establishment of the service, these communities would be permitted to show whether they could substantially support the service they had requested.

The General Passenger Fare Investi gation hearings continued, their outcome the more significant because the major trunklines affected were unsubsidized operators dependent solely on service mail, cargo, or passenger revenues for profitable operation. 

'Super Skyways' 

Two midair collisions involving airliners and military jets drew a spotlight of publicity on private and Government efforts to improve the already outstanding record which, for the seventh straight year showed a fatality rate of less than one in every 100 million passenger-miles of scheduled air transportation. Following the collisions, the Board authorized establishment of positive control routes or "super skyways," a system to guarantee the positive traffic separation of planes flying on certain routes from altitudes of 17,000 to 22,000 feet. 1958/1959 

Most U.S. commercial air transport continued to grow but airfreight operations encountered some financial difficulties. But one other development was even more noteworthy than those. Fiscal 

1959 was the year of the birth of pure jet service. Pan American World Airways put the first Boeing 707-120 turbojet into service on October 26, 1958 

between New York and Paris. National 

Air Lines first used the 707 in domestic service on December 10, 1958 and American Airlines first used it in transcontinental service on January 25, 1959. 

Seating and cargo capacity in jet aircraft were substantially greater. Other strains of this new generation of aircraft soon were put into service in the next 

2 years Douglas DC-8s, Convairs, and

British and French products. Air trans portation was turning an important corner. 

Its review of Alaskan air services and traffic needs completed, the Board eliminated route duplication among carriers, eliminated certain points no longer requiring service, and authorized route adjustments as part of its plan to improve the entire Alaskan route structure. 

The continuing review of local service carrier development and needs prompted several major CAB decisions concerning these carriers. In one of the most no table, the Seven States Area Investiga tion, the Board's liberalized "use it or lose it" policy was implemented to authorize new or additional local air service. 

The Board initiated a survey of domestic all-cargo operation carriers to determine questions of subsidy eligibility and renewal or modification of existing all-cargo routes and it also removed the last of the 12 trunk carriers, Pan

American World Airways and North east Airlines, from subsidy entirely.

In an opinion and order in the Large 

Irregular Air Carrier Investigation, the 

Board authorized unlimited domestic charter service by 23 supplemental carriers. Twelve of the certificates were for 

5-year terms; 11 for 2 years.

The CAB on January 1, 1959 reas sumed responsibility for analysis and determination of probable causes of all light aircraft accidents in accordance with the Federal Aviation Act of 1958 

and officially transferred its safety rulemaking functions to the Federal Avia- 175

tion Agency.

The act became law August 23, 1958. 1959/1960 In fiscal 1960, the Board still concentrated on its study of local service airline needs. As trunkline carriers began purchasing newer, larger aircraft equipment for their expanded long-haul service, the need for local service transport between smaller communities and large metropolitan cities continued to mount.

The increase from 1955 through 1960 was about 113 percent.

On completion of its general passenger-fare investigation, the CAB announced acceptance of a 10½-percent allowable rate of return for the domestic trunkline industry. Reductions in discount rates in 1958 and a surcharge for turbojet aircraft service established in 1959 had the combined effect of increasing the June 1960 general passenger-fare level for domestic travel about 14 percent above that in effect as of 

January 1958. 

The Board also approved an agreement among four international route operators permitting development of a multicarrier program which would in sure the Department of Defense and other agencies reliable and responsive service at reasonable rates during periods of crisis or increased air traffic. 

The Board revised its regulations regarding the operating authority of international airfreight forwarders and modified its transatlantic charter rules in accordance with those established by member carriers of the IATA to promote basic uniform charter standards for all classes of carriers.

Participating in extensive negotiations with the United Kingdom, France, Italy and a number of other nations the CAB reexamined the entire global air traffic network, with particular attention on the realinement of route patterns in the 

Caribbean. 

To correct misleading practices which certain carriers had committed in advertising turboprop as jet aircraft, the CAB held out certain standards to the airline industry governing such advertising. 1960/1961 

Implementation of Reorganization Plan No. 3, which effected a number of organizational changes in the CAB to expedite its work and promote its efficiency, was a major fiscal 1961 step. As part of this program, a small planning office to aid in developing policies was created, and the functions of the economic staff were realined by the estab lishment of a Bureau of International Affairs and a Bureau of Economic Regulation. 

The Board approved temporary certification of supplemental air carriers for limited operations until a permanent policy for licensing these carriers could be established.

For the first time the Board approved certification of single-carrier service across the southern strip of States in the 

Southern Transcontinental Service Case. 

The first economy tourist fares on trans Pacific routes and routes in the

Western Hemisphere were approved and the CAB also established a rate floor for military traffic to develop a modern airlift capacity and permit economic operations.

The Board permitted reduced, yet sound (above minimum level), cargo rates on certain domestic carriers to promote cargo volume and fully utilize new equipment capacities, and realined a number of local-service routes in the interest of subsidy reductions. The 

Board also approved a number of loans to these carriers to facilitate their acquiring more modern aircraft and equipment. 1961/1962 By fiscal 1962, fourth year of the jet age, turbine-powered jetcraft were being used for about three-fourths of domestic trunk and seven-eighths of the international/territorial passenger-mile volume. But the air industry struggled to boost declining earnings.

Cognizant of that struggle, the CAB approved a 3-percent domestic passenger fare increase but continued to endorse strongly promotional and reduced fares.

Encouraging extensive experimentation by the carriers of various excursion, airbus, off-peak, no-reservation, and other reduced fares, the Board itself authorized a survey of such promotional fares to determine their effects on traffic volume and other data.

The CAB that year began investigations into U.S. carrier services across the Atlantic, to South America, and between the Pacific Northwest and 

Alaska and also into several competitive 176

domestic trunkline routes in the United

States with a view toward reducing the amount of direct competition between them. Greater consolidation of routes in these areas was being studied as a possible means of strengthening the Nation's route structure. 

Regional Airport Studies 

The Board initiated regional airport studies to see whether the services rendered at two or more adjacent airports could be consolidated at a single, centrally-located airport. 

To shorten the time lapse between accident investigation and issuance of its report, the Board initiated a task force approach to analysis and reports on major accidents and instituted its first electronic data processing system to record accident data.

After a thoroughgoing review of supplemental air carriers, and legislative recommendations to the Congress, the

Board was granted (in new legislation effective after the close of the fiscal year) jurisdiction on a permanent statutory basis to license and regulate the service of these carriers. 1962/1963 Although general passenger traffic and revenues on the whole continued to increase in fiscal 1963, the airline industry still grappled with the problem of earnings losses, occasioned largely by the use of larger, faster jet aircraft with seating capacity which well exceeded the volume of traffic. Promotional fares were one method of generating increased traffic and the CAB encouraged all efforts in that respect. For the first time, military standby fares were put into effect at half the regular coach fare. 

They were an immediate success. 

The Board also eliminated and modified a number of trunkline service routes at low traffic-generating points and it conducted investigations of local service at 14 points to determine, on the basis of its "use it or lose it" policy, whether service to these marginal traffic-generating areas was justifiable. 

In collaboration with other Federal agencies—the Federal Aviation Agency, the Bureau of Public Roads, the Post Office Department, and the Department of Defense-the Board continued its regional or area airport consolidation studies with an aim toward improving service to the public while conserving Federal grants to be allocated to individual airports of the National Airport System.

The Board disapproved of an IATA 

agreement to increase transatlantic and transpacific air fares.

Under its planning activities, the CAB maintained close contact with all supersonic transport developments in order to report to Congress on the economic feasibility and development of such an aircraft.

The President called on the Board to assist the Bureau of the Budget and private research organizations in a comprehensive review of U.S. international civil aviation policy since 1954. The 

White House relased the completed policy statement in April 1963.

Previewed the fitness of 30 supplemental air carriers (Public Law 87528), granting interim authority to 15 of the carriers. 

The CAB announced its tentative determination to disapprove the merger of

American and Eastern Air Lines. Shortly thereafter the carriers advised the Board that the merger agreement had been terminated and the proceeding was dismissed. 1963/1964 Fiscal 1964 was a year of trend reversals and improvements. All air carrier statistics (air traffic, miles, and profits) swept upward dramatically and subsidy. payments and passenger and freight rates trended downward, giving the public better service at lower cost. Airline earnings rose radically, about 115 percent over 1963, and revenues increased from $66 million to $114 million, a 73percent rise. Total carrier return on investment jumped almost 40 percent.

Supporting the Administration's cost reduction program, the CAB achieved a saving of 1.1 percent of its annual appropriations for fiscal 1964 and greatly increased its productivity level through greater use of data processing techniques. 

The Board approved the reduction of domestic first-class rates by 5 to 14 percent and the expansion of economy service rates to 15 new city pairs and it also was instrumental in achieving 

IATA's reduction of North Atlantic passenger fares, a saving to American travelers of about $50 million a year. 177

The CAB that year established a refined subsidy class rate for the 13 local service carriers receiving subsidy. It provided that profits earned exceeding a fair and reasonable level would be shared by the Government. The "profit sharing" provision would aid overall in reducing subsidy requirements.

Among other activities, the CAB: 

Participated in research projects involving development of supersonic commercial aircraft, the interagency Short

Haul Transport Evaluation Team, an interagency study of international transport agreements (at the end of fiscal 1964, a total of 57 bilateral transport agreements with foreign nations had been concluded with the United States since the beginning of the Board's work), and studies related to financial fitness of supplemental carriers and to airline costs in general. The Board also was active in planning studies dealing with supersonic transport, cargo costing, passenger fares. and subsidy reductions. 1964/1965 Both domestic and international air carrier operations again reflected healthy U.S. air transportation industry in fiscal 1965.

Net income of the domestic trunk carriers, after adjustment for taxes and other special items, was $180 million. 

The comparable 1964 figure was $58 million. U.S. carriers in international and territorial operations carried 22.9 pecent more passengers than the previous fiscal year, and net income after taxes and other special items, for the international and territorial operations, was up 

13 percent from the preceding year, from $74.3 to $84.3 million. 

At the same time, Federal expenditures for air transportation decreased $32.2 million as a result of CAB actions.

Of that amount, $19 million was realized in the establishment of minimum rates for military air transportation contracts and $13.2 million was saved through the service mail rate established for the 

Post Office Department and the Department of Defense for the transat lantic and transpacific areas. 

Subsidies Decrease

Subsidy payments also turned downward for the first time in the recent "jet" years, dropping almost $1.9 million from the peak in fiscal 1964. Under the "profit-sharing" provisions of the class rate, local service carriers whose earnings exceeded a stated rate of return for the year were to refund about $2 million.

The CAB also: 

Approved more promotional fares and extended and modified "Visit 

U.S.A." fares, thereby encouraging air travel to the United States from other nations.

Effected policy changes which encouraged the development of carrier specialization in air transportation. One of these established blocked-space service (carrying cargo at wholesale rates for a shipper's use on a regularly recurring basis of 60 days or more) to promote all-cargo specialists in large volume airfreight service. 

Disapproved the IATA ban on inflight entertainment on international flights as a restrictive agreement adverse to the public interest. 

Approved free baggage allowance increases (two bags of stated dimensions with no weight restrictions) on all trunkline and three local service carriers.

Adopted Air Transport Mobilization Order No. 1 in support of the war air service program designed to assure the effective carriage of priority traffic on civil air routes and the operation of designated civil aircraft in its support in case of war emergency. 

Created a Planning, Programing and

Research Division in its Bureau of Economics and commenced studies of air economic traffic forecasting, airline trends, tourism, and other related transportation research. 

Achieved a $305,000 reduction, or 2.7 percent of its $11.1 million annual appropriation, in carrying out the President's cost reduction program. 1965/1966 Fiscal 1966 in the air industry was another prosperous year and many CAB actions resulted in savings for the Fed eral Government. The Board partici pated in a number of studies-internationally, with its work in the international aviation policy review, and domestically, concerning supersonic transport, V/STOL, and airline economic trends.

The Board also instituted investigations into the need for expanded trunkline competition and new long-haul 178

services and it approved the realinement of certain domestic local-service carrier routes to eliminate both service at low traffic-generating points and obsolete restrictions established during the infancy of local-service. This was done to transfer trunkline routes to local-service carriage where economically feasible, to consolidate service to two or more points at a single airport, and to accelerate the downward subsidy trend for local service carriers.

Eliminated by congressional mandate, 

Federal subsidy payments to certificated helicopter carriers and encouraged trunklines, as the major beneficiaries of helicopter service, to aid in these helicopter operations. 

Approved incentive rate reductions for trunkline carriage of containerized cargo.

Endorsed continued promotional fares by approving both "Discover America" excursion fares and youth fares.

Adopted new minimum rates for military airlift by civil air carriers which were expected to save the Federal Government about $40.8 million in Defense

Department funds in fiscal 1967. The rates were effective April 1, 1966 for international services and July 1, 1966 

for domestic services. By making April 1 

the effective date for international services, an additional $3.75 million was saved in fiscal 1966. 

Achieved additional reductions in the rate of compensation paid by the Post

Office Department to U.S. carriers for carriage of mail-an overall 8 percent for domestic operations for annual estimated savings of $5 million and reductions in the Latin American service mail rate of 27.5 percent for a saving of about $1 million annually at the then current volumes. 

1966/1967 

Despite the airline strike of midsummer 1966, the commercial air transport industry maintained stability and continued to grow while Federal Government support, in the form of subsidies, continued to decline. Establishment of the Department of Transportation wrought changes in CAB function and organization. All aircraft accident investigation functions were transferred from the CAB to the National Transpor tation Safety Board. 

The Board also approved reduced rates for airfreight in domestic and international service, and the addition of several all-cargo flights to handle growing volumes of airfreight traffic.

The CAB allowed local service car riers more liberal operating authority and access to higher density markets on a subsidy-ineligible basis to improve route structure service and stimulate higher earnings.

Transpacific Case The Transpacific Route Investigation affecting competitive service proposed by 17 airlines between many large U.S. cities and points throughout the Pacific, including Hawaii, was begun. 

The Board also: 

Concluded an air transport agreement with the Soviet Union, discussed possibilities of one with Vietnam, and acquiesced to the termination of an established one with Italy. 

Awarded temporary certificates on a selective geographic basis to 10 supplemental carriers for charter service in foreign and overseas air transportation.

Fixed rates on all Space Available 

Military Mail (letters, small packages, voices recordings, etc.) to and from overseas military bases, as authorized by 

Congress (Public Law 89-725). 

Approved the implementation of ad ditional promotional fares by domestic carriers and the extension of existing ones into fiscal 1968. 179
